RANZCR Pathology Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Pale, firm area with preserved ghost-like tubular outlines, absent nuclei and a hyperaemic rim): Occlusion of an end-arterial branch of the renal artery gives coagulative necrosis, in which denatured proteins preserve the tissue outline for days. The wedge shape with its base on the capsule is the typical geometry of a solid organ infarct. Liquefaction is typical of brain and abscesses, caseation of tuberculosis, and saponification of fat necrosis, none of which fit a renal cortical embolic infarct.
  2. The correct answer is B (Enzymatic digestion of necrotic neural tissue by microglia, with removal of debris leaving a cyst): Cerebral infarcts undergo liquefactive necrosis because brain has little connective tissue and abundant lipid-rich, enzyme-susceptible neural elements. Microglia and later macrophages digest the dead tissue, and the end result is a cystic space bordered by gliosis rather than a fibrous scar. Coagulative preservation and collagen scarring are features of infarcts elsewhere, and apoptosis does not produce a cavity.
  3. The correct answer is C (Structureless granular eosinophilic debris bordered by epithelioid macrophages and giant cells): Caseous necrosis is a distinctive mixture of coagulative and liquefactive change in which the cellular architecture is completely obliterated, giving amorphous granular debris. It is enclosed by a granulomatous reaction of epithelioid histiocytes and Langhans-type giant cells. Pus, ghost outlines and palisading necrobiosis each belong to other disease patterns.
  4. The correct answer is D (Lipase release hydrolyses triglycerides to fatty acids that combine with calcium to form soaps): Enzymatic fat necrosis follows release of pancreatic lipases into the peritoneal fat, which splits triglycerides into free fatty acids. These bind calcium in a process called saponification, producing the chalky white deposits. Dystrophic calcification of granulomas and hypercalcaemic deposition need different settings, and ischaemia gives coagulative necrosis.
  5. The correct answer is D (Activation of effector caspases that cleave cellular proteins and activate endonucleases): Apoptosis is a regulated process in which initiator caspases activate effector caspases such as caspase 3, which degrade the cytoskeleton and nuclear proteins and activate DNases. The cell shrinks and fragments into apoptotic bodies that are cleared without inflammation. Mitochondrial pore opening with ATP loss, lysosomal autolysis and complement lysis are mechanisms of necrosis or immune injury.
  6. The correct answer is C (Flocculent amorphous densities in swollen mitochondria with plasma membrane disruption): Reversible injury shows swelling, blebs, ribosomal detachment, chromatin clumping and ATP depletion, all of which can recover if oxygen returns. Irreversibility is marked by profound membrane damage and inability to restore mitochondrial function, seen as amorphous densities in mitochondria. The other three changes belong to the earlier reversible stage.
  7. The correct answer is B (Selectin-mediated rolling, integrin-mediated firm adhesion, transmigration across venules, then chemotaxis toward the focus): Leukocyte recruitment proceeds by margination, selectin-mediated rolling, integrin-mediated firm adhesion and diapedesis through the venular wall. Chemokines then guide the cell along a gradient to the infection. Swapping selectins and integrins or putting chemotaxis first reverses the real order, and histamine-induced gaps explain exudate, not cell emigration.
  8. The correct answer is C (Th1 derived interferon gamma activating macrophages to become epithelioid histiocytes): Granulomas are a form of type IV hypersensitivity in which CD4 T cells secrete interferon gamma, transforming macrophages into epithelioid cells that fuse into giant cells. Sarcoidosis is characterised by non-caseating granulomas of this kind. Immune complexes, IgE and neutrophil chemotaxis produce vasculitis, allergy and pyogenic inflammation respectively.
  9. The correct answer is A (Proliferating fibroblasts and new capillaries in loose oedematous matrix with scattered inflammatory cells): Granulation tissue appears within three to five days and consists of new thin-walled capillaries, proliferating fibroblasts and a loose matrix of type III collagen. It gives the pink, granular appearance of the wound base. Dense collagen belongs to a mature scar, and pus or foreign body granulomas indicate infection or reaction to material.
  10. The correct answer is D (Thick, hyalinised, glassy eosinophilic collagen bundles arranged haphazardly in the dermis): A keloid is an excessive scar that grows beyond the original injury and is composed of thick, glassy, randomly arranged collagen bundles. A hypertrophic scar is confined to the wound and shows oriented collagen with myofibroblast nodules, and it may regress. Fascicular spindle cell growth suggests dermatofibrosarcoma, and granulation tissue is a transient early stage.
  11. The correct answer is B (Large volume of granulation tissue with abundant myofibroblasts generating sustained contractile force): Healing by secondary intention involves a large tissue defect that fills with abundant granulation tissue. Myofibroblasts containing alpha smooth muscle actin contract and pull the edges together, and this is the reason for visible contraction. A sutured incision heals by primary intention with minimal granulation and little contraction.
  12. The correct answer is B (Suppressed inflammatory cell recruitment and collagen synthesis, weakening the early granulation tissue): Glucocorticoids dampen the inflammatory phase and inhibit fibroblast proliferation and collagen synthesis, so granulation tissue is weak and wounds may dehisce. Scurvy impairs collagen cross-linking but the clinical context is steroid use. Excess TGF beta causes fibrosis, and the absence of purulence argues against infection.
  13. The correct answer is B (Immunoglobulin light chain fragments from a clonal plasma cell proliferation): Primary AL amyloidosis is caused by clonal plasma cells producing light chains, often lambda, that deposit in kidney, heart, tongue and soft tissue. The macroglossia and periorbital purpura are characteristic. AA amyloid follows chronic inflammation, beta-2 microglobulin follows long-term dialysis, and transthyretin causes cardiac or neuropathic amyloid.
  14. The correct answer is C (Apple-green birefringence from non-branching fibrils arranged in a cross beta pleated sheet): Amyloid fibrils are non-branching, about 7 to 10 nm wide, and share a cross beta pleated sheet structure irrespective of the precursor protein. This structure binds Congo red and gives apple-green birefringence under polarised light. Collagen, haemosiderin and alpha helical proteins do not produce this diagnostic optical property.
  15. The correct answer is C (Serum amyloid A, an acute phase protein driven by persistent interleukin 6 and interleukin 1): Reactive systemic AA amyloidosis complicates chronic inflammatory states such as rheumatoid arthritis, bronchiectasis and chronic infection. Hepatic serum amyloid A is raised by cytokines and, when cleaved, forms fibrils that deposit in kidney, spleen and liver. Light chain, cerebral and calcitonin amyloid arise in different settings.
  16. The correct answer is A (Dystrophic calcification occurring in damaged tissue despite normal serum calcium and phosphate levels): Dystrophic calcification occurs in dead or degenerating tissue, such as damaged valves, atheroma and old necrosis, with normal serum calcium. Metastatic calcification needs hypercalcaemia or a raised calcium phosphate product and affects normal tissue. Calciphylaxis occurs in end-stage kidney disease and is a vascular skin disorder.
  17. The correct answer is B (Alkaline interior of acid secreting tissues favours precipitation when calcium phosphate product is raised): Metastatic calcification occurs in normal tissue when serum calcium or the calcium phosphate product is raised, as in renal failure with secondary hyperparathyroidism. It favours lungs, gastric mucosa and kidneys because acid loss makes their interiors alkaline, which favours precipitation. Dystrophic deposition, saponification and ectopic bone formation need prior tissue damage or different mechanisms.
  18. The correct answer is C (Ghon complex, a subpleural parenchymal focus with caseating hilar lymph node involvement): Primary tuberculosis typically forms a Ghon focus in the lower part of the upper lobe or upper part of the lower lobe, close to the pleura. With the caseating hilar nodes it forms the Ghon complex. Ranke complex is the later healed, calcified stage, and miliary disease reflects bloodstream spread.
  19. The correct answer is B (Saprophytic colonisation of the cavity by Aspergillus hyphae forming a fungal ball without tissue invasion): A mobile intracavitary mass with an air crescent in a preformed cavity is an aspergilloma, a tangle of hyphae, mucus and debris growing without invasion. It typically causes haemoptysis from the vascular cavity wall. Invasive disease occurs in immunosuppressed patients, and allergic disease is a hypersensitivity reaction in asthmatic airways.
  20. The correct answer is A (Foamy eosinophilic intra-alveolar exudate containing cysts that stain with silver methods): Pneumocystis jirovecii causes a foamy, honeycombed alveolar exudate in which the cysts are demonstrated with Gomori methenamine silver. The perihilar ground glass pattern and a low CD4 count are typical. Cryptococci, Aspergillus hyphae and cytomegalovirus inclusions are separate, recognisable patterns on cytology.
  21. The correct answer is C (Shaggy roughened surfaces covered by fibrin strands in a bread-and-butter pattern): Post-infarction pericarditis in the first days is fibrinous, with a fibrin-rich exudate that coats the pericardium and is torn apart by cardiac movement, producing a shaggy surface and a rub. Granulomatous pericarditis would suggest tuberculosis, and dense calcified plates indicate a chronic constrictive process. A smooth serosa implies a simple transudate.
  22. The correct answer is D (Adherent yellow-white plaques of fibrin, mucus and neutrophils erupting from crypts in a volcano pattern): Clostridioides difficile toxins injure colonic epithelium and produce a pseudomembrane composed of fibrin, mucus and inflammatory cells that erupts from damaged crypts like a volcano. Crohn disease, ulcerative colitis and ischaemic colitis each have distinct histology, and antibiotic exposure with diarrhoea points to the toxin-mediated lesion.
  23. The correct answer is B (Inner germinal layer, middle laminated acellular layer and an outer host fibrous pericyst): Echinococcus granulosus forms a unilocular cyst with a nucleated germinal layer, an acellular laminated membrane and an outer fibrous pericyst contributed by the host. Brood capsules and daughter cysts are typical, and rupture can cause anaphylaxis. A mucinous cystic neoplasm has epithelium and ovarian type stroma, and an abscess contains pus.
  24. The correct answer is C (Transmural lymphoid aggregates with non-caseating granulomas and fissuring ulcers): Crohn disease is a transmural, skip-lesion inflammatory process with lymphoid aggregates, fissures leading to fistulae, and non-caseating granulomas in around half of cases. Ulcerative colitis is mucosal and continuous. Ileocaecal tuberculosis mimics it but shows caseation, and pseudomembranes indicate toxin-mediated colitis.
  25. The correct answer is B (Generation of reactive oxygen species and calcium overload once blood flow is restored): Reperfusion can paradoxically worsen injury in cells that are still viable. Oxygen re-entry generates free radicals, calcium overload triggers hypercontraction (contraction bands), and inflammation adds further damage. Persistent acidosis belongs to the ischaemic period, and contrast hypersensitivity does not explain these histological changes.
  26. The correct answer is A (Wet gangrene, coagulative necrosis modified by liquefaction from bacteria and neutrophils): Gangrene is clinical coagulative necrosis of a limb, usually from ischaemia. When bacterial infection is superimposed, liquefactive change is added, giving wet gangrene with foul discharge and, with gas-forming organisms, soft tissue gas. Dry gangrene stays mummified and uninfected, whereas fat and caseous necrosis arise in other settings.
  27. The correct answer is D (Accumulation of triglyceride droplets in hepatocytes from increased fatty acid delivery and impaired metabolism): Steatosis is reversible injury in which triglycerides accumulate in hepatocytes because delivery of fatty acids exceeds oxidation and export. The fat-laden cells scatter ultrasound and give a bright liver. Iron, alpha-1 antitrypsin and copper accumulations are separate disorders with different histology and imaging.
  28. The correct answer is D (Metaplasia of squamous to intestinal type columnar epithelium, predisposing to adenocarcinoma): Chronic acid exposure replaces oesophageal squamous mucosa with intestinal-type columnar epithelium containing goblet cells, which is metaplasia. It is a risk factor for low and high grade dysplasia and oesophageal adenocarcinoma. Dysplasia is a different step, and basal hyperplasia or atrophy do not account for goblet cells.
  29. The correct answer is C (Necrotic epithelial debris mixed with condensed plasma proteins leaked through damaged alveolar walls): Diffuse alveolar damage, the lesion of ARDS, begins with endothelial and type I pneumocyte injury. Protein-rich fluid leaks into alveoli and condenses with necrotic epithelial debris to form hyaline membranes. Alveolar proteinosis, lobar pneumonia and organising pneumonia are different patterns, and plugs of granulation tissue occur later in repair.
  30. The correct answer is C (Broad ribbon-like hyphae, mostly non-septate, branching at right angles with vessel invasion): Mucorales fungi thrive in acidotic, hyperglycaemic hosts and invade blood vessels, causing thrombosis and tissue infarction that produces black eschars. Their hyphae are broad, ribbon-like and branch at about 90 degrees. Aspergillus is narrow and septate with acute angle branching, and the other two organisms have different morphology.
  31. The correct answer is D (Dilated perivascular spaces filled with gelatinous capsular polysaccharide and yeasts, with little inflammation): Cryptococcus neoformans has a polysaccharide capsule that produces a mucoid, gelatinous material expanding the Virchow-Robin spaces, giving soap bubble lesions in the basal ganglia. The host inflammatory response is poor in profound immunosuppression. Toxoplasmosis shows ring enhancement with necrosis, and neurocysticercosis and abscesses have different appearances.
  32. The correct answer is B (Dead larval cysts that have degenerated and undergone dystrophic calcification): Taenia solium larvae form cysts in the brain that eventually die and degenerate, provoking a granulomatous reaction and then dystrophic calcification. The eccentric dot is the scolex within a viable or degenerating cyst. Calcification in normal calcium states reflects previous damage, not hypercalcaemia, and a living cyst is not calcified.
  33. The correct answer is A (Granulation tissue and fibrosis encircling a core of liquefactive necrosis and neutrophils): An abscess is a localised collection of pus consisting of liquefactive necrosis and neutrophils. Over weeks the host walls it off with granulation tissue and fibrous tissue, giving the enhancing rim on CT. Granulomas, simple biliary cysts and alcoholic hepatitis do not show a pyogenic core with a vascular granulation wall.
  34. The correct answer is C (Interleukin 1 and TNF cause fever, while histamine increases vascular permeability): Interleukin 1 and TNF act on the hypothalamus, inducing prostaglandin E2 synthesis and fever. Histamine, released from mast cells, causes venular endothelial contraction and increased permeability. Leukotriene B4 and C5a are chemotactic, interleukin 8 is a chemokine, and prostacyclin and thromboxane have mainly vascular and platelet actions.
  35. The correct answer is B (Central immature fibroblastic tissue maturing outward to osteoid and mature bone at the periphery): Myositis ossificans is a reactive heterotopic ossification after trauma, with a zonal pattern of maturation from a cellular fibroblastic centre to mature bone at the edge. This is the reverse of osteosarcoma, in which immature tissue lies at the periphery. Fat necrosis and osteochondroma have no such zonation, and the peripheral rim of calcification is the imaging counterpart.
  36. The correct answer is A (Alternating bands of platelet-fibrin and red cells known as lines of Zahn): The laminated bands are produced by repeated deposition of platelet-fibrin layers and trapped red cells under flow, and they mark an antemortem thrombus. A postmortem clot is soft, gelatinous and uniformly dark, so it is the tempting alternative. Septic emboli and simple stasis clot lack the alternating architecture. Calf deep vein thrombus is the usual source after orthopaedic surgery.
  37. The correct answer is C (A point mutation making activated factor V resistant to cleavage by protein C): Factor V Leiden is the commonest inherited thrombophilia, and the altered factor V is not inactivated normally by activated protein C. This leaves the Virchow hypercoagulable limb unchecked, typically causing venous rather than arterial events. Antithrombin, protein S and prothrombin variants are genuine thrombophilias but are less frequent. Hormonal therapy adds a further acquired risk.
  38. The correct answer is D (Venous thrombus crossing a patent foramen ovale into the systemic arterial circulation): The combination of a venous thrombus, cerebral infarction and early bubbles in the left heart indicates a right to left shunt. A patent foramen ovale lets venous emboli bypass the pulmonary filter and cause arterial infarcts. Valvular, carotid atheroembolic and atrial fibrillation sources are all genuine causes of stroke but do not explain a venous origin or shunt.
  39. The correct answer is D (Marrow fat globules entering torn veins and lodging in pulmonary and cerebral microvasculature): Long bone fractures release marrow fat that enters torn venous channels and impacts in pulmonary capillaries, with some passing to the brain and skin. The delay of one to two days and petechiae are characteristic, and the starfield pattern of small cerebral foci reflects microembolism. Septic, cardiogenic and decompression mechanisms each give a different history and distribution.
  40. The correct answer is D (A wedge-shaped, dark red, haemorrhagic area with its base on the pleura): Lung has a dual blood supply, so occlusion of a pulmonary artery produces haemorrhagic rather than pale infarction, particularly with congestion from heart failure. The lesion is wedge-shaped with the base on the pleura and the apex towards the hilum. Pale infarcts occur in solid organs with end-arterial supply. Cavitation suggests secondary infection, and lobar pneumonia is not wedge-shaped.
  41. The correct answer is A (Coagulative necrosis with preserved cell outlines and a surrounding hyperaemic border): Solid organs with end-arterial supply such as the kidney, spleen and heart undergo coagulative necrosis after ischaemia, and the architecture persists for days. The wedge shape reflects the vascular territory. Liquefactive necrosis is the pattern in brain, caseous necrosis characterises tuberculosis, and fat necrosis follows enzymatic fat injury.
  42. The correct answer is B (Liquefactive necrosis, macrophage clearance and astrocytic gliosis around a cystic space): Brain tissue is rich in lipid and lysosomal enzymes and has no collagenous stroma, so infarcts liquefy and are removed by microglia and macrophages. The reactive astrocytes form a glial scar rather than collagen, leaving a fluid-filled cavity. Fibrous scarring is the heart and kidney outcome, and caseation is not a consequence of ischaemia.
  43. The correct answer is B (Cytokine-driven vasodilation with increased vascular permeability and reduced systemic vascular resistance): Bacterial products activate macrophages and endothelium, releasing tumour necrosis factor, interleukin 1 and nitric oxide, which dilate vessels and leak plasma. The warm, flushed periphery distinguishes this from cardiogenic and hypovolaemic shock. Anaphylaxis also causes vasodilation but is triggered by IgE-mediated mast cell degranulation, not infection.
  44. The correct answer is D (Hyaline membranes lining alveolar walls from diffuse alveolar damage with increased permeability): Non-cardiogenic oedema in acute respiratory distress syndrome reflects injury to the alveolar capillary barrier, with protein-rich fluid and fibrin forming hyaline membranes in diffuse alveolar damage. Heart failure oedema is a low protein transudate with haemosiderin macrophages. Alveolar proteinosis and bronchopneumonia give different histology and a patchy distribution.
  45. The correct answer is A (Patchy tubular epithelial necrosis with sloughed cells and casts within the proximal tubules): Prolonged hypoperfusion causes ischaemic acute tubular injury, with patchy necrosis of proximal tubular cells and casts. Kidneys appear normal or enlarged and echogenic without obstruction. Diffuse cortical necrosis requires abrupt, severe ischaemia, usually in obstetric catastrophes with disseminated coagulation. Crescentic and interstitial lesions do not fit this history.
  46. The correct answer is A (Gas bubbles occluding small marrow vessels, producing ischaemic bone necrosis): Nitrogen comes out of solution during rapid decompression and forms bubbles that obstruct small vessels, leading to osteonecrosis. The subchondral crescent sign and serpiginous marrow margin define avascular necrosis. Inflammatory arthritis, osteoarthritis and marrow infiltration produce different bone and joint patterns.
  47. The correct answer is D (The neoplastic cells have not breached the basement membrane to reach lymphatics or vessels): The defining difference is penetration of the basement membrane, which gives access to stroma and vessels. A lesion confined above it has cytology of malignancy, including marked pleomorphism and mitoses, but cannot spread by the usual routes. Telomerase is typically reactivated in high-grade lesions. Normal growth control is not retained in carcinoma in situ.
  48. The correct answer is D (Direct intraluminal growth into the venous system with haematogenous spread to lung): Renal cell carcinoma characteristically invades the renal vein and may extend as a tumour thrombus into the inferior vena cava, from which emboli reach the lungs. Haematogenous metastasis is common. Lymphatic spread occurs but is not the signature route. Ureteric seeding and peritoneal implants are features of urothelial and ovarian tumours respectively.
  49. The correct answer is B (A propensity for vascular invasion, particularly of portal and hepatic venous radicles): Hepatocellular carcinoma arises from hepatocytes in cirrhosis and tends to invade vascular channels, producing tumour thrombus in the portal or hepatic veins. A capsule may be present but does not prevent invasion. Cholangiocarcinoma arises from bile ducts, and colorectal metastases rarely arise in cirrhotic liver.
  50. The correct answer is B (Translocation placing the MYC gene beside an immunoglobulin heavy chain locus): Burkitt lymphoma, including the endemic jaw presentation, shows t(8;14) in most cases, which juxtaposes MYC with the heavy chain locus and drives proliferation. The starry sky appearance arises from macrophages ingesting apoptotic cells. BCR-ABL1, MYCN amplification and BCL2 translocation define chronic myeloid leukaemia, neuroblastoma and follicular lymphoma.
  51. The correct answer is C (Constitutive tyrosine kinase activity from fusion of two genes): The Philadelphia chromosome arises from the reciprocal translocation t(9;22), creating BCR-ABL1, whose protein has unregulated tyrosine kinase activity that drives myeloid proliferation. This is the target of tyrosine kinase inhibitors. Cyclin D1 overexpression follows t(11;14) in mantle cell lymphoma, and gene amplification is a separate oncogenic mechanism.
  52. The correct answer is C (Germline RB1 mutation with loss of the second allele occurring in many somatic cells): Hereditary retinoblastoma follows Knudson's two-hit model: one RB1 allele is mutated in the germline and the second is lost somatically, so multiple retinal cells are at risk and tumours are bilateral. Carriers also have raised risk of osteosarcoma. Sporadic retinoblastoma needs two somatic hits in one cell and is usually unilateral.
  53. The correct answer is B (TP53, encoding a transcription factor that triggers cell cycle arrest and apoptosis): The combination of early breast cancer, sarcoma, adrenocortical carcinoma and brain tumours defines Li-Fraumeni syndrome, caused by germline TP53 mutation. The p53 protein is a guardian of the genome. BRCA1 raises breast and ovarian risk, APC causes polyposis, and PTEN causes Cowden syndrome, which has a different spectrum.
  54. The correct answer is C (Colorectal adenocarcinoma arising in adenomas, usually by the fourth decade): Hundreds of adenomatous polyps indicate familial adenomatous polyposis, caused by germline APC mutation. Each adenoma carries malignant potential, so cancer is near certain without prophylactic colectomy. Peutz-Jeghers polyps are hamartomas, Crohn disease is inflammatory, and serrated pathway cancers are sporadic and BRAF driven.
  55. The correct answer is B (Neurofibromatosis type 2 caused by loss of merlin on chromosome 22): Bilateral vestibular schwannomas are the hallmark of neurofibromatosis type 2, and affected patients also develop meningiomas and ependymomas. The gene encodes merlin on chromosome 22. Type 1 features neurofibromas, optic gliomas and cafe-au-lait macules. Tuberous sclerosis and VHL disease have other central nervous system lesions.
  56. The correct answer is D (Angiomyolipoma composed of dysmorphic vessels, smooth muscle and mature fat): The CNS findings, cardiac rhabdomyoma and fat-density kidney masses indicate tuberous sclerosis, with mTOR pathway activation. Renal angiomyolipomas contain blood vessels, smooth muscle and fat, which explains macroscopic fat on imaging. Clear cell carcinoma is lipid-rich on histology but not macroscopically fatty. Oncocytoma and Wilms tumour are unrelated.
  57. The correct answer is B (Clear cell renal cell carcinoma, frequently multifocal and bilateral): Haemangioblastoma with pancreatic cysts points to von Hippel-Lindau disease, from germline VHL loss with increased hypoxia-inducible factor signalling. Patients develop multiple clear cell renal carcinomas, often bilateral, as well as phaeochromocytoma. Papillary and chromophobe subtypes have other familial associations. Urothelial carcinoma is not part of this syndrome.
  58. The correct answer is A (The extent of local invasion and nodal involvement at the time of diagnosis): Grading describes how closely tumour cells resemble normal tissue, using differentiation, pleomorphism and mitotic activity. Staging describes anatomical extent, namely the primary tumour size and depth, nodal involvement and distant metastasis. For most solid tumours, including colon cancer, stage is the stronger predictor of outcome and guides treatment.
  59. The correct answer is B (Small cell carcinoma with neuroendocrine granules and ectopic antidiuretic hormone secretion): Hyponatraemia with inappropriately concentrated urine suggests SIADH, and central small cell lung cancer is the typical source, via neuroendocrine cells that secrete peptides ectopically. Squamous carcinoma more often causes hypercalcaemia through parathyroid hormone related peptide. Adenocarcinoma and large cell carcinoma rarely produce hormones.
  60. The correct answer is A (Preferential glucose uptake and glycolysis even when oxygen is available): Many malignant cells depend on aerobic glycolysis, the Warburg effect, taking up large amounts of glucose through upregulated transporters. FDG is trapped after phosphorylation by hexokinase, producing a hot spot. Increased fatty acid or glutamine metabolism may occur but does not account for FDG avidity, and reduced glucose need would give the opposite result.
  61. The correct answer is C (Fragmentation of elastic fibres with cystic medial degeneration from defective fibrillin-1): Marfan syndrome results from mutations in fibrillin-1, which also increases transforming growth factor beta signalling, causing elastic tissue loss and cystic medial degeneration. This predisposes to root dilatation and dissection. Atherosclerosis, giant cell aortitis and syphilitic aortitis all injure the aorta but do not explain a young patient with the skeletal and ocular features.
  62. The correct answer is B (Defective chloride channel function causing thick, viscous secretions in ducts and airways): Mutations of CFTR reduce chloride secretion and increase sodium absorption, dehydrating secretions that obstruct airways and pancreatic ducts. The result is bronchiectasis, pancreatic atrophy and malabsorption. Primary ciliary dyskinesia, alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency and antibody deficiency can cause bronchiectasis but not the pancreatic disease.
  63. The correct answer is B (45,X, with absence of one X chromosome in all or most cells): Turner syndrome results from monosomy X, causing gonadal dysgenesis, short stature and left-sided cardiovascular lesions such as coarctation and bicuspid aortic valve. Renal anomalies including horseshoe kidney are common. Klinefelter syndrome affects males, triple X has a mild phenotype, and androgen insensitivity presents with a female phenotype but a 46,XY karyotype.
  64. The correct answer is B (Excess intestinal iron absorption from reduced hepcidin, with parenchymal hepatocyte iron deposition): HFE-related haemochromatosis causes inappropriately low hepcidin and excess iron absorption, with deposition in hepatocytes, pancreas, heart and joints. The normal spleen signal favours primary parenchymal overload rather than transfusional siderosis. Wilson disease and alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency cause liver disease without this iron pattern. Cirrhosis raises hepatocellular carcinoma risk.
  65. The correct answer is A (Apple-green birefringence under polarised light after Congo red staining): Infiltrative thickened myocardium with low ECG voltage suggests cardiac amyloidosis, in which extracellular misfolded protein forms beta-pleated sheets. Congo red stains the deposits and yields apple-green birefringence under polarised light. Perls stain identifies iron, PAS identifies glycogen and neutral mucins, and trichrome highlights collagen.
  66. The correct answer is C (Small, warty fibrinous vegetations of immune complex origin on either surface of the valve): Libman-Sacks lesions in lupus are sterile, verrucous and may occur on both leaflet surfaces, reflecting immune complex and antiphospholipid related injury. Infective vegetations are large and friable, rheumatic and marantic lesions sit along closure lines. The absence of fever and positive blood cultures distinguishes it from infection.
  67. The correct answer is D (Well-formed granulomas without central necrosis, often with Schaumann bodies): Sarcoid granulomas are tightly formed, non-necrotising and may contain asteroid or Schaumann bodies. Tuberculous granulomas, driven by type IV hypersensitivity to mycobacterial antigen, show central caseous necrosis. Stellate microabscesses suggest cat scratch disease, and palisading granulomas around necrobiosis occur with rheumatoid nodules. Exclusion of infection remains necessary.
  68. The correct answer is B (Storiform fibrosis with dense lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate and obliterative phlebitis): Autoimmune pancreatitis type 1 is the pancreatic manifestation of IgG4-related disease, characterised by storiform fibrosis, IgG4-positive plasma cells and obliterative phlebitis. Adenocarcinoma is the main mimic and must be excluded, but shows desmoplastic stroma and atypical glands. Alcoholic chronic and acute necrotising pancreatitis have different histology.
  69. The correct answer is D (Obliterative bronchiolitis with submucosal fibrosis narrowing the bronchiolar lumens): Chronic lung allograft dysfunction in the form of bronchiolitis obliterans arises from alloimmune injury leading to fibrous obliteration of small airways, causing air trapping on expiratory imaging. Acute cellular rejection is perivascular lymphocytic, hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, and reperfusion injury occurs in the first days.
  70. The correct answer is C (Widespread microvascular injury with excess collagen deposition in skin and visceral organs): Systemic sclerosis combines endothelial injury, fibroblast activation and progressive fibrosis, producing skin thickening, oesophageal smooth muscle atrophy with dysmotility and interstitial lung disease. Lupus, vasculitis and myasthenia gravis are immune disorders with different pathology. Pulmonary hypertension from vascular remodelling is a further complication.
  71. The correct answer is C (Dense neutrophilic infiltrate surrounding myocytes with coagulative necrosis and loss of nuclei): At about three days the infarct shows maximal acute inflammation: neutrophils infiltrate dead myocytes that show coagulative necrosis, and the macroscopic centre is yellow with a hyperaemic border. Granulation tissue with macrophages appears from about one week, so it is too late. Wavy fibres belong to the first hours, and dense scar needs several weeks.
  72. The correct answer is D (Maximal enzymatic softening of necrotic myocardium before collagen scarring has strengthened the infarcted wall): Free wall rupture classically occurs between about three and seven days, when inflammatory cells have digested the necrotic wall and granulation tissue has not yet provided tensile strength. The result is haemopericardium and tamponade. Early hours produce arrhythmia and pump failure rather than rupture. Dressler syndrome arises weeks later, and aneurysm involves a healed fibrous wall.
  73. The correct answer is A (Immune-mediated pericardial inflammation triggered by antibodies directed against released myocardial antigens): Dressler syndrome is a delayed post-infarction pericarditis, appearing weeks after the event and thought to be an autoimmune response to myocardial antigens. Early fibrinous pericarditis overlying a transmural infarct appears within days and is not immune-mediated. Rupture presents abruptly and early. An infective cause would not fit the normal troponin and weeks-long interval.
  74. The correct answer is D (Calcium influx into reperfused irreversibly injured myocytes causing hypercontraction and haemorrhagic necrosis): Reperfusion of irreversibly injured myocytes allows calcium entry, producing hypercontracted sarcomeres seen as contraction bands, with haemorrhage from damaged microvasculature. Without reperfusion, necrosis is coagulative with a neutrophil infiltrate. Catecholamine injury can show contraction bands but the coronary history here points to reperfusion. Hibernation involves viable, not necrotic, myocytes.
  75. The correct answer is A (The posteromedial papillary muscle receives blood from a single coronary artery, making it vulnerable to infarction): The posteromedial papillary muscle is supplied usually by the posterior descending artery alone, so inferior infarction readily necroses it. The anterolateral muscle has a dual supply from the left anterior descending and circumflex arteries, making it more resistant. Chordae are avascular. Rupture occurs several days after infarction, when the necrotic muscle is weakest.
  76. The correct answer is B (Thinned fibrous scar replacing myocardium, lined internally by mural thrombus, with an intact outer wall): A true post-infarction aneurysm has a wall made of dense collagenous scar containing all or part of the original ventricular layers, with thrombus on the lumen surface and a risk of embolism. A pseudoaneurysm has a narrow neck and a wall formed by pericardium and clot. Hypertrophic and arrhythmogenic disease have entirely different histology.
  77. The correct answer is D (Haphazard arrangement of hypertrophied myocytes with interstitial fibrosis and enlarged, bizarre nuclei): Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy shows myocyte disarray, hypertrophy, bizarre nuclei and interstitial fibrosis, with asymmetric septal thickening and a mitral leaflet plaque from contact with the septum in systole. Fibrofatty replacement characterises arrhythmogenic disease. Lymphocytic infiltrate indicates myocarditis, and Congo red positivity indicates amyloid.
  78. The correct answer is C (TTN, encoding the giant sarcomeric protein titin, with truncating variants): Truncating variants in TTN account for the largest share of familial dilated cardiomyopathy. MYH7 is better known in hypertrophic disease, although it can contribute to dilated forms. PKP2 is the leading gene in arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy. PRKAR1A underlies Carney complex, which includes cardiac myxomas.
  79. The correct answer is A (Extracellular transthyretin-derived fibrils that stain with Congo red and show apple-green birefringence): Wild-type transthyretin amyloid is the typical cause in elderly men with bone-tracer uptake and no paraprotein, and the fibrils are Congo red positive. AL amyloid is also Congo red positive but needs a monoclonal light chain. Haemochromatosis gives iron-laden myocytes, and sarcoidosis gives patchy granulomatous disease.
  80. The correct answer is D (Catecholamine surge causing transient myocardial stunning with contraction band necrosis in the apical myocardium): Stress cardiomyopathy follows a surge of catecholamines that causes reversible apical ballooning, with contraction band necrosis and little coagulative necrosis. Normal coronary arteries argue against thrombotic occlusion. Myocarditis would show lymphocytic infiltrates, and hypertrophic disease would show a thickened septum rather than a transient apical abnormality.
  81. The correct answer is C (Loss of function of desmosomal proteins such as plakophilin 2 impairing cell-to-cell adhesion): Arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy is mostly caused by desmosomal gene mutations, notably PKP2, so that stressed myocytes detach and are replaced by fibrofatty tissue, predominantly in the right ventricle. Sarcomere mutations cause hypertrophic disease, titin truncation causes dilatation, and amyloid causes restrictive thickening.
  82. The correct answer is D (Enterovirus such as Coxsackie B, with direct myocyte injury and a secondary immune response): Lymphocytic myocarditis in developed countries is most often caused by Coxsackie B and other enteroviruses. Chagas disease occurs in Latin America and shows parasites in myocytes. Diphtheria is now rare with immunisation. Lyme carditis characteristically presents with conduction block rather than heart failure.
  83. The correct answer is B (Aschoff body with Anitschkow caterpillar cells and a central zone of fibrinoid necrosis): Acute rheumatic fever produces Aschoff bodies in the heart, containing activated macrophages called Anitschkow cells and fibrinoid necrosis. Chronic rheumatic valve disease follows repeated damage, with fibrosis, commissural fusion and chordal shortening. The other lesions are non-rheumatic: thrombotic vegetations, granulomas, or myxomatous degeneration.
  84. The correct answer is C (Calcified nodules on the aortic aspect of the cusps without fusion of the commissures): Degenerative calcific stenosis shows calcified masses at the cusp bases protruding on the aortic side, without commissural fusion, and arises earlier in a congenitally bicuspid valve. Commissural fusion is characteristic of rheumatic disease. Friable vegetations imply endocarditis, and myxoid ballooning describes prolapse.
  85. The correct answer is C (Thinned fibrosa and expanded spongiosa containing abundant proteoglycan matrix): Myxomatous degeneration shows attenuation of the fibrosa and thickening of the spongiosa through proteoglycan deposition, common in Marfan syndrome. Rheumatic valves show scarring and neovascularisation. Fibrin aggregates occur in non-bacterial thrombotic endocarditis, and cusp-base calcium is characteristic of calcific aortic stenosis.
  86. The correct answer is B (Staphylococcus aureus, a virulent organism able to infect previously normal valves): Right-sided endocarditis in people who inject drugs is usually due to S. aureus, which causes acute destructive disease of normal valves, and septic emboli lodge in the lungs. Viridans streptococci cause subacute disease on damaged left-sided valves. S. epidermidis suits prosthetic devices, and enterococci follow instrumentation.
  87. The correct answer is D (Hypercoagulable state with deposition of sterile platelet and fibrin thrombi on undamaged valve leaflets): Non-bacterial thrombotic endocarditis produces small sterile vegetations along the line of closure on normal valves, typically in cancer-associated hypercoagulability, and often embolises. Libman-Sacks lesions are verrucous, on both leaflet surfaces, in lupus. Infective vegetations are larger and destructive. Carcinoid affects mostly right-sided valves.
  88. The correct answer is B (Immune complex deposition with fibrinoid necrosis and organising granulation tissue beneath verrucous deposits): Libman-Sacks endocarditis in systemic lupus erythematosus shows verrucous vegetations on one or both leaflet surfaces, with fibrinoid necrosis and immune complexes. Marantic lesions sit along the closure line and lack valve inflammation. Infective vegetations contain organisms, and carcinoid plaques involve right-sided surfaces through serotonin effects.
  89. The correct answer is A (Serotonin-rich blood reaching the right heart before pulmonary inactivation, causing plaque-like fibrous endocardial thickening): Carcinoid syndrome with hepatic metastases lets vasoactive amines, particularly serotonin, bypass hepatic metabolism and reach the right heart, where fibrous plaques thicken the endocardium of the tricuspid and pulmonary valves. The lungs inactivate these substances so left-sided valves are spared. The alternative mechanisms produce different lesions on different valves.
  90. The correct answer is B (Friable fibrin and platelet vegetations fragmenting into septic emboli that lodge in cerebral arteries): Left-sided vegetations are friable and release septic emboli that cause infarcts and mycotic aneurysms in the brain, and painless Janeway lesions reflect embolic microabscesses. Immune complex effects can produce glomerulonephritis and Osler nodes but not typical stroke. Atrial thrombus and paradoxical embolism are unrelated to this valve lesion.
  91. The correct answer is B (Tuberculous pericarditis progressing to dense fibrous scarring and obliteration of the pericardial cavity): Constrictive pericarditis results from organisation and calcification of a prior inflammatory process, and tuberculosis is the leading cause worldwide. Viral pericarditis usually resolves completely. Uraemic and post-infarction pericarditis can leave adhesions but rarely cause calcified constriction. The thickened rigid shell limits diastolic filling.
  92. The correct answer is A (Fibrinous pericarditis, with a bread-and-butter appearance from fibrin strands stretched between the layers): Uraemia causes fibrinous pericarditis, in which fibrin-rich exudate coats the surfaces and creates a shaggy, bread-and-butter look when the layers are separated. Suppurative disease contains pus, and serous disease produces a clear fluid with smooth surfaces. Caseating pericarditis suggests tuberculosis and produces cheesy necrotic material.
  93. The correct answer is B (Direct tumour invasion or metastatic seeding of the pericardium producing a haemorrhagic exudate): Malignant pericardial effusion is typically bloody because tumour deposits are vascular and friable, and it can produce tamponade. Heart failure produces a transudate. Dressler effusions are generally small and serous. Rheumatic pericarditis is fibrinous and associated with the other features of rheumatic fever.
  94. The correct answer is A (Gelatinous, polypoid mass with stellate cells in an abundant myxoid matrix, attached by a short stalk): Myxoma is the commonest primary cardiac tumour in adults, usually left atrial and arising near the fossa ovalis. Microscopically, scattered stellate cells lie within abundant myxoid ground substance, and constitutional symptoms reflect interleukin-6 release. Rhabdomyoma has spider cells, papillary fibroelastoma has avascular fronds, and vegetations would be inflammatory rather than neoplastic.
  95. The correct answer is C (Rhabdomyoma, composed of large vacuolated glycogen-rich cells with strands radiating from the nucleus): Cardiac rhabdomyoma is the most common paediatric cardiac tumour and is closely linked to tuberous sclerosis through TSC1 or TSC2 mutations. It shows spider cells with vacuolated, glycogen-rich cytoplasm. Fibroma is also paediatric but is a single scarred nodule and not associated with tuberous sclerosis. Myxoma and fibroelastoma occur mainly in adults.
  96. The correct answer is D (Avascular papillary fronds with a core of elastic fibres and collagen, covered by endothelium): Papillary fibroelastoma is the commonest valvular tumour, a frond-like avascular lesion with an elastic core covered by endothelium that can embolise platelet thrombus to cause stroke. Myxoma originates at the septum. Marantic vegetations lack a stroma. Rhabdomyoma is intramural and typically diagnosed in infancy.
  97. The correct answer is B (Anterosuperior displacement of the infundibular septum causing right ventricular outflow obstruction): In tetralogy of Fallot, anterosuperior malalignment of the infundibular septum yields a malaligned ventricular septal defect, an overriding aorta, pulmonary outflow obstruction and secondary right ventricular hypertrophy. The degree of obstruction determines cyanosis. Transposition and patent ductus involve other defects, and atrial shunts do not produce right ventricular outflow obstruction.
  98. The correct answer is A (Patent ductus arteriosus, maintained with prostaglandin E1, allowing mixing between the parallel circuits): In complete transposition, the aorta arises from the right ventricle and the pulmonary artery from the left, so systemic and pulmonary circulations run in parallel. Survival depends on mixing through a patent ductus, foramen ovale or ventricular septal defect, and prostaglandin E1 keeps the ductus open. Maternal diabetes is a recognised risk factor.
  99. The correct answer is D (Paradoxical embolism of venous thrombus across the septal defect into the systemic arterial circulation): Secundum atrial septal defects arise at the fossa ovalis, and transient right-to-left flow, for example with straining, lets venous thrombi cross to the arterial side. Myxoma is not suggested by the venous thrombosis. Right-sided septic emboli travel to the lungs, and a ductal shunt reversal produces differential cyanosis, not stroke.
  100. The correct answer is A (Narrowing distal to the left subclavian artery near the ligamentum arteriosum, with enlarged collateral intercostal vessels): Turner syndrome is associated with coarctation, and in adults the narrowing is postductal, at or just below the ligamentum arteriosum. Enlarged intercostal collaterals erode the inferior rib margins. Preductal coarctation presents in infancy with a duct-dependent lower body. Root dilatation and an aberrant subclavian are separate lesions.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Prostaglandin E2 produced locally, together with the low oxygen tension of fetal blood): The ductus is kept open in utero by prostaglandin E2 and low oxygen tension. After birth rising oxygen and falling prostaglandins cause constriction, and bradykinin contributes to closure. Prematurity, maternal rubella and high altitude predispose to persistence. Indomethacin promotes closure by inhibiting prostaglandin synthesis.
  2. The correct answer is D (Medial hypertrophy and intimal fibrosis of small pulmonary arteries with plexiform lesions and irreversible vascular obstruction): Chronic left-to-right shunting subjects the pulmonary circulation to high flow and pressure, causing medial hypertrophy, intimal proliferation and plexiform lesions. Resistance eventually exceeds systemic levels, shunt reversal follows, and the murmur softens. Thromboembolism, venous hypertension and fibrosis give pulmonary hypertension by other mechanisms.
  3. The correct answer is D (A thin fibrous cap over a large lipid core containing many inflammatory macrophages): Plaque rupture with superimposed thrombosis is the usual mechanism of acute coronary events, and caps with little collagen, few smooth muscle cells and heavy macrophage activity are the most prone to fissure. Heavily calcified or fibrous lesions are more stable. Intimal smooth muscle thickening without lipid is an early change and rarely ruptures.
  4. The correct answer is B (Macrophage derived foam cells filled with cholesteryl ester droplets): The fatty streak is the earliest lesion of atherosclerosis and is composed of lipid laden macrophages with a few T cells, lying flat and not obstructing flow. Cholesterol clefts and a necrotic core belong to mature atheromas. Neutrophilic fibrinoid necrosis suggests vasculitis, and adventitial lymphocytes suggest aortitis.
  5. The correct answer is B (Atherosclerotic intimal damage with medial elastin degradation and smooth muscle loss): Abdominal aortic aneurysms arise from advanced atherosclerosis, where intimal plaque and chronic inflammation lead to proteolytic loss of medial elastin and smooth muscle and a thinned, weak wall. Cystic medial degeneration characterises thoracic aneurysms in Marfan syndrome. Vasa vasorum endarteritis is syphilitic, and giant cell inflammation is a vasculitis.
  6. The correct answer is C (Needle shaped clefts within small arteries, surrounded by foreign body giant cells): Catheter manipulation can dislodge atheromatous debris from the aorta, and the cholesterol crystals lodge in small arteries, leaving biconvex clefts with a foreign body reaction. The intact pedal pulses reflect occlusion of small distal vessels. Fibrinoid necrosis, granulomas and venous thrombus describe other vascular diseases.
  7. The correct answer is D (Defective fibrillin 1 leading to excessive transforming growth factor beta activity in the media): Marfan syndrome results from FBN1 mutation, reducing microfibril integrity and releasing excess TGF beta, which drives medial degeneration with mucoid pooling and elastin loss. Vascular Ehlers Danlos syndrome involves COL3A1 and causes rupture of vessels, but the skeletal and ocular features here point to Marfan syndrome. Atherosclerosis and granulomatous vasculitis do not explain this young patient.
  8. The correct answer is A (An intimal tear allows blood to enter the media and track longitudinally along it): Aortic dissection begins with an intimal tear, typically in a hypertensive aorta with medial degeneration, allowing blood to split the media longitudinally and create a false channel. The flap beyond the left subclavian origin makes this a Stanford type B dissection. Plaque rupture, vasculitis and vasa vasorum embolism do not produce a medial haematoma tracking along the aorta.
  9. The correct answer is D (Obliterative endarteritis of the vasa vasorum causing ischaemic injury to the outer media): Tertiary syphilis infects the adventitia of the proximal aorta and causes endarteritis of the vasa vasorum with plasma cell infiltrates. The resulting medial ischaemia produces scarring, elastic loss and the tree bark intimal wrinkling, with aortic root dilatation leading to valve incompetence. Immune complex, granulomatous and embolic mechanisms do not produce this pattern.
  10. The correct answer is C (Permanent visual loss from ischaemia of the optic nerve supply): Giant cell arteritis is a granulomatous vasculitis of large and medium arteries branching from the external carotid, and involvement of the ophthalmic or posterior ciliary arteries can cause irreversible blindness. Glomerulonephritis with haemoptysis belongs to small vessel ANCA disease. Visceral microaneurysms suggest polyarteritis nodosa, and childhood coronary aneurysms suggest Kawasaki disease.
  11. The correct answer is A (Granulomatous panarteritis with giant cells, leading to intimal fibrosis and luminal stenosis): Takayasu arteritis is a granulomatous large vessel vasculitis centred on the aorta and its major branches, which in a young woman leads to fibrous thickening, stenosis and loss of pulses. Fibrinoid necrosis of medium arteries suggests polyarteritis nodosa. Pauci immune small vessel disease describes microscopic polyangiitis, and distal vessel thrombosis in a smoker suggests thromboangiitis obliterans.
  12. The correct answer is B (Segmental transmural fibrinoid necrosis at different stages of healing in the same vessel): Polyarteritis nodosa is a necrotising vasculitis of medium arteries, associated with hepatitis B, with segmental lesions of differing ages in one specimen, leading to microaneurysms, infarcts and nerve ischaemia. The lungs are generally spared. A string of beads pattern belongs to fibromuscular dysplasia, necrotising granulomas to granulomatosis with polyangiitis, and a false channel to dissection.
  13. The correct answer is A (Coronary artery aneurysm formation with risk of thrombosis and myocardial infarction): Kawasaki disease is a medium vessel vasculitis of childhood with a tendency to involve the coronary arteries, and aneurysms may thrombose or rupture. Early intravenous immunoglobulin lowers this risk. Valvular, aortic root and pericardial problems are not the leading complication of this vasculitis.
  14. The correct answer is C (Necrotising granulomatous inflammation with vasculitis in the respiratory tract): Granulomatosis with polyangiitis combines necrotising granulomas of the upper and lower respiratory tract, small vessel vasculitis and glomerulonephritis, and PR3 ANCA is typical. Sarcoid granulomas are non necrotising. Eosinophilic disease needs asthma and eosinophilia, and polyarteritis nodosa is ANCA negative with no capillary lesions.
  15. The correct answer is B (Pauci immune necrotising crescentic glomerulonephritis with focal fibrinoid necrosis of capillary loops): Microscopic polyangiitis is an ANCA associated small vessel vasculitis and its renal lesion is pauci immune crescentic glomerulonephritis, meaning little or no immune deposition. Immune complex and anti GBM diseases show granular or linear staining. IgA nephropathy produces mesangial deposits and does not typically cause pulmonary haemorrhage.
  16. The correct answer is C (Acute inflammatory thrombus with microabscesses and granulomas, sparing the vessel wall architecture): Thromboangiitis obliterans, or Buerger disease, affects small and medium arteries and veins of the extremities in young smokers. The thrombus contains neutrophilic microabscesses with granulomas, while the wall framework is preserved. Giant cell arteritis, atherosclerosis and polyarteritis nodosa have different distributions and wall changes.
  17. The correct answer is C (Medial fibroplasia with thickened fibromuscular ridges alternating with thinned medial segments): Fibromuscular dysplasia typically affects the mid to distal renal and extracranial carotid arteries in young women, and medial fibroplasia is the commonest subtype, yielding the string of beads appearance. Atherosclerotic stenosis occurs at the ostium in older patients. Onion skin change is a small vessel lesion, and Takayasu arteritis involves the aorta.
  18. The correct answer is D (Venous stasis combined with endothelial injury and a surgery induced hypercoagulable state): Virchow triad comprises endothelial injury, abnormal blood flow (stasis or turbulence) and hypercoagulability. Postoperative immobility, venous manipulation and the surgical acute phase response favour venous thrombosis. Arterial plaque rupture, heparin induced thrombocytopenia and vasculitis are separate mechanisms that do not account for this picture.
  19. The correct answer is C (Dual blood supply from bronchial arteries protects the lung parenchyma from infarction): The lung has a bronchial arterial supply in addition to the pulmonary arteries, so infarction is uncommon unless the bronchial circulation is compromised, as in left heart failure, or emboli lodge in small peripheral branches. A saddle embolus mainly causes haemodynamic collapse. The other options misstate pulmonary vascular physiology.
  20. The correct answer is B (Lines of Zahn consisting of alternating platelet and fibrin layers with red cell layers): Lines of Zahn are grossly and microscopically visible laminations produced when thrombus forms in flowing blood, and they distinguish an antemortem thrombus from a postmortem clot, which is gelatinous and not attached. Recanalisation indicates an old thrombus. Cholesterol clefts belong to atheroma.
  21. The correct answer is C (Tumour release of procoagulant mucins and tissue factor activating the coagulation cascade): Migratory thrombophlebitis in a patient with visceral cancer, classically pancreatic adenocarcinoma, is Trousseau syndrome. Tumour mucins and tissue factor promote a hypercoagulable state. Factor V Leiden is inherited and presents with a lifelong tendency, and the other mechanisms do not fit this clinical setting.
  22. The correct answer is C (Spontaneous involution over several years with fibrofatty replacement of the vascular channels): Infantile haemangioma is a benign endothelial neoplasm that is GLUT1 positive, grows in the first months and then involutes, usually by age five to ten, leaving fibrofatty tissue. Vascular malformations lack GLUT1 staining and grow with the child. Malignant change is extremely rare.
  23. The correct answer is A (No intervening brain parenchyma, with hyalinised walls lacking smooth muscle and elastic lamina): Cerebral cavernous malformations are clusters of thin walled sinusoidal vessels with no interposed neural tissue and show old haemorrhage with haemosiderin and calcification. Familial cases involve CCM1, CCM2 or CCM3 mutations. Arteriovenous malformations contain gliotic brain tissue between feeding arteries and veins, and neoplasm or granulomas do not form this lesion.
  24. The correct answer is B (Occupational exposure to vinyl chloride monomer, also seen with arsenic and thorium dioxide): Hepatic angiosarcoma is a rare malignant endothelial tumour linked to vinyl chloride, arsenic and Thorotrast exposure. Hepatitis C, oral contraceptive related adenoma and human herpesvirus 8 are linked to other liver or vascular tumours with different histology.
  25. The correct answer is D (Pulmonary arteriovenous malformation creating a right to left shunt that bypasses lung filtration): Hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia results from mutations in ENG or ACVRL1, affecting TGF beta signalling, with mucocutaneous telangiectasia and visceral arteriovenous malformations. Pulmonary malformations let venous blood and septic emboli bypass the capillary bed, causing hypoxaemia and paradoxical cerebral abscess. Cardiac and bronchial lesions do not fit this familial condition.
  26. The correct answer is B (Plexiform lesions in small pulmonary arteries, with a BMPR2 mutation in heritable cases): Pulmonary arterial hypertension shows medial hypertrophy, intimal fibrosis and plexiform lesions of small muscular arteries, and heritable forms carry BMPR2 mutations. Organised thrombi in large arteries define chronic thromboembolic disease, and pulmonary venous change reflects left heart disease. Neither of these fits the stated findings.
  27. The correct answer is C (Irreversible intimal fibrosis and medial hypertrophy of small arteries from prolonged increased flow): Chronic left to right shunting subjects the pulmonary arterial bed to high flow and pressure, producing medial hypertrophy, intimal fibrosis and eventually plexiform lesions. Resistance rises until it exceeds systemic resistance, and the shunt reverses to produce cyanosis. This change is structural and not readily reversed, unlike simple vasoconstriction.
  28. The correct answer is D (Incomplete resolution of thromboemboli, with organised fibrotic residue within the pulmonary arteries): Chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension follows acute emboli that fail to lyse, leaving fibrous webs and bands in the proximal arteries and secondary small vessel disease. Unmatched perfusion defects point to a vascular obstruction rather than parenchymal fibrosis or alveolar haemorrhage, and pulmonary endarterectomy is a recognised treatment.
  29. The correct answer is B (Human herpesvirus 8, which drives endothelial proliferation through viral genes): Kaposi sarcoma is a vascular tumour caused by human herpesvirus 8, also called Kaposi sarcoma herpesvirus, in the setting of immunosuppression. Histology shows spindle cells with slit like channels and hyaline globules. Epstein Barr virus, papillomavirus and hepatitis B cause other cancers.
  30. The correct answer is A (The glomus body, a specialised arteriovenous anastomosis controlling skin temperature): Glomus tumours are benign perivascular tumours of modified smooth muscle cells that normally regulate blood flow in the glomus body of the dermis, and they occur at the nail bed and are exquisitely painful. Meissner corpuscles, chemoreceptors and sweat glands are not their normal counterparts.
  31. The correct answer is A (Concentric laminated smooth muscle and basement membrane thickening with fibrinoid necrosis, unlike hyaline deposits): Malignant hypertension causes hyperplastic arteriolosclerosis, with onion skin lamination of arterioles and fibrinoid necrosis, leading to renal failure with petechial haemorrhages. Hyaline arteriolosclerosis is the pattern of benign hypertension and diabetes. Medial calcification and atheroma affect other vessels.
  32. The correct answer is A (Monckeberg medial calcific sclerosis in the muscular arteries of an elderly patient): Monckeberg sclerosis is calcification of the media of muscular arteries in older adults, usually causing no stenosis and rarely any ischaemia, though it makes vessels incompressible. Atherosclerosis involves the intima and narrows the lumen. Hyaline arteriolosclerosis and fibromuscular dysplasia differ in vessel size, location and patient group.
  33. The correct answer is D (Absent internal elastic lamina and deficient media at the arterial branch point): Berry aneurysms arise at bifurcations of the circle of Willis where the internal elastic lamina and media are deficient, with haemodynamic stress and associations including autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease. Rupture causes subarachnoid haemorrhage. Vasculitis, atheroma and septic emboli cause different aneurysm types.
  34. The correct answer is D (Absorption of trapped alveolar gas into the blood distal to an occluded airway): Complete bronchial obstruction stops air entering, and gas already in the alveoli is absorbed into pulmonary capillaries, so the lung collapses and the mediastinum shifts toward the affected side. Pleural effusion compression produces contralateral shift. Surfactant loss is the mechanism of neonatal and adult respiratory distress. Scarring explains cicatrisation atelectasis, which is not the main process behind a central obstructing tumour.
  35. The correct answer is B (Destruction of respiratory bronchioles with relative sparing of the distal alveoli): Centriacinar emphysema is the smoking related pattern, affecting the proximal acinus around respiratory bronchioles and favouring the upper lobes. Panacinar disease involves the whole acinus and is basal. Paraseptal disease is subpleural and may cause pneumothorax. Bronchiolitis obliterans causes air trapping through airway fibrosis rather than parenchymal destruction.
  36. The correct answer is D (Unopposed neutrophil elastase digestion of alveolar walls throughout the entire acinus): Alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency leaves neutrophil elastase unopposed, giving panacinar emphysema with a basal predominance, and hepatocyte retention of misfolded protein explains the liver disease. Bronchiectasis is airway dilatation. Asthma shows eosinophils and smooth muscle hypertrophy. Lymphangioleiomyomatosis produces thin-walled cysts in women and is not basal emphysema.
  37. The correct answer is D (Hypertrophy of submucosal mucous glands with a Reid index greater than 0.5): Chronic bronchitis is a clinical diagnosis, and the pathological correlate is mucous gland hypertrophy measured by the Reid index, the ratio of gland thickness to wall thickness. Basement membrane thickening with eosinophils and Charcot-Leyden crystals belongs to asthma. Caseating granulomas indicate tuberculosis. Cartilage destruction and dilatation define bronchiectasis.
  38. The correct answer is D (Smooth muscle hypertrophy, thickened basement membrane, and eosinophil rich submucosal inflammation): Fatal asthma shows airway remodelling with smooth muscle hypertrophy, basement membrane thickening and an eosinophil and T helper 2 rich infiltrate, plus mucus plugs. Alveolar neutrophilic exudate is pneumonia. Gland enlargement dominates chronic bronchitis. Granulomas along bronchovascular bundles characterise sarcoidosis.
  39. The correct answer is C (Defective chloride and bicarbonate secretion producing dehydrated viscous mucus that impairs mucociliary clearance): CFTR dysfunction reduces chloride and bicarbonate secretion and increases sodium absorption, producing thick mucus, chronic infection and bronchiectasis with an upper lobe distribution. Primary ciliary dyskinesia may share bronchiectasis but has normal sweat chloride. Hypersensitivity pneumonitis is an interstitial process. Cartilage deficiency is a rare congenital cause.
  40. The correct answer is A (Hypersensitivity reaction to fungal antigens colonising the airways): Central bronchiectasis with mucoid impaction in an asthmatic with high IgE indicates allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis, a type I and type III reaction to Aspergillus. Aspiration produces basal disease. Tuberculosis scarring is usually focal and apical. Cystic fibrosis has upper lobe predominance but peripheral and diffuse features and a normal IgE pattern.
  41. The correct answer is D (Grey hepatisation with fibrinosuppurative exudate and breakdown of red cells): Grey hepatisation occurs around days four to six, when red cells have lysed and the alveoli contain fibrin and neutrophils, giving a dry grey cut surface. Red hepatisation has abundant red cells. Congestion occurs in the first day. Resolution follows with enzymatic clearance and may leave organising pneumonia if incomplete.
  42. The correct answer is C (Patchy suppurative consolidation centred on bronchi and bronchioles, often bilateral and basal): Bronchopneumonia begins in the airways and produces patchy neutrophil rich consolidation, typically bibasal in the elderly or debilitated. Lobar pneumonia fills an entire lobe. Atypical pneumonias are interstitial. Lung abscess is a cavity with suppuration, a complication rather than the basic pattern.
  43. The correct answer is D (Liquefactive necrosis from anaerobic bacteria after aspiration of oropharyngeal contents): Aspiration in an alcoholic patient seeds anaerobic and mixed oral flora into dependent segments, producing suppurative liquefactive necrosis and a cavity with an air-fluid level. Tuberculosis caseates and is usually apical. Infarcts are coagulative and wedge shaped. Rheumatoid nodules rarely contain air-fluid levels.
  44. The correct answer is B (Ghon complex, a subpleural parenchymal focus with caseating hilar lymph node involvement): The Ghon complex is the subpleural parenchymal focus (Ghon focus) in the lower upper lobe or upper lower lobe plus caseating hilar lymph node involvement. A Ranke complex is the later fibrocalcific healed form. A Simon focus is an apical seeded lesion from haematogenous spread. An Assmann focus is the early infraclavicular lesion of reactivation.
  45. The correct answer is C (Delayed type hypersensitivity in a sensitised host with high oxygen tension favouring bacillary growth): Secondary tuberculosis arises in a previously sensitised host. Strong hypersensitivity causes caseation and liquefaction, with cavities forming in the well oxygenated apices. Haematogenous seeding in a naive host results in miliary or disseminated disease. Nodal obstruction causes segmental or middle lobe collapse. Pleural extension is a complication, not the origin.
  46. The correct answer is A (Haematogenous dissemination producing millet seed sized granulomas in multiple organs): Miliary tuberculosis follows erosion of a focus into a vein or lymphatic duct draining to the bloodstream, seeding granulomas of about 2 mm throughout lungs, liver, spleen and marrow. Endobronchial spread gives centrilobular tree-in-bud nodules. Lymphatic spread produces perilymphatic nodules. Aspiration affects dependent zones.
  47. The correct answer is B (Concentric whorled hyalinised collagen with birefringent particles at the periphery): Silicotic nodules show concentric layers of hyalinised collagen with weakly birefringent silica particles at the periphery, and eggshell calcification of hilar nodes is a classic radiological clue. Schaumann bodies belong to sarcoidosis. Asbestos bodies characterise asbestosis. Coal macules occur in simple coal workers' pneumoconiosis with less collagen.
  48. The correct answer is A (Interstitial fibrosis accompanied by iron-coated fibres within the lung parenchyma): Asbestosis is diagnosed by a UIP-like fibrosis plus asbestos bodies, which are asbestos fibres coated with iron and protein. Fibroblast foci with temporal heterogeneity occur in IPF too and cannot discriminate. Uniform inflammation fits NSIP. Intraluminal granulation polyps indicate organising pneumonia.
  49. The correct answer is D (Coal macule composed of carbon laden macrophages surrounding respiratory bronchioles): Simple coal workers' pneumoconiosis produces coal macules, accumulations of carbon laden macrophages around respiratory bronchioles, with macronodules containing collagen as it advances. Caplan nodules need rheumatoid arthritis. Progressive massive fibrosis is the complicated stage larger than 2 cm. Whorled collagen with birefringent silica belongs to silicosis.
  50. The correct answer is C (A positive beryllium lymphocyte proliferation test with exposure history): Chronic beryllium disease is a type IV hypersensitivity producing sarcoid-like granulomas, and the discriminator is documented exposure with a positive lymphocyte proliferation test. Schaumann and asteroid bodies occur in both. ACE elevation is nonspecific. Perilymphatic distribution and hilar nodes are common to both diseases.
  51. The correct answer is C (Non-necrotising epithelioid granulomas with multinucleate giant cells and a sparse lymphocytic rim): Sarcoid granulomas are compact, non-necrotising collections of epithelioid cells with giant cells, often containing Schaumann or asteroid bodies, in a perilymphatic distribution. Caseation suggests tuberculosis. Palisading necrobiosis suggests rheumatoid disease or an infective process. Geographic necrosis with vasculitis points to granulomatosis with polyangiitis.
  52. The correct answer is B (Cellular bronchiolitis, interstitial chronic inflammation, and poorly formed non-necrotising granulomas): Hypersensitivity pneumonitis combines bronchiolocentric inflammation, interstitial lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate and small loose granulomas, with air trapping from small airway involvement. Diffuse alveolar damage characterises ARDS. Fibroblast foci and honeycombing indicate UIP. Eosinophilic disease shows prominent eosinophils rather than lymphocytes.
  53. The correct answer is C (Patchy fibrosis of varying age with fibroblast foci alternating with normal lung): Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis shows the UIP pattern: temporal heterogeneity, fibroblast foci at the leading edge, subpleural honeycombing and spared intervening lung. NSIP is temporally uniform. Organising pneumonia has intraluminal granulation tissue. Alveolar proteinosis fills airspaces with lipoproteinaceous material and no fibrosis.
  54. The correct answer is B (Temporally uniform interstitial inflammation and fibrosis with preserved lung architecture): NSIP is the typical pattern in systemic sclerosis, with uniform cellular or fibrotic septal thickening and sparing of the immediate subpleural lung on CT. UIP has temporal heterogeneity and honeycombing. Desquamative interstitial pneumonia is dominated by intra-alveolar macrophages. Hyaline membranes reflect acute lung injury.
  55. The correct answer is C (Eosinophilic membranes of fibrin and necrotic epithelial debris lining alveolar ducts): The exudative phase of ARDS shows diffuse alveolar damage with hyaline membranes, oedema and endothelial and type I pneumocyte injury. Segmental neutrophilic exudate is pneumonia. Granulation plugs form organising pneumonia or later proliferative repair. Haemosiderin macrophages indicate chronic congestion.
  56. The correct answer is A (Deep veins of the lower limb, typically at or above the popliteal level): Most pulmonary emboli arise from deep vein thrombosis in the legs, especially the popliteal, femoral and iliac veins. Superficial varicose veins seldom embolise. Right heart thrombi do occur but are less frequent. Pelvic vein thrombosis is a recognised source in gynaecological settings but is not the typical orthopaedic post-operative origin.
  57. The correct answer is A (Acute pressure overload of the right ventricle with reduced left ventricular filling): Massive central embolism abruptly raises pulmonary vascular resistance, so the right ventricle fails and the septum bows toward the left, limiting left ventricular filling and cardiac output. Infarction is a peripheral phenomenon in a minority of cases. Reperfusion injury is not the mechanism of collapse. Paradoxical coronary embolism requires a right to left shunt.
  58. The correct answer is D (Haemorrhagic coagulative necrosis of lung tissue with the base on the pleural surface): Pulmonary infarcts are red, haemorrhagic and wedge shaped with a pleural base, because bronchial arterial flow is limited and the necrosis is coagulative. They are more likely in the setting of pre-existing cardiac or pulmonary disease. Caseation implies tuberculosis. Abscesses are liquefactive. Organising pneumonia does not produce a wedge of coagulative necrosis.
  59. The correct answer is C (BMPR2, a bone morphogenetic protein receptor gene on chromosome two): Heritable pulmonary arterial hypertension is most often due to loss of function mutations in BMPR2, producing endothelial dysfunction, medial hypertrophy, intimal fibrosis and plexiform lesions. CFTR causes cystic fibrosis. SERPINA1 variants cause alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency. TSC2 mutations underlie tuberous sclerosis and lymphangioleiomyomatosis.
  60. The correct answer is C (Smooth linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane): Anti-GBM disease shows linear IgG along the basement membrane because antibodies target the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen, and the pulmonary haemorrhage is more likely in smokers. Granular deposits occur in immune complex disease. Pauci-immune crescents are typical of ANCA vasculitis. Mesangial IgA suggests IgA nephropathy.
  61. The correct answer is D (Geographic necrosis with granulomatous inflammation and necrotising vasculitis of small vessels): Granulomatosis with polyangiitis shows geographic basophilic necrosis with granulomas and small vessel vasculitis, with upper airway, lung and kidney involvement. Caseation with acid-fast bacilli indicates tuberculosis. Eosinophilic granulomatosis is typically asthma associated with p-ANCA. Hyaline membranes reflect ARDS.
  62. The correct answer is B (Foamy eosinophilic intra-alveolar exudate with cysts staining on silver methenamine): Pneumocystis jirovecii produces a frothy eosinophilic alveolar exudate and cysts that stain with Gomori methenamine silver. Acutely branching septate hyphae with angioinvasion indicate Aspergillus. Encapsulated budding yeast is Cryptococcus. Owl's eye intranuclear inclusions indicate cytomegalovirus.
  63. The correct answer is C (Fungal ball of intertwined hyphae within a pre-existing cavity without tissue invasion): An aspergilloma is a saprophytic colonisation of a previous cavity, with a mobile fungal ball producing the air crescent and bleeding from the cavity wall. Angioinvasion occurs in immunocompromised hosts. Allergic disease shows eosinophilic mucin and bronchiectasis. Histoplasmosis is a granulomatous yeast infection.
  64. The correct answer is B (Haemorrhage surrounding a nodule of infarcted lung caused by fungal vascular invasion): The CT halo sign in invasive aspergillosis reflects haemorrhage around a nodule of coagulative necrosis, from hyphae invading and thrombosing small pulmonary arteries. Lipoproteinosis is a separate disease. Caseating granuloma is not the substrate in a neutropenic patient. Eosinophilic infiltrates occur in allergic forms rather than angioinvasive disease.
  65. The correct answer is D (Histoplasma capsulatum, a dimorphic fungus found in soil enriched by bird or bat droppings): Histoplasma is a small intracellular yeast found in macrophages, and healed disease produces calcified granulomas in the lungs, nodes and spleen. Cryptococcus has a mucicarmine positive capsule and is extracellular. Coccidioides forms large spherules with endospores. Blastomyces shows larger yeasts with broad-based budding.
  66. The correct answer is C (CD1a and langerin positivity, with Birbeck granules on electron microscopy): Pulmonary Langerhans cell histiocytosis is a smoking related disease where Langerhans cells express CD1a and langerin and contain Birbeck granules, evolving from nodules to cavitating nodules to cysts. CD68 alone marks macrophages. CD20 marks B cells. Cytokeratin with TTF-1 suggests lung adenocarcinoma.
  67. The correct answer is B (Perivascular epithelioid smooth muscle like cells staining for HMB-45 and actin): Lymphangioleiomyomatosis, associated with TSC mutations and sporadic in women of reproductive age, results from proliferation of perivascular epithelioid cells that express smooth muscle actin and HMB-45, forming cysts and obstructing lymphatics. Langerhans cell histiocytosis spares women less often and is smoking related. The other cell types do not explain renal angiomyolipoma.
  68. The correct answer is C (Buds of loose fibroblastic tissue filling alveolar ducts and adjacent alveoli): Organising pneumonia shows plugs of granulation tissue (Masson bodies) in alveolar ducts and alveoli with preserved lung architecture and mild chronic inflammation. Hyaline membranes indicate DAD. Fibroblast foci with honeycombing indicate UIP. Necrotising granulomas suggest infection or vasculitis, not a steroid responsive organising process.
  69.
