The correct answer is B (Impaired surfactant clearance by alveolar macrophages due to antibodies against GM-CSF): Autoimmune pulmonary alveolar proteinosis follows neutralising antibodies to GM-CSF that impair macrophage surfactant catabolism, filling alveoli with PAS positive lipoproteinaceous material. Overproduction is not the cause. Surfactant protein B mutations cause neonatal respiratory failure. Inflammatory exudate would produce diffuse alveolar damage with hyaline membranes.
  70. The correct answer is D (Eosinophilic hyaline membranes lining collapsed distal airspaces and alveolar ducts): Neonatal respiratory distress syndrome results from surfactant deficiency in prematurity, with atelectasis, hyaline membranes and a ground glass granular radiographic pattern. Group B streptococcal pneumonia can mimic it but shows neutrophils and organisms. Bronchopulmonary dysplasia is a later complication of ventilation. Meconium aspiration affects term or post-term infants.
  71. The correct answer is B (Necrotic centre with concentric dust rings surrounded by palisaded histiocytes): Rheumatoid pneumoconiosis (Caplan syndrome) produces larger peripheral nodules with a necrobiotic core, concentric dust layers and palisading histiocytes in dust exposed workers with rheumatoid arthritis. Whorled hyalinised collagen is silicotic. Sarcoid granulomas are non-necrotising and perilymphatic. The coal macule is the simple stage, without necrobiosis.
  72. The correct answer is A (Distal acinar emphysema affecting the subpleural lung adjacent to septa and pleura): Paraseptal (distal acinar) emphysema involves the periphery of the lobule along the pleura and septa, forming subpleural blebs and bullae that rupture to cause spontaneous pneumothorax in young tall men. Centriacinar disease is smoking related and proximal. Panacinar disease is basal and associated with alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency. Irregular emphysema follows scarring.
  73. The correct answer is A (Neoplastic cells lining intact alveolar walls with no stromal, vascular or pleural invasion): A pure ground-glass nodule reflects tumour cells replacing the alveolar lining while air-containing spaces and the septal architecture are preserved. Absence of invasion explains the indolent course over years. Solid sheets with necrosis would give a dense, enlarging lesion, a scar centre implies invasive disease, and hamartoma shows cartilage and fat rather than atypical lining cells.
  74. The correct answer is A (Keratinising cells joined by intercellular bridges, secreting parathyroid hormone-related peptide): Central cavitating tumours in heavy smokers are typically squamous cell carcinoma, which shows keratin and intercellular bridges. Hypercalcaemia with suppressed parathyroid hormone is humoral hypercalcaemia of malignancy from parathyroid hormone-related peptide. Small cell carcinoma produces ACTH or ADH, adenocarcinoma is peripheral and rarely cavitates, and erythropoietin excess belongs to renal and hepatic tumours.
  75. The correct answer is C (Scant cytoplasm, finely granular chromatin lacking nucleoli, nuclear moulding and a very high mitotic rate): Small cell carcinoma has scant cytoplasm, nuclear moulding, fine chromatin without nucleoli and a very high mitotic count, and crush artefact is typical because the cells are fragile. The hyponatraemia is SIADH from ectopic antidiuretic hormone. Keratin pearls point to squamous carcinoma, mucin and glands to adenocarcinoma, and organoid nests with rare mitoses to carcinoid.
  76. The correct answer is B (Atypical carcinoid, defined by two to ten mitoses per 2 mm2 or foci of necrosis): Carcinoids are separated by mitotic count and necrosis: typical has under two mitoses per 2 mm2 and no necrosis, atypical has two to ten mitoses or necrosis. This tumour has four mitoses and focal necrosis so it is atypical. High-grade large cell neuroendocrine and small cell carcinomas exceed ten mitoses and show extensive necrosis.
  77. The correct answer is D (Lower plexus roots (C8, T1) and the stellate ganglion or sympathetic chain): A superior sulcus tumour grows through the apical pleura into the lower trunk and roots of the brachial plexus, producing ulnar-side arm pain. The adjacent sympathetic chain and stellate ganglion lie on the neck of the first rib, so invasion gives Horner syndrome. Phrenic and recurrent laryngeal injury cause diaphragm paralysis and hoarseness, and the upper trunk supplies the lateral arm.
  78. The correct answer is D (Activating mutation in the EGFR tyrosine kinase domain, often an exon 19 deletion): EGFR activating mutations, especially exon 19 deletion and L858R, are enriched in never-smoking women of East Asian ancestry with adenocarcinoma and predict response to tyrosine kinase inhibitors. KRAS mutations track with smoking, and ALK fusion affects younger never-smokers rather than older smokers. RB1 and TP53 loss defines small cell carcinoma.
  79. The correct answer is C (Disorganised mature cartilage and fat with clefts lined by benign respiratory epithelium): Fat with popcorn calcification in a well-defined nodule indicates a hamartoma, a benign mesenchymal lesion of cartilage, fat and connective tissue with entrapped respiratory epithelium forming clefts. Carcinoid is a solid enhancing endobronchial tumour, a healed granuloma may calcify but has no fat, and lepidic adenocarcinoma has no cartilage.
  80. The correct answer is B (Tumour emboli and permeation within perilymphatic channels of the pulmonary interstitium): Lymphangitic spread produces tumour in the lymphatics along the septa and bronchovascular bundles, thickening them in a smooth or beaded fashion while the lobular architecture remains intact. Haematogenous metastases form round nodules, airspace exudate fills alveoli, and sarcoid granulomas also follow lymphatics but do not explain the effusions in a patient with known breast cancer.
  81. The correct answer is A (T2b, as the tumour exceeds 4 cm but does not exceed 5 cm): For lung cancer, T1 is 3 cm or less, T2a exceeds 3 cm up to 4 cm, T2b exceeds 4 cm up to 5 cm, T3 exceeds 5 cm up to 7 cm and T4 exceeds 7 cm. A 4.5 cm tumour without local invasion therefore sits in T2b. The absence of nodal and distant disease does not alter the T category.
  82. The correct answer is C (Small cell carcinoma, with antibodies against presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channels): Facilitation of strength with exercise, autonomic dryness and areflexia describe Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome, caused by antibodies to presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channels that reduce acetylcholine release. It is most often paraneoplastic with small cell carcinoma. Postsynaptic acetylcholine receptor antibodies cause myasthenia gravis, and anti-Hu causes sensory neuronopathy.
  83. The correct answer is A (They are acellular hyaline collagen deposits on the parietal pleura without malignant potential): Asbestos pleural plaques are parietal, basket-weave hyaline collagen with few cells and often calcify. They mark exposure but are not precursors of mesothelioma, and they spare the costophrenic angles and apices. Diffuse visceral pleural fibrosis, early sarcomatoid change and postinfective fibrothorax have different distributions and histology.
  84. The correct answer is D (Epithelioid is most frequent, while sarcomatoid has the poorest prognosis): Epithelioid mesothelioma accounts for roughly 60 percent of cases and carries the best outlook, biphasic disease is intermediate, and sarcomatoid disease is least common with the worst survival. The CT description of a circumferential rind encasing the lung with volume loss is typical of any subtype and does not discriminate between them.
  85. The correct answer is A (Chronic pleural inflammation impairing glucose transport and raising local cellular metabolism): Rheumatoid effusions are exudates with very low glucose and pH because inflamed pleura traps fluid, blocks glucose entry and consumes it locally, with cholesterol crystals from chronicity. Heart failure and nephrotic syndrome produce transudates with normal glucose, and chylothorax contains high triglyceride with milky appearance.
  86. The correct answer is D (Thickened parietal and visceral pleura from fibrin and inflammation enclosing purulent fluid): In the fibrinopurulent stage of a parapneumonic effusion fibrin and granulation tissue thicken both pleural layers, which enhance with contrast and are separated by pus, giving the split pleura sign. Mesothelioma forms a nodular rind, plaques are calcified parietal and non-enhancing, and a transudate would not show enhancing layers.
  87. The correct answer is A (NAB2-STAT6 gene fusion causing strong nuclear STAT6 expression): A pedunculated pleural mass of bland spindle cells with staghorn vessels is a solitary fibrous tumour, defined by NAB2-STAT6 fusion and nuclear STAT6 staining. Hypoglycaemia is the Doege-Potter syndrome from tumour IGF-II. Ewing and synovial sarcomas have different fusions, and CDKN2A and BAP1 loss characterise mesothelioma.
  88. The correct answer is B (Infolding of lung into thickened visceral pleura with collapse of enfolded parenchyma): Rounded atelectasis, or folded lung, arises when an effusion or pleural fibrosis causes the visceral pleura to invaginate, trapping and collapsing adjacent lung. Vessels sweep into the mass as a comet tail, and it is usually seen with asbestos pleural disease. Cancer, abscess and infarct lack this configuration.
  89. The correct answer is A (Disruption of the thoracic duct with leakage of chylomicron-rich lymph): Triglyceride above 1.1 mmol/L in milky lymphocytic fluid confirms chylothorax. The thoracic duct runs in the posterior mediastinum beside the oesophagus and is vulnerable at oesophagectomy. Venous injury gives blood, an anastomotic leak gives infected acidic fluid, and phrenic injury does not cause an effusion.
  90. The correct answer is B (Yolk sac tumour elements within a mixed non-seminomatous germ cell tumour): Marked alpha-fetoprotein elevation in a mediastinal germ cell tumour indicates yolk sac differentiation, so the tumour is non-seminomatous. Mild hCG elevation can come from syncytiotrophoblast cells. Pure seminoma does not raise AFP, embryonal carcinoma alone usually does not, and mature teratoma is benign.
  91. The correct answer is C (Autoantibodies to postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction): Fatigable ocular and bulbar weakness with a thymoma indicates myasthenia gravis, caused by antibodies against postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors. Thymoma can also associate with pure red cell aplasia and hypogammaglobulinaemia, but these do not cause fatigable weakness. Presynaptic calcium channel antibodies cause Lambert-Eaton syndrome.
  92. The correct answer is B (Stage III, macroscopic invasion of neighbouring organs such as pericardium or vessels): In Masaoka-Koga staging, stage I is encapsulated, II is microscopic capsular or macroscopic fat and pleural invasion, III is macroscopic invasion of neighbouring organs, IVa is pleural or pericardial dissemination and IVb is lymphogenous or haematogenous metastasis. Invasion of the great vein and pericardium without implants is therefore stage III.
  93. The correct answer is D (Rebound hyperplasia with preserved architecture and microscopic fat in normal zones): Thymic enlargement after chemotherapy is common rebound hyperplasia, with normal shape and architecture. Microscopic fat in the gland causes signal loss on opposed-phase imaging, which a lymphoma or thymoma lacks. Thymolipoma has macroscopic fat and drapes over adjacent structures rather than preserving the gland's shape.
  94. The correct answer is A (It contains mature derivatives of ectoderm, mesoderm and endoderm, such as skin and cartilage): Mature cystic teratoma is the commonest mediastinal germ cell tumour and is benign, composed of mature tissues from all three germ layers, producing the combination of fluid, fat and calcification. Immature teratoma contains primitive neural elements, seminoma is solid with lymphocyte-rich septa, and a pericardial cyst has simple mesothelial lining.
  95. The correct answer is A (An additional X chromosome giving a 47,XXY constitutional karyotype): Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY) carries an increased risk of mediastinal non-seminomatous germ cell tumours, and the described phenotype fits. Isochromosome 12p is a somatic change within germ cell tumours rather than an inherited predisposition. Turner and Down syndromes have different tumour associations.
  96. The correct answer is C (Nodular sclerosis classical Hodgkin lymphoma with Reed-Sternberg variant cells): Lacunar Reed-Sternberg variant cells in collagen bands with CD30 and CD15 positivity, weak PAX5 and absent CD45 define nodular sclerosis classical Hodgkin lymphoma. Primary mediastinal large B-cell lymphoma expresses CD45 and CD20 and is often CD15 negative. Thymoma and seminoma are epithelial and germ cell tumours with different markers.
  97. The correct answer is C (Ciliated columnar lining with mucous glands, smooth muscle and cartilage in the wall): Bronchogenic cysts derive from the ventral foregut bud and typically sit near the carina, lined by respiratory epithelium with mucous glands, cartilage and smooth muscle. Pericardial cysts have mesothelial lining, oesophageal duplication cysts have a double muscle coat with myenteric plexus, and keratinising lining suggests teratoma.
  98. The correct answer is C (Diffuse strong nuclear and cytoplasmic S100 protein positivity): Alternating Antoni A cellular and Antoni B myxoid areas with nuclear palisading define a schwannoma, a Schwann cell tumour that is diffusely S100 positive. The foraminal widening arises from a dumbbell extension along the nerve root. Cytokeratin marks epithelial tumours, synaptophysin marks neuroblastoma and STAT6 marks solitary fibrous tumour.
  99. The correct answer is D (Amplification of the MYCN oncogene on the short arm of chromosome 2): Raised catecholamine metabolites with Homer Wright rosettes in a young child establish neuroblastoma. MYCN amplification is the key adverse genetic marker. Ewing sarcoma and alveolar rhabdomyosarcoma are other small round blue cell tumours with their own fusions, and NF1 loss underlies neurofibromas rather than neuroblastoma.
  100. The correct answer is C (Hyaline vascular Castleman disease, a localised unicentric lymphoproliferative disorder): Atrophic germinal centres with onion-skin mantle layers and penetrating hyalinised vessels give the lollipop appearance of hyaline vascular Castleman disease, typically a solitary, intensely enhancing mass without systemic symptoms. The plasma cell variant shows interfollicular plasma cells and fever, follicular lymphoma lacks mantles, and sarcoidosis shows granulomas.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Thymus, fat, lymph nodes and the left brachiocephalic vein): The prevascular compartment lies in front of the pericardium and great vessels and contains the thymus, fat, lymph nodes and left brachiocephalic vein, which is why the classic anterior mass differential is thymic, germ cell, thyroid and lymphoma. The visceral compartment holds the heart, airway, oesophagus and great vessels, and the paravertebral compartment lies behind.
  2. The correct answer is A (Transmural tear of the distal left posterolateral oesophagus above the diaphragm): Barotrauma from forceful vomiting against a closed upper sphincter tears the full thickness of the distal oesophagus, classically on the left posterolateral wall, leaking air and contents into the mediastinum and pleura. Mallory-Weiss tears are mucosal and cause haematemesis, and cervical perforation or aortic rupture have different mechanisms.
  3. The correct answer is A (Exuberant fibrosis driven by an immune reaction to fungal antigen in mediastinal nodes): Fibrosing mediastinitis is a dense fibroinflammatory response, most often to Histoplasma capsulatum antigen, that encases the mediastinal vessels and airways and causes superior vena cava and pulmonary vein obstruction. Calcified nodes are typical. Active necrotising infection, metastatic nodes and sarcoid granulomas have different appearances.
  4. The correct answer is C (Effects are synergistic, with risk multiplied well beyond the sum of each exposure): Asbestos and tobacco act synergistically, so the relative risk of bronchial carcinoma in a smoker with asbestos exposure is far greater than the sum of each alone. Asbestos also causes mesothelioma, but it raises lung carcinoma risk too, and plaques are markers of exposure rather than precursors.
  5. The correct answer is A (Activating KRAS mutation, most often affecting codon 12 or 13): Invasive mucinous adenocarcinoma presents as pneumonia-like consolidation with bronchorrhoea and is strongly associated with KRAS mutation, whereas EGFR and ALK alterations are uncommon. RB1 and TP53 loss define small cell carcinoma. The mucin-laden columnar cells spread along alveolar walls and across lobes.
  6. The correct answer is B (Strong nuclear p40 and p63 with absent TTF-1 and napsin A staining): Squamous cell carcinoma expresses p40 and p63, usually with no TTF-1 or napsin A, which mark adenocarcinoma. Neuroendocrine markers with a high Ki-67 suggest small cell or large cell neuroendocrine carcinoma, and calretinin, D2-40 and WT1 point to mesothelioma, which is not a carcinoma.
  7. The correct answer is D (Thymic carcinoma, typically squamous, with no lymphocyte-rich thymoma background): Thymic carcinoma shows frank cytological atypia, lacks the immature T cell component that defines thymoma, and often expresses CD5 and KIT. Type B3 thymoma is the closest mimic because it is epithelial predominant, but its cells are only mildly atypical. Types AB and B1 are lymphocyte-rich with bland cytology.
  8. The correct answer is D (Haematogenous spread via pulmonary arteries, with arrest in peripheral capillary beds): Sarcomas typically spread haematogenously, and tumour emboli travel to the lungs through the pulmonary arteries and lodge in basal peripheral capillary beds, forming round nodules of varying size. Lymphatic spread produces septal thickening, and aerogenous spread produces consolidation or tiny nodules.
  9. The correct answer is B (Adenocarcinoma of the lung, the type most often producing hypertrophic osteoarthropathy): Hypertrophic osteoarthropathy produces clubbing and periosteal new bone and is most frequently seen with non-small cell lung cancer, particularly adenocarcinoma. It is thought to follow humoral factors such as vascular endothelial growth factor. Small cell carcinoma and carcinoid produce hormone syndromes instead.
  10. The correct answer is A (Positive calretinin, D2-40 and nuclear WT1 with negative TTF-1 and claudin-4): Mesothelioma expresses mesothelial markers such as calretinin, D2-40, WT1 and CK5/6 and lacks carcinoma markers such as TTF-1, claudin-4, MOC-31, BG8 and CEA. Lung adenocarcinoma shows the reverse. PAX8 and oestrogen receptor suggest a Mullerian primary, and a panel of two positive and two negative markers is used because no single marker is definitive.
  11. The correct answer is D (Intestinal metaplasia with goblet cells replacing the squamous lining above the gastro-oesophageal junction): Barrett oesophagus is columnar metaplasia of the distal oesophagus, and goblet cells (intestinal metaplasia) are the feature that marks it as the cancer-predisposing change. Gastric-type columnar lining is tempting because it is also columnar, but it lacks goblet cells. Basal cell hyperplasia and eosinophils are changes of reflux oesophagitis within squamous mucosa. Glycogenic acanthosis is a benign squamous appearance.
  12. The correct answer is C (Chronic acid and bile exposure causing epithelial injury with regenerative basal hyperplasia): Basal zone expansion and elongated papillae are the reparative response to repeated acid-peptic injury of the squamous lining. A few eosinophils and neutrophils may be present, but dense clusters would point to eosinophilic oesophagitis. Herpes causes ulcers with multinucleate cells at the edge. Bullous autoimmune disease would show subepithelial splitting rather than hyperplasia.
  13. The correct answer is A (Loss of inhibitory ganglion cells in the myenteric plexus with failure of sphincter relaxation): Primary achalasia results from degeneration of inhibitory (nitric oxide and VIP) neurons in the myenteric plexus, so the sphincter cannot relax and the body loses peristalsis. Peptic stricture is a plausible cause of dysphagia but has a different history and irregular narrowing. Scleroderma leaves an open sphincter with reflux. A pharyngeal pouch lies in the neck.
  14. The correct answer is D (It arises from squamous epithelium and is most common in the middle third of the oesophagus): Alcohol and tobacco are the major risk factors for squamous carcinoma, which most often involves the middle oesophagus. Adenocarcinoma, linked to Barrett metaplasia, favours the distal third. Keratin production excludes neuroendocrine and stromal origins, and a muscularis propria stromal tumour would appear as a submucosal mass.
  15. The correct answer is C (Left gastric veins draining into the azygos system through submucosal oesophageal veins): In portal hypertension, blood from the left gastric (coronary) vein reverses into the submucosal oesophageal plexus and then the azygos system, producing varices that bleed easily because they lie directly beneath the mucosa. The rectal route gives haemorrhoids, and the paraumbilical route gives caput medusae. Retroperitoneal collaterals do not supply the oesophagus.
  16. The correct answer is D (Dense eosinophil counts at both proximal and distal levels, with eosinophil microabscesses): Eosinophilic oesophagitis is an allergen-driven condition with dense eosinophilic infiltrates (commonly 15 or more per high-power field), often in both proximal and distal samples, with microabscesses and rings or furrows at endoscopy. Reflux oesophagitis is usually distal and has mild eosinophilia. Goblet metaplasia and herpetic change are separate entities.
  17. The correct answer is B (A false diverticulum herniating through Killian dehiscence between the thyropharyngeus and cricopharyngeus muscles): Zenker diverticulum is a mucosal and submucosal pulsion pouch through a weak triangle (Killian dehiscence) above the cricopharyngeus, so it is a false diverticulum. Traction diverticula are true and mid-oesophageal. Epiphrenic pouches sit just above the diaphragm. A duplication has its own wall.
  18. The correct answer is C (Full-thickness rupture of the distal posterolateral left oesophageal wall from raised intraluminal pressure): Boerhaave syndrome is spontaneous transmural rupture, typically of the left posterolateral distal oesophagus, after a sudden rise in pressure during vomiting, giving mediastinitis, air in the soft tissues and contrast leak. Mallory-Weiss is a mucosal tear at the junction presenting with bleeding. Caustic and tumour perforation have other histories.
  19. The correct answer is D (Autoimmune destruction of parietal cells causing achlorhydria, intrinsic factor loss and G-cell hyperplasia): Antiparietal and anti-intrinsic factor antibodies destroy body and fundal parietal cells, producing achlorhydria, loss of intrinsic factor (vitamin B12 deficiency) and compensatory antral G-cell hyperplasia with high gastrin. H. pylori favours the antrum, gastrinoma gives hypertrophic folds with excess acid, and NSAID injury is reactive.
  20. The correct answer is A (Most are linked to Helicobacter pylori infection and the ulcer is rarely malignant): Duodenal ulcers are strongly associated with H. pylori and with increased acid delivery, and they are benign in the great majority of cases. Autoimmune gastritis reduces acid. Gastrinoma ulcers are multiple or in unusual sites. Heaped-up irregular edges characterise malignant gastric ulcers.
  21. The correct answer is C (Germline loss of function in CDH1 encoding E-cadherin): A rigid linitis plastica stomach with signet ring cells is diffuse-type adenocarcinoma, and hereditary diffuse gastric cancer arises from germline CDH1 mutation, which removes cell adhesion. Intestinal-type tumours are linked to H. pylori, metaplasia and mismatch repair deficiency, while the polyposis genes cause other phenotypes.
  22. The correct answer is B (Chronic Helicobacter pylori gastritis, with t(11;18) predicting poor response to eradication): Gastric extranodal marginal zone (MALT) lymphoma arises from H. pylori-driven lymphoid tissue and often regresses after eradication, though t(11;18) producing the API2-MALT1 fusion predicts resistance. t(8;14) is Burkitt, t(14;18) is follicular, and t(11;14) is mantle cell lymphoma.
  23. The correct answer is C (KIT (CD117) and DOG1 positivity with an activating KIT mutation): GIST arises from interstitial cells of Cajal and typically expresses KIT and DOG1 with activating KIT (or PDGFRA) mutations, which also underpins imatinib treatment. Smooth muscle tumours express desmin, nerve sheath tumours express S100, and epithelial tumours express cytokeratin. Size and mitotic count stratify behaviour.
  24. The correct answer is A (Benign fundic gland polyps related to long-term acid suppression): Fundic gland polyps contain dilated oxyntic glands lined by normal parietal and chief cells and are linked to prolonged PPI use, with sporadic and FAP-related forms as well. Adenomas, Peutz-Jeghers hamartomas and hyperplastic polyps have different histology.
  25. The correct answer is A (Villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia and increased intraepithelial lymphocytes): Coeliac disease, driven by HLA-DQ2 or DQ8 restricted T-cell reaction to gliadin, produces villous atrophy with crypt hyperplasia and raised intraepithelial lymphocytes. PAS-positive macrophages indicate Whipple disease, and the other options describe collagenous or lymphatic disorders with different architecture.
  26. The correct answer is B (Enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma arising from clonal intraepithelial lymphocytes of the proximal small bowel): Refractory coeliac disease and enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma are recognised complications, typically proximal jejunal with ulceration after years of disease, and small bowel adenocarcinoma risk is also raised. GIST, mantle cell lymphoma and ileal NET are not specifically linked to coeliac disease.
  27. The correct answer is C (Transmural granulomatous inflammation with skip lesions, deep fissuring ulcers and creeping fat): Crohn disease is characterised by skip lesions, transmural inflammation, noncaseating granulomas in some cases, deep fissuring ulcers, strictures and creeping fat. Ulcerative colitis is continuous and mucosal, ischaemia follows a vascular territory, and Whipple disease shows foamy macrophages.
  28. The correct answer is D (Crypt architectural distortion with cryptitis and crypt abscesses confined to mucosa and superficial submucosa): Ulcerative colitis is continuous, mucosal disease beginning in the rectum with crypt distortion, cryptitis and abscesses. Granulomas and transmural change favour Crohn. Collagenous colitis has a thickened collagen band, and C. difficile gives pseudomembranes.
  29. The correct answer is B (Noncaseating epithelioid granulomas not related to ruptured crypts): Patchy involvement and rectal sparing already point to Crohn disease, and well-formed granulomas away from damaged crypts strengthen the diagnosis. Basal plasmacytosis and diffuse crypt distortion favour ulcerative colitis, and crypt abscesses are non-specific.
  30. The correct answer is A (Hepatic metastases release serotonin into the systemic circulation beyond first-pass hepatic metabolism): Ileal NETs secrete serotonin, which is normally inactivated by the liver after portal drainage; syndrome occurs when hepatic metastases release it directly into hepatic veins. The desmoplastic mesenteric reaction explains the calcified mass. Gastrin and histamine are not the main mediators.
  31. The correct answer is C (A true ileal diverticulum containing ectopic gastric mucosa with ulceration of adjacent ileum): Meckel diverticulum is a remnant of the vitello-intestinal duct, a true diverticulum on the antimesenteric ileum, and ectopic gastric mucosa secretes acid that ulcerates adjacent ileum, which pertechnetate scanning demonstrates. The other lesions do not take up pertechnetate in this way.
  32. The correct answer is B (A pathological lead point such as a polyp or tumour is usually identified): Most childhood cases are idiopathic with lymphoid hyperplasia, but adult intussusception commonly has a structural lead point such as a polyp, lipoma or malignancy that is dragged into the distal bowel by peristalsis. The other options are paediatric or rare causes.
  33. The correct answer is B (Watershed injury between the superior and inferior mesenteric territories during low flow): The splenic flexure (Griffiths point) and rectosigmoid junction are watershed zones with marginal collateral supply, so non-occlusive low flow causes ischaemic colitis there. SMA embolus affects small bowel and right colon. Ulcerative colitis is continuous from the rectum, and the rectum is usually spared in ischaemia.
  34. The correct answer is C (Arterial embolus lodging distal to the middle colic origin, causing segmental small bowel infarction): Emboli from the atrium usually lodge just beyond the origin of the SMA, sparing the proximal jejunum and transverse colon in some cases, and cause acute infarction of the supplied bowel with pain out of proportion to signs. Venous and non-occlusive forms lack a focal arterial thrombus, and chronic stenosis is gradual.
  35. The correct answer is B (Ischaemic coagulative necrosis of immature bowel with bacterial gas production in the wall): Necrotising enterocolitis affects premature infants after feeding, combining mucosal ischaemia, immature barrier function and bacterial overgrowth, so gas-forming organisms produce pneumatosis intestinalis and portal venous gas. Hirschsprung, atresia and milk allergy have different timing and pathology.
  36. The correct answer is A (Absence of ganglion cells in the submucosal and myenteric plexuses with hypertrophic nerve trunks): Hirschsprung disease reflects failure of neural crest migration (often RET mutation) so the distal bowel lacks ganglion cells and remains tonically contracted, with proximal dilatation. Biopsy shows aganglionosis with hypertrophic nerve trunks. Neuronal dysplasia and pseudo-obstruction have other histology.
  37. The correct answer is B (Villous architecture and increasing size, with high-grade dysplasia as the pathological marker): Adenoma size, villous architecture and the grade of dysplasia determine cancer risk, with high-grade dysplasia the immediate precursor to invasion. A tubular, small polyp carries low risk. Hyperplastic and hamartomatous polyps have different, mostly low, malignant potential.
  38. The correct answer is B (APC loss, then KRAS mutation, then SMAD4 loss, then TP53 mutation): In the classical adenoma-carcinoma sequence, APC inactivation initiates adenoma formation, KRAS mutation promotes growth, then SMAD4 (18q) loss and TP53 mutation drive progression to carcinoma. The serrated pathway instead involves BRAF mutation, CIMP and MLH1 methylation.
  39. The correct answer is D (A sporadic cancer from a sessile serrated lesion with CpG island methylation silencing MLH1): Loss of MLH1 and PMS2 together with BRAF V600E points to sporadic MLH1 promoter methylation in the serrated (CIMP-high) pathway, typically proximal and in older women. Lynch tumours usually retain wild-type BRAF, APC pathway tumours retain mismatch repair, and MUTYH disease has a polyposis phenotype.
  40. The correct answer is B (Congenital hypertrophy of the retinal pigment epithelium and duodenal adenomas): Familial adenomatous polyposis arises from germline APC mutation on chromosome 5q with hundreds of adenomas, and extracolonic features include duodenal adenomas, CHRPE, desmoid tumours and osteomas. The other options describe Peutz-Jeghers, Lynch and juvenile polyposis syndromes.
  41. The correct answer is C (Germline mismatch repair gene mutation causing microsatellite instability): Lynch syndrome results from a germline mismatch repair defect (MSH2, MSH6, MLH1, PMS2), giving microsatellite-unstable proximal mucinous cancers with tumour-infiltrating lymphocytes and extra-colonic tumours such as endometrial cancer. Loss of MSH2 and MSH6 without BRAF mutation fits Lynch, not sporadic methylation or polyposis.
  42. The correct answer is B (STK11 (LKB1), a tumour suppressor kinase on chromosome 19p): Peutz-Jeghers syndrome combines mucocutaneous pigmentation with hamartomatous polyps showing arborising smooth muscle, caused by germline STK11 mutation, with raised risks of intussusception and cancers of the gut, breast, pancreas and gonads. APC, SMAD4 and MUTYH cause other polyposis phenotypes.
  43. The correct answer is B (A hamartomatous juvenile polyp, usually benign and solitary in a child): Solitary juvenile polyps are hamartomatous, typically in the rectum of children, with cystic mucus-filled glands in oedematous inflamed stroma, and rarely have malignant potential. Multiple polyps with a family history raise the possibility of juvenile polyposis syndrome (SMAD4 or BMPR1A).
  44. The correct answer is B (Autosomal recessive biallelic MUTYH mutation impairing base excision repair of oxidative DNA damage): MUTYH-associated polyposis causes an attenuated adenomatous phenotype with tens to hundreds of adenomas through recessive inheritance, with oxidative damage leaving G:C to T:A transversions, commonly in KRAS. Unaffected parents and affected siblings suggest recessive transmission. The other syndromes are dominant.
  45. The correct answer is D (pT3 N2a, tumour through muscularis propria with four positive regional lymph nodes): Invasion through the muscularis propria into pericolorectal tissue is pT3, while pT4a means penetration to the visceral peritoneal surface. Four to six positive regional nodes is N2a, while one node is N1a and two or three nodes is N1b. N1c is tumour deposits without nodal metastasis.
  46. The correct answer is C (The circumferential resection margin is threatened, predicting local recurrence after total mesorectal excision): The circumferential (radial) resection margin is the mesorectal fascia surface, and tumour within 1 mm is regarded as threatened or involved, strongly predicting local recurrence and prompting neoadjuvant therapy. It does not by itself mean nodal, sacral or distant disease.
  47. The correct answer is D (The wide right colon allows bulky polypoid growth with occult bleeding, the narrow left favours obstruction): Right-sided cancers are bulky exophytic tumours in a capacious lumen that bleed slowly and cause anaemia, while left-sided lesions tend to be annular napkin-ring constrictions in a narrower lumen with formed stool, causing obstruction and altered bowel habit. A right-sided tumour therefore presents late with anaemia, and a left-sided tumour presents earlier with a change in bowel habit.
  48. The correct answer is C (Lower rectal veins drain through the inferior and middle rectal veins into the systemic circulation): The upper rectum drains via the superior rectal vein to the portal system and liver, while the lower rectum drains via middle and inferior rectal veins to the internal iliac veins and inferior vena cava, allowing lung metastases without liver involvement. The nodal routes explain regional spread, not haematogenous lung disease.
  49. The correct answer is B (Luminal obstruction by a faecolith or lymphoid hyperplasia with raised pressure and bacterial invasion): Acute appendicitis usually follows luminal obstruction by faecolith or lymphoid hyperplasia, increasing intraluminal pressure, impairing venous drainage and permitting bacterial invasion of the wall, with possible perforation. Mucinous neoplasm and Crohn disease are less common causes.
  50. The correct answer is A (Low-grade appendiceal mucinous neoplasm complicated by pseudomyxoma peritonei): Low-grade appendiceal mucinous neoplasm can rupture and seed the peritoneum, producing pseudomyxoma peritonei with gelatinous ascites and redistribution at the liver, spleen and omentum. Goblet cell carcinoma, NET and obstructive mucocele do not combine neoplastic mucinous epithelium with peritoneal mucin spread.
  51. The correct answer is C (A well-differentiated neuroendocrine tumour with very low metastatic risk at this size and site): Most appendiceal neuroendocrine tumours are incidental, small (under 1 cm) and located at the tip, with excellent prognosis, and risk of nodal spread rises with size above 2 cm. Chromogranin and synaptophysin expression confirms neuroendocrine differentiation, excluding goblet cell, mucinous and stromal tumours.
  52. The correct answer is B (Human papillomavirus infection, with spread to the superficial inguinal nodes): Anal squamous cell carcinoma is strongly linked to oncogenic HPV (especially type 16), with immunosuppression a risk factor. Below the dentate line lymph drains to the superficial inguinal nodes, while above it drains to the internal iliac and inferior mesenteric nodes.
  53. The correct answer is A (Collagenous colitis, with a subepithelial collagen band thicker than 10 micrometres): Microscopic colitis causes watery diarrhoea with a normal-appearing mucosa; the collagenous form shows a thickened subepithelial collagen band with lymphocytosis, and NSAID use is a recognised association. Ulcerative colitis, ischaemic and pseudomembranous colitis show structural or exudative changes.
  54. The correct answer is C (Fibrin, mucin and neutrophils erupting from damaged crypts in a volcano pattern): Toxin-producing Clostridioides difficile damages crypts, and the pseudomembrane is an exudate of fibrin, mucin and neutrophils spilling from crypts like a volcano, typically after antibiotic exposure. Fulminant disease can cause toxic megacolon, and the other options describe other colitides.
  55. The correct answer is B (Heaped, irregular margins with shouldered edges and abrupt fold termination): Malignant gastric ulcers have irregular, rolled or heaped edges, a necrotic uneven base and folds that stop abruptly at the margin, whereas benign ulcers are punched out with clean bases and converging folds. Erosive gastritis consists of shallow multiple lesions.
  56. The correct answer is B (Multiple endocrine neoplasia type 1, with parathyroid and pituitary tumours): Zollinger-Ellison syndrome arises from a gastrin-secreting neuroendocrine tumour, usually in the duodenum or pancreas, and about a quarter occur in MEN1 (MEN1 gene, with parathyroid hyperplasia and pituitary tumours). MEN2A, VHL and NF1 have different tumour spectra.
  57. The correct answer is D (Mantle cell lymphoma presenting as multiple lymphomatous polyposis): Multiple lymphomatous polyposis is the classic gastrointestinal presentation of mantle cell lymphoma, with cyclin D1 overexpression from t(11;14). Follicular, marginal zone and Burkitt lymphomas have other translocations and morphology.
  58. The correct answer is A (A gastric intestinal-type adenoma, a dysplastic precursor to adenocarcinoma): Gastric adenomas show dysplastic intestinal-type epithelium, arise on a background of atrophic gastritis and metaplasia, and carry significant risk of carcinoma, so complete removal and surveillance are advised. The other polyps are reactive, cystic or stromal.
  59. The correct answer is A (Transmural inflammation with a fissuring ulcer tracking through the wall into adjacent tissues): Crohn fissuring ulcers penetrate the full thickness of the bowel wall and form fistulas, including perianal ones that cross the sphincter, often complex and recurrent. Cryptoglandular disease explains most fistulas in patients without Crohn disease, while tuberculosis and ischaemia are not typical here.
  60. The correct answer is D (Proliferation of unpaired new arteries as native portal tracts and portal venous inflow are lost): As a dysplastic nodule progresses to hepatocellular carcinoma, portal tracts are lost and the lesion is supplied by unpaired arteries from neoangiogenesis. This gives arterial hyperenhancement, and the loss of portal venous inflow with early venous drainage gives washout. Arterioportal shunts cause transient wedge-shaped enhancement without washout. Kupffer cell uptake and sinusoidal congestion do not produce a nodule with this wash-in and washout pattern.
  61. The correct answer is A (Large eosinophilic polygonal hepatocytes with prominent nucleoli between parallel bands of lamellar collagen): Fibrolamellar carcinoma affects young adults without cirrhosis and has a normal alpha-fetoprotein. It is composed of oncocytic polygonal cells in a lamellar fibrous stroma, which may condense into a central scar that calcifies. Conventional HCC shows thick trabeculae with reticulin loss. Focal nodular hyperplasia has a similar scar but is benign and lacks atypia.
  62. The correct answer is D (Subtype with activating CTNNB1 mutation, associated with androgen exposure): Beta-catenin activated adenomas carry the highest risk of malignant transformation, and are associated with male sex, androgens and glycogen storage disease. Intralesional fat points to the HNF1A inactivated type, which is rarely malignant. Inflammatory adenomas are the commonest subtype and more often bleed. Size above 5 cm adds risk but does not define the molecular subtype.
  63. The correct answer is C (Polyclonal hyperplastic response of hepatocytes to a focal abnormality of arterial blood supply): Focal nodular hyperplasia is a polyclonal hyperplastic response to a vascular malformation, with a central scar containing a malformed artery. The scar is T2 hyperintense and enhances late, unlike the scar of fibrolamellar carcinoma, which is low on T2. Adenomas are monoclonal. Cirrhotic nodules need a background of diffuse injury.
  64. The correct answer is B (Dilated blood-filled vascular spaces lined by flat endothelium with slow internal flow): Cavernous haemangioma consists of dilated vascular channels lined by flat endothelium and separated by fibrous septa. Contrast enters the periphery and fills the slow-flow spaces gradually. Malignant lesions show washout rather than progressive fill-in. Rim enhancement from necrotic tumour does not progress centripetally to isoattenuation.
  65. The correct answer is C (Iron within periportal hepatocytes, with relative sparing of Kupffer cells and macrophages): Hereditary haemochromatosis from HFE mutation causes parenchymal iron overload, beginning in periportal hepatocytes. The reticuloendothelial system is initially spared, so the spleen keeps normal signal. Transfusional siderosis deposits iron in Kupffer cells and splenic macrophages, darkening the spleen. Congestion does not cause primary periportal iron loading.
  66. The correct answer is C (Impaired biliary excretion of copper with failure to incorporate copper into ceruloplasmin): Wilson disease results from ATP7B mutation, which impairs copper transport into bile and into ceruloplasmin. Copper accumulates in liver, basal ganglia and cornea (Descemet membrane). Menkes disease involves ATP7A and copper deficiency. HFE mutation causes iron overload with a different organ pattern.
  67. The correct answer is B (Stage of fibrosis, from perisinusoidal deposition through bridging septa to cirrhosis): Fibrosis stage is the histological feature that best predicts liver-related events and mortality in steatotic liver disease. Steatosis, ballooning and lobular inflammation contribute to activity grading, but they do not independently predict outcome as well. Steatosis may even regress as cirrhosis develops.
  68. The correct answer is C (Eosinophilic cytoplasmic aggregates of damaged cytokeratin filaments ringed by neutrophils): Mallory-Denk bodies are aggregates of cytokeratin intermediate filaments and ubiquitin within ballooned hepatocytes, and are typical of alcohol-associated hepatitis, with satellitosis by neutrophils. The AST to ALT ratio above 2 supports alcohol. Alpha-1 antitrypsin globules, ground-glass cells and Councilman bodies belong to other diseases.
  69. The correct answer is B (Stellate cells transformed into myofibroblast-like cells after cytokine activation): Stellate cells in the space of Disse store vitamin A normally and become collagen-secreting myofibroblasts after injury. Kupffer cells release TGF-beta that activates them, but they are not the main source of collagen. Endothelial cells and cholangiocytes contribute little to the bulk of scar.
  70. The correct answer is D (Its short hepatic veins drain directly into the inferior vena cava below the obstruction): In Budd-Chiari syndrome the main hepatic veins are occluded, producing centrilobular congestion in most of the liver. The caudate lobe drains through separate short veins directly into the inferior vena cava, so it hypertrophies. Its blood supply and sinusoidal structure are not different from the remaining liver.
  71. The correct answer is C (Periportal and paracholedochal collateral veins bypassing the occluded main portal vein): Portal vein thrombosis, here after umbilical catheterisation, leads to hepatopetal collateral veins around the portal vein and bile duct. This is called cavernous transformation. Prehepatic portal hypertension then produces splenomegaly and varices. Recanalisation and malformations do not produce this tangle.
  72. The correct answer is A (Integration of viral DNA into the host genome, with disruption of growth control genes): Hepatitis B can cause carcinoma without cirrhosis because viral DNA integrates into host chromosomes, and HBx protein deregulates growth control. The cirrhosis pathway through regenerative cycles is the main route in other aetiologies. Aflatoxin acts as a co-factor rather than the direct viral mechanism.
  73. The correct answer is C (Portal lymphoid aggregates with bile duct damage, together with macrovesicular steatosis): Chronic hepatitis C shows portal lymphoid follicles, often with mild bile duct injury, plus steatosis, which is common and most marked in genotype 3. Ground-glass cells and sanded nuclei indicate hepatitis B. Plasma cell rich interface hepatitis suggests autoimmune hepatitis, and granulomatous duct destruction suggests PBC.
  74. The correct answer is B (Liquefied hepatocellular debris with sparse inflammation, and trophozoites at the viable edge): Amoebic abscess is liquefactive necrosis of hepatocytes, producing anchovy-paste fluid that is largely acellular because trophozoites lyse neutrophils. Entamoeba histolytica trophozoites are found in the viable wall rather than the necrotic centre. Pyogenic abscess is neutrophilic, hydatid cysts have a laminated wall, and tuberculosis produces caseating granulomas.
  75. The correct answer is A (Thin nucleated germinal layer, the innermost parasite tissue producing protoscolices): Echinococcus granulosus cysts have an inner germinal layer (endocyst) that generates brood capsules and scolices. Outside it lies a laminated acellular layer, then a host-derived pericyst. The floating membrane is a detached endocyst. The pericyst and parenchymal reaction are host tissue.
  76. The correct answer is C (Stromal invasion with neoplastic hepatocytes extending into portal tracts): Stromal invasion is the most reliable feature separating early hepatocellular carcinoma from a high-grade dysplastic nodule. Small cell change, cell density and unpaired arteries occur in dysplastic nodules too. Focal reticulin thinning alone is not diagnostic.
  77. The correct answer is C (PAS-positive, diastase-resistant globules of misfolded protein retained in endoplasmic reticulum): Alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency (PiZZ) retains misfolded Z protein in hepatocyte endoplasmic reticulum, seen as PAS-positive globules that resist diastase. The same genotype causes basal panacinar emphysema through unopposed neutrophil elastase. Glycogen is diastase-sensitive, and haemosiderin and copper stain differently.
  78. The correct answer is A (Aberrant systemic venous drainage to this region, bypassing fat-promoting portal inflow): Focal sparing near the gallbladder fossa reflects aberrant venous inflow, such as cystic or parabiliary veins, that bypasses the portal blood driving fat deposition. The area is geographic, with normal vessels crossing and no mass effect. Tumours and infarcts would distort architecture or show enhancement abnormalities.
  79. The correct answer is B (Bile supersaturated with cholesterol, crystal nucleation, and gallbladder hypomotility permitting growth): Cholesterol stones form when bile is supersaturated with cholesterol relative to bile salts and phospholipids, crystals nucleate, and gallbladder hypomotility lets them grow. Female sex, parity, obesity and oestrogen are risk factors. Infection and bilirubin precipitation characterise pigment stones.
  80. The correct answer is B (Bacterial beta-glucuronidase releasing free bilirubin that precipitates as calcium salts): Brown pigment stones form in infected, stagnant ducts, often after biliary surgery. Bacterial enzymes deconjugate bilirubin glucuronide, and calcium bilirubinate precipitates. Black pigment stones arise in the gallbladder in haemolysis or cirrhosis. Cholesterol stones relate to supersaturation.
  81. The correct answer is C (Gas-forming anaerobes such as Clostridium perfringens, with Escherichia coli): Emphysematous cholecystitis is a gas-forming infection of the gallbladder wall, typically in diabetic men, and often arises from vascular compromise. Clostridium perfringens and enteric organisms such as Escherichia coli are usual. It has a high risk of gangrene and perforation, so it is treated urgently.
  82. The correct answer is A (Cholesterol crystals within Rokitansky-Aschoff sinuses in the thickened wall, as in adenomyomatosis): Adenomyomatosis causes mural thickening with hyperplastic epithelium and intramural diverticula called Rokitansky-Aschoff sinuses. Cholesterol crystals and debris in the sinuses produce comet-tail artefact. Gas would be dirty shadowing in an acutely ill patient, and porcelain gallbladder shows curvilinear wall calcification.
  83. The correct answer is A (Adenocarcinoma arising from dysplastic epithelium of biliary or intestinal type): Most gallbladder carcinomas are adenocarcinomas arising from dysplasia, and gallstones with chronic inflammation are the main risk. Spread is typically direct into liver segments IV and V. Squamous, neuroendocrine and sarcomatoid types are uncommon.
  84. The correct answer is B (Chronic liver fluke infestation causing biliary epithelial inflammation and dysplasia): Liver flukes such as Opisthorchis viverrini and Clonorchis sinensis cause chronic cholangitis, and are major risk factors for cholangiocarcinoma in endemic regions. Hepatitis B causes HCC chiefly. PSC with colitis and thorotrast are recognised but do not fit this exposure history.
  85. The correct answer is D (Concentric onion-skin fibrosis around medium and large bile ducts, with eventual duct obliteration): Primary sclerosing cholangitis causes concentric periductal fibrosis that obliterates ducts, producing the beaded appearance on cholangiography. It is associated with ulcerative colitis and carries a risk of cholangiocarcinoma. The florid duct lesion is PBC, and interface hepatitis suggests autoimmune hepatitis.
  86. The correct answer is B (Small interlobular and septal bile ducts, injured by lymphocytic cholangitis): Primary biliary cholangitis is an autoimmune injury to small interlobular and septal ducts, which explains the normal MRCP. PSC in contrast affects larger ducts and produces strictures on imaging. Ductal plate malformation is congenital.
  87. The correct answer is D (Anomalous pancreaticobiliary junction with pancreatic juice reflux and later carcinoma risk): Type I choledochal cysts are fusiform dilatations of the extrahepatic duct, and are linked to an anomalous pancreaticobiliary junction with a long common channel. Reflux of pancreatic enzymes causes epithelial injury, and carcinoma risk persists after drainage unless the cyst is excised. Caroli disease and ARPKD affect intrahepatic ducts.
  88. The correct answer is A (Portal vein branch and artery surrounded by the dilated duct lumen): Caroli disease is a ductal plate malformation of large intrahepatic ducts, producing saccular dilatations. The central dot sign arises because portal radicles are bridged by the dilated duct. Stones and sludge may occur, but they lack the enhancement of vessels.
  89. The correct answer is C (Pressure erosion of an inflamed gallbladder wall creating a fistula into the duodenum): Chronic cholecystitis can cause a large stone to erode through the wall into the adjacent duodenum, forming a fistula. The stone travels distally and obstructs at a narrow point such as the terminal ileum. Pneumobilia and an ectopic stone form the Rigler triad with obstruction.
  90. The correct answer is D (Lipase digestion of triglycerides to fatty acids that bind calcium as insoluble soaps): In acute pancreatitis, released lipases digest fat, and the liberated fatty acids bind calcium to form soaps, called saponification. This produces chalky white plaques and can lower serum calcium. Metastases, tuberculosis and infarction have different gross and microscopic appearances.
  91. The correct answer is D (Fibrous and granulation tissue forming a wall that lacks any epithelial lining): A pseudocyst is a collection of fluid enclosed by fibrous or granulation tissue, with no epithelium, and develops after four weeks. True cysts such as mucinous and serous neoplasms have an epithelial lining. Enzymes in the fluid are usually high.
  92. The correct answer is D (Walled-off necrosis, a mature encapsulated collection containing necrotic tissue): After four weeks, a necrotic collection with an enhancing wall is walled-off necrosis. A pseudocyst contains fluid only, and arises in interstitial oedematous pancreatitis. Acute necrotic collections lack a wall and occur earlier.
  93. The correct answer is B (Left-sided portal hypertension diverting splenic venous return through short gastric veins): Chronic pancreatitis can thrombose the adjacent splenic vein, so splenic return drains through short gastric veins into gastric fundal varices. This is sinistral or left-sided portal hypertension, with splenomegaly, and splenectomy can cure it. Oesophageal varices and cirrhosis are absent.
  94. The correct answer is B (Lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate with storiform fibrosis and obliterative phlebitis): Type 1 autoimmune pancreatitis is an IgG4-related disease with a lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate, storiform fibrosis and obliterative phlebitis. Type 2 shows granulocytic epithelial lesions and lacks IgG4 elevation. Calcific chronic pancreatitis and adenocarcinoma have different histology and response to steroids.
  95. The correct answer is B (Activating point mutation of KRAS, most often codon 12, found in early precursors): KRAS mutation is the commonest and earliest alteration in pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, found in over 90 per cent and already in low-grade PanIN. CDKN2A inactivation comes next, and TP53 and SMAD4 loss are later. BRCA2 germline mutations account for a small proportion.
  96. The correct answer is C (T4, because tumour involves a major visceral arterial trunk): Involvement of the coeliac axis, superior mesenteric artery or common hepatic artery defines T4 in pancreatic cancer, irrespective of size. T3 describes extension beyond the pancreas without major arterial involvement. Size-based categories T1 and T2 apply only to tumour confined to the pancreas.
  97. The correct answer is C (Intraductal papillary mucin-producing epithelium with duct dilatation, lacking ovarian-type stroma): Intraductal papillary mucinous neoplasm arises in the pancreatic ducts, communicates with the duct system and produces mucin, which may extrude from a patulous ampulla. It lacks ovarian-type stroma. Main duct involvement carries a higher risk of malignancy than branch duct disease.
  98. The correct answer is A (Mucinous columnar epithelium underlaid by densely cellular ovarian-type stroma): Mucinous cystic neoplasms occur almost exclusively in women, in the body or tail, and are defined by ovarian-type stroma beneath mucinous epithelium. They do not communicate with the duct system. Serous cystadenomas are microcystic, IPMNs communicate, and pseudocysts lack epithelium.
  99. The correct answer is B (Clear cuboidal cells rich in glycogen, forming a benign serous neoplasm): Serous cystadenomas are lined by glycogen-rich cuboidal cells, giving a honeycomb of microcysts and often a central scar with calcification. They are benign and are associated with von Hippel-Lindau disease. Mucinous lesions are lined by mucin-secreting columnar cells.
  100. The correct answer is C (CTNNB1 exon 3 mutation with nuclear accumulation of beta-catenin): Solid pseudopapillary neoplasm affects young women and is characterised by CTNNB1 mutation, with aberrant nuclear beta-catenin staining. It has low malignant potential. VHL alterations relate to serous lesions, KRAS to ductal cancer and MEN1 to neuroendocrine tumours.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Grade 2 well-differentiated tumour, with intermediate proliferation between 3 and 20 per cent): Pancreatic neuroendocrine tumours are graded by Ki-67 and mitotic count. Grade 1 is below 3 per cent, grade 2 is 3 to 20 per cent and grade 3 is above 20 per cent. Poorly differentiated carcinomas are a separate category. Insulinomas are hypervascular and enhance avidly in the arterial phase.
  2. The correct answer is D (Protein plugs in small ducts that calcify, obstructing flow and causing upstream acinar injury): In alcohol-related chronic pancreatitis, protein plugs form in small ducts and calcify into stones, obstructing outflow and causing acinar loss and fibrosis. Hyperparathyroidism is a recognised cause of chronic pancreatitis but is not suggested here. Fat necrosis calcification is extraductal.
  3. The correct answer is C (Perforated low-grade mucinous neoplasm of the appendix with mucin-producing epithelial cells): Pseudomyxoma peritonei results from rupture of a mucinous neoplasm, usually of the appendix, with redistribution of mucinous ascites. It scallops the liver and spleen because the mucin is viscous and does not infiltrate. Serous and mesothelial tumours do not produce this mucin.
  4. The correct answer is B (Epithelioid granulomas with central caseous necrosis and Langhans giant cells): Tuberculous peritonitis shows caseating granulomas with Langhans giant cells, and the exudative ascites is often of high density. Sarcoid granulomas are non-necrotising. Mesothelioma and pseudomyxoma have distinct tumour histology.
  5. The correct answer is B (Exudative ascites from peritoneal disease increasing capillary permeability and blocking lymphatics): A serum-ascites albumin gradient below 11 g/L with high protein indicates ascites not caused by portal hypertension, such as peritoneal carcinomatosis or tuberculosis. These produce an exudate through increased permeability and lymphatic blockage. Portal hypertensive causes give a gradient of 11 g/L or more.
  6. The correct answer is B (Translocation t(11;22)(p13;q12) creating an EWSR1-WT1 fusion transcription factor): Desmoplastic small round cell tumour affects young men and involves the peritoneum diffusely. It is defined by t(11;22)(p13;q12) producing EWSR1-WT1. Ewing sarcoma shares EWSR1 but fuses with FLI1. Alveolar rhabdomyosarcoma and synovial sarcoma have other fusions.
  7. The correct answer is D (Transcoelomic shedding of tumour cells that settle where peritoneal fluid pools): Peritoneal metastases arise by transcoelomic spread, with cells shed from a serosa-penetrating tumour carried by peritoneal fluid flow. They collect in the pouch of Douglas, the right paracolic gutter and the subphrenic space. Haematogenous and lymphatic routes do not explain this distribution.
  8. The correct answer is A (Gut bacterial translocation to mesenteric nodes with seeding of ascites low in opsonins): Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis occurs in cirrhosis through translocation of intestinal bacteria, and impaired immunity with low ascitic opsonic activity lets infection persist. It is monomicrobial, with an ascitic neutrophil count of at least 0.25 x 10^9/L. Perforation gives polymicrobial secondary peritonitis.
  9. The correct answer is A (Dominant mesangial deposits of IgA, usually with C3 and sometimes IgG or IgM): Haematuria within a day or two of a mucosal infection, with normal complement, is the typical synpharyngitic presentation of IgA nephropathy, in which IgA dominates in the mesangium. Subepithelial humps belong to post-streptococcal disease, which presents later with low C3. Linear IgG indicates anti-GBM disease, and full-house staining indicates lupus nephritis.
  10. The correct answer is C (Subepithelial immune deposits with basement membrane spikes on silver stain, often anti-PLA2R mediated): Uniform capillary wall thickening without proliferation in an adult with nephrotic syndrome points to membranous nephropathy. Immune complexes sit on the subepithelial side and the basement membrane grows between them as spikes, and most primary cases involve antibodies to the phospholipase A2 receptor. Minimal change shows normal light microscopy, diabetic disease needs nodules, and membranoproliferative disease shows hypercellular lobulated tufts.
  11. The correct answer is A (Discrete subepithelial humps, with endocapillary proliferation and neutrophils on light microscopy): Nephritic syndrome with low C3 after streptococcal skin infection is post-infectious glomerulonephritis. The immune complexes form characteristic subepithelial humps and the glomeruli are hypercellular from endocapillary proliferation with neutrophils. Mesangial deposits, Alport changes and isolated foot process fusion do not fit a self-limited, complement-consuming illness.
  12. The correct answer is D (Smooth linear IgG along the basement membrane, directed at the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen): Lung haemorrhage with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis in a young smoker suggests anti-GBM disease. Autoantibodies bind the non-collagenous domain of the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen shared by glomerular and alveolar basement membranes, giving smooth linear IgG. ANCA vasculitis is pauci-immune and the granular patterns belong to immune complex diseases.
  13. The correct answer is B (Ovoid acellular mesangial nodules of sclerosis at the centre of lobulated glomerular tufts): Long-standing diabetes with retinopathy and proteinuria produces diffuse glomerulosclerosis, and the nodular form is the most specific lesion, called the Kimmelstiel-Wilson nodule. Hyaline arteriolosclerosis of both afferent and efferent arterioles accompanies it. Amyloid can mimic nodules but stains with Congo red and is not the likely cause here, and wire loops indicate lupus.
  14. The correct answer is D (Subendothelial and mesangial deposits with full-house staining and tubuloreticular inclusions): Diffuse endocapillary proliferation in more than half of glomeruli defines class IV lupus nephritis. Subendothelial immune complexes deposit with all immunoglobulin classes and complement, giving full-house staining, and endothelial tubuloreticular inclusions are typical on electron microscopy. Membranous, anti-GBM and IgA patterns do not fit low complement with anti-dsDNA.
  15. The correct answer is D (PKD1 encoding polycystin-1 on chromosome 16, with autosomal dominant inheritance): Adult bilateral cystic kidneys with liver cysts and a parent on dialysis is autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease, caused by PKD1 in most families and PKD2 in the remainder. PKHD1 causes the recessive paediatric disease. VHL and TSC2 mutations produce cysts as part of syndromes that also have tumours.
  16. The correct answer is C (Radially arranged dilated collecting ducts in the kidney with congenital hepatic fibrosis): Potter sequence with symmetrically enlarged echogenic kidneys in a neonate indicates autosomal recessive polycystic kidney disease from PKHD1 mutation. Fusiform dilatation of collecting ducts radiates perpendicular to the cortex, and the liver shows ductal plate malformation with congenital hepatic fibrosis. Dysplastic cartilage is the hallmark of multicystic dysplasia, and the dominant form rarely presents in the neonatal period.
  17. The correct answer is D (Medial fibroplasia, with irregular fibrous thickening of the media alternating with thinning, sparing the intima): A young woman with renovascular hypertension and alternating narrowing and dilatation in the distal artery has fibromuscular dysplasia. The commonest histological type is medial fibroplasia, where thickened fibrous media alternate with thinned segments, producing the angiographic chain appearance. Atherosclerosis affects the origin in older patients, and arteritis shows inflammation.
  18. The correct answer is C (Fibrinoid necrosis of arterioles with onion-skin thickening of interlobular arteries): Severe accelerated hypertension with retinal and renal involvement is malignant hypertension. The small vessels show fibrinoid necrosis of arterioles and hyperplastic arteriolitis with onion-skinning of the interlobular arteries, and the surface may show petechial haemorrhages. Hyaline arteriolosclerosis occurs in slow benign disease with shrunken kidneys.
  19. The correct answer is A (Loss of VHL tumour suppressor function on chromosome 3p with stabilised hypoxia-inducible factor): Clear cell carcinoma is the commonest renal cancer and nearly all cases have inactivation of VHL on 3p, sporadic or from germline von Hippel-Lindau disease. Loss of VHL stabilises hypoxia-inducible factor and drives angiogenic growth factor expression, explaining the vascularity and renal vein extension. MET, fumarate hydratase and TFE3 alterations belong to other subtypes.
  20. The correct answer is D (Foamy macrophages within the papillary cores and frequent psammoma bodies in the stroma): Papillary carcinoma is typically hypovascular on contrast imaging and shows fibrovascular cores that contain foamy macrophages, with psammoma bodies often present. Chromophobe carcinoma has halos and raisinoid nuclei, oncocytoma has a central scar, and clear cell carcinoma has a rich capillary network that explains its avid enhancement.
  21. The correct answer is B (Oncocytoma, with nests of round eosinophilic cells containing abundant mitochondria and uniform round nuclei): A mahogany-brown, well-circumscribed homogeneous tumour with a central scar is a classic oncocytoma, composed of cells whose cytoplasm is packed with mitochondria. Chromophobe carcinoma is the main differential and has perinuclear clearing and irregular nuclei. Clear cell carcinoma is heterogeneous, and angiomyolipoma contains macroscopic fat.
  22. The correct answer is A (Rupture of thick-walled aneurysmal vessels lacking an elastic lamina within the tumour): Tuberous sclerosis commonly causes multiple bilateral angiomyolipomas, benign tumours of fat, smooth muscle and dysmorphic vessels. The vessels are thick walled and aneurysmal without a normal elastic lamina, so larger tumours can bleed catastrophically into the retroperitoneum, known as Wunderlich syndrome. Clear cell carcinoma rarely contains macroscopic fat.
  23. The correct answer is B (Class IV, since a convex solid component is present, and most such lesions are malignant): In Bosniak 2019 an enhancing nodule, defined as a convex protrusion of at least 4 mm with obtuse margins, upgrades a cyst to class IV. Class IV lesions are mostly malignant and generally managed surgically. Class III has thick or irregular enhancing walls or septa without a nodule, and IIF has smooth minimal thickening that needs follow-up.
  24. The correct answer is D (Multilocular cystic renal neoplasm of low malignant potential, with excellent prognosis after excision): A cystic tumour made up entirely of small cysts with clear cell lining and no expansile nodules is a multilocular cystic renal neoplasm of low malignant potential. Resection is curative in practically all cases. Mixed epithelial and stromal tumour needs ovarian-type stroma, and carcinoma requires expansile solid growth.
  25. The correct answer is C (Fat malabsorption binds luminal calcium, leaving free oxalate that is absorbed in the colon): Ileal disease causes fat malabsorption, and unabsorbed fatty acids bind calcium so that oxalate stays soluble and is absorbed across the colon, causing hyperoxaluria and calcium oxalate stones. Low urinary calcium argues against hypercalciuria. Uric acid stones are radiolucent and struvite requires alkaline urine.
  26. The correct answer is C (Magnesium ammonium phosphate, formed as urease-splitting Proteus bacteria alkalinise the urine): Alkaline urine with recurrent infection and a staghorn stone indicates struvite, also called infection stone. Urease from organisms such as Proteus splits urea to ammonia, raising the pH and precipitating magnesium ammonium phosphate. Uric acid forms in acidic urine, cystine in hereditary cystinuria, and oxalate is not infection related.
  27. The correct answer is A (Tubular atrophy and interstitial fibrosis from sustained back pressure and ischaemia): Prolonged outflow obstruction raises pressure in the collecting system, dilating calyces and compressing the parenchyma. Back pressure and ischaemia cause tubular atrophy with interstitial fibrosis and the cortex becomes thin. Acute tubular necrosis, papillary necrosis and cortical necrosis do not explain chronic bilateral thinning.
  28. The correct answer is B (Sheets of foamy macrophages with chronic inflammation, linked to obstruction and Proteus infection): A destroyed kidney with a staghorn stone and a bear-paw arrangement of low-density cavities is xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis. Chronic obstruction and infection, often with Proteus, lead to sheets of lipid-laden macrophages that can mimic renal cell carcinoma. Gas-forming infection is acute, and tuberculosis shows caseation.
  29. The correct answer is C (Aromatic amines such as 2-naphthylamine and benzidine, with tobacco smoke as an additive risk): Aromatic amines used in dye manufacture are classic occupational urothelial carcinogens and act together with cigarette smoking, the most common risk factor. Schistosomiasis causes squamous carcinoma, and cyclophosphamide and radiotherapy are recognised causes but are not suggested by the history.
  30. The correct answer is A (Squamous cell carcinoma, arising from metaplasia due to chronic Schistosoma haematobium infection): Chronic infection with Schistosoma haematobium causes calcified eggs in the bladder wall, repeated ulceration and squamous metaplasia, and squamous cell carcinoma develops in endemic areas. Urothelial carcinoma predominates in western countries. Urachal adenocarcinoma arises at the dome, not the trigone.
  31. The correct answer is D (Adenocarcinoma arising from urachal remnant tissue at the dome, often with a colloid pattern): A midline mass at the dome extending toward the umbilicus with calcification and mucus points to a urachal tumour, which is an adenocarcinoma with a colonic or mucinous pattern in most cases. The overlying mucosa is normal because the tumour arises extramucosally. Urothelial and squamous tumours arise from the lining.
  32. The correct answer is D (pT4a, because tumour has invaded an adjacent pelvic organ such as the prostate): Invasion of the prostatic stroma, seminal vesicles, uterus or vagina is T4a. Perivesical fat involvement is T3, and the muscularis propria alone is T2. T4b requires fixation to or invasion of the pelvic or abdominal wall, which is excluded in this patient.
  33. The correct answer is D (T1, with high risk of progression, so repeat resection and intravesical therapy are considered): Invasion of the lamina propria without muscularis propria involvement is T1. Because T1 high-grade tumours have a significant risk of progression, repeat resection and intravesical therapy are considered. Ta does not breach the basement membrane, Tis is flat disease, and T2 needs muscle invasion.
  34. The correct answer is B (Nodular hyperplasia of glands and stroma in the transition zone, driven by dihydrotestosterone): Benign prostatic hyperplasia arises in the transition zone, close to the urethra, which explains urinary obstruction. Both glandular and stromal elements proliferate in nodules under the influence of dihydrotestosterone formed by 5-alpha-reductase. Cancer typically arises in the peripheral zone.
  35. The correct answer is C (Grade group 3, corresponding to Gleason score 4+3=7 with a predominant pattern 4): Fused glands are Gleason pattern 4 and well-formed discrete glands are pattern 3. With pattern 4 predominant the score is 4+3=7, which is grade group 3, whereas 3+4=7 is group 2. Group 4 needs a score of 8, and group 1 is score 6 or less.
  36. The correct answer is D (Grade group 1, low risk of progression, so active surveillance is an accepted option): Well-formed, discrete glands are Gleason pattern 3 only, giving Gleason score 3+3=6, which is grade group 1. Such tumours have a very low risk of metastasis, so active surveillance is appropriate in selected patients. Higher groups require pattern 4 or 5 components that are absent here.
  37. The correct answer is B (Absent basal cell markers such as p63 and high-molecular-weight cytokeratin, with positive AMACR): Prostate adenocarcinoma glands lack the outer basal cell layer, so p63 and high-molecular-weight cytokeratin are negative, and AMACR is usually positive. Benign glands retain basal cells. Neuroendocrine markers and CK7 with GATA3 suggest different tumours.
  38. The correct answer is A (T3b, since tumour has invaded the seminal vesicle beyond the gland): Extension beyond the capsule is T3, and seminal vesicle invasion defines T3b. T3a is extraprostatic extension without seminal vesicle involvement. T2 is organ confined, and T4 requires fixation or invasion of structures such as the rectum, levator muscles or pelvic wall.
  39. The correct answer is B (Osteoblastic metastases seeded via the valveless vertebral venous plexus connecting with pelvic veins): Prostate cancer characteristically forms sclerotic osteoblastic metastases in the axial skeleton. Tumour cells travel retrograde through the valveless Batson vertebral venous plexus, which connects with the prostatic and pelvic veins. Osteoblastic reaction is driven by tumour factors that stimulate new bone formation.
  40. The correct answer is D (Sheets of uniform polygonal clear cells divided by fibrous septa infiltrated by lymphocytes): A uniform, cream, fleshy tumour with normal alpha-fetoprotein in a man in the third to fourth decade fits seminoma. Histology shows nests and sheets of large uniform cells with clear cytoplasm separated by fibrous septa containing lymphocytes. Necrosis and haemorrhage suggest embryonal carcinoma, and glomeruloid structures indicate yolk sac tumour.
  41. The correct answer is D (Perivascular structures with a central capillary surrounded by tumour cells within a cystic space): A raised alpha-fetoprotein in an infant with a testicular mass is yolk sac tumour, the commonest prepubertal germ cell tumour. It produces glomerulus-like Schiller-Duval bodies where a central vessel is draped by tumour cells in a space. Seminoma does not raise alpha-fetoprotein and choriocarcinoma raises beta-hCG.
  42. The correct answer is C (Choriocarcinoma, which invades vessels early and metastasises haematogenously rather than by lymphatics): A small primary with haemorrhage, raised beta-hCG, gynaecomastia and pulmonary nodules is choriocarcinoma. It contains syncytiotrophoblast and cytotrophoblast that invade blood vessels and spread early through the bloodstream to lung, liver and brain. Seminoma and embryonal carcinoma spread mainly through lymphatics.
  43. The correct answer is C (Left para-aortic nodes at the level of the left renal hilum): The testis develops near the kidney and its lymphatics follow the gonadal vessels to the retroperitoneum. A left tumour drains first to left para-aortic nodes near the renal hilum, while a right tumour drains to interaortocaval nodes. Inguinal nodes are involved only if the scrotal skin is invaded.
  44. The correct answer is D (Germ cell neoplasia in situ, the precursor of most post-pubertal germ cell tumours): Atypical OCT3/4-positive germ cells along the basement membrane of tubules are germ cell neoplasia in situ. It precedes most post-pubertal seminomas and non-seminomatous tumours, and cryptorchidism and infertility are risk factors. Spermatocytic tumour arises independently and Leydig and Sertoli changes do not contain atypical germ cells.
  45. The correct answer is A (Mature teratoma elements resistant to chemotherapy, with potential for later malignant transformation): A growing, cystic residual mass with normal markers after chemotherapy for a mixed tumour is the growing teratoma pattern. Teratoma components are not chemosensitive, they enlarge by cystic change, and they can transform into somatic malignancy, so resection is advised. Necrosis and fibrosis usually shrink.
  46. The correct answer is D (HPV-independent carcinoma arising from chronic inflammation such as lichen sclerosus): Keratinising, well-differentiated penile squamous carcinoma in an uncircumcised man with phimosis and a white atrophic glans arises on lichen sclerosus and chronic inflammation, independent of HPV. Basaloid and warty tumours are HPV related. Paget disease is an intraepidermal adenocarcinoma.
  47. The correct answer is D (Stepwise spread to superficial inguinal, then deep inguinal, and then pelvic nodes): Penile lymphatics drain to the superficial inguinal nodes, then deep inguinal nodes, and then to the pelvic nodes. Involvement of the inguinal nodes is the most important prognostic factor. Para-aortic drainage is the testicular route, and distant blood-borne spread appears late.
  48. The correct answer is D (Monomorphic sheets of chromophobe cells with scant cytoplasm, positive for prolactin on immunohistochemistry): Hyperprolactinaemia with a large sellar mass is a macroprolactinoma, which is a monomorphic chromophobe or weakly acidophilic adenoma with prolactin staining. Pleomorphism and mitoses suggest carcinoma, which is very rare and defined by metastasis. Densely granulated acidophil cells with growth hormone describe the acromegaly adenoma. Lymphocytic infiltration describes hypophysitis, which does not form a hormone secreting mass.
  49. The correct answer is A (Haemorrhagic infarction within a pre-existing pituitary adenoma causing rapid expansion and chiasmal compression): Sudden headache, visual field loss and blood products within a sellar mass indicate haemorrhage or infarction of a pre-existing adenoma, usually previously unrecognised. An aneurysm would produce subarachnoid blood and cranial nerve palsy rather than a fluid level within a sellar mass. Rathke cleft cysts rarely haemorrhage acutely. Sheehan syndrome follows obstetric haemorrhage in a physiologically enlarged gland and does not produce chiasmal compression.
  50.
