The correct answer is D (Adamantinomatous craniopharyngioma with wet keratin, palisaded epithelium, stellate reticulum and CTNNB1 mutation): Children with a calcified cystic suprasellar mass most often have adamantinomatous craniopharyngioma, with wet keratin, palisading epithelium and CTNNB1 beta catenin mutation. The papillary subtype occurs in adults, is rarely calcified and carries BRAF V600E. A Rathke cleft cyst lacks the solid calcified component. Germinoma is solid and typically presents with diabetes insipidus.
  51. The correct answer is A (Concentric biventricular hypertrophy that may progress to dilated cardiomyopathy with impaired contractility): Growth hormone and IGF-1 excess cause myocardial hypertrophy with interstitial fibrosis, leading to biventricular concentric hypertrophy and eventually dilated cardiomyopathy. Pericardial constriction is not a feature. Right sided valve plaques belong to carcinoid syndrome. Asymmetric septal hypertrophy with systolic anterior motion describes hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, which is sarcomeric.
  52. The correct answer is B (Primary thyroid lymphoma, usually extranodal marginal zone or diffuse large B cell type): Hashimoto thyroiditis carries a markedly increased risk of primary thyroid lymphoma, which should be suspected when a long standing goitre enlarges rapidly. Medullary carcinoma is not associated with autoimmune thyroid disease. Follicular carcinoma is not specifically increased and would present as a nodule. Riedel thyroiditis is fibrotic, produces a hard fixed gland and does not enlarge over months with echogenic septations.
  53. The correct answer is A (Granulomas with multinucleate giant cells around disrupted follicles and a mixed inflammatory infiltrate): A painful gland with raised ESR and suppressed uptake after a viral illness is de Quervain thyroiditis, with colloid granulomas and giant cells. Lymphoid follicles with Hurthle cells are Hashimoto thyroiditis, which is painless. Hyperplasia with scalloped colloid is Graves disease, which has high uptake. Dense fibrosis with muscle invasion is Riedel thyroiditis.
  54. The correct answer is D (Autoantibodies stimulating the TSH receptor, causing follicular hyperplasia and excess hormone): Graves disease results from IgG antibodies that stimulate the TSH receptor, producing diffuse hyperplasia, uniform uptake and extrathyroid features such as ophthalmopathy and pretibial myxoedema. Cytotoxic destruction describes Hashimoto thyroiditis. A toxic nodule gives focal uptake with suppression of the rest of the gland. A TSH secreting adenoma is rare and does not cause ophthalmopathy.
  55. The correct answer is A (Optically clear overlapping nuclei with grooves and intranuclear cytoplasmic pseudoinclusions): Cystic nodal metastases with thyroglobulin and microcalcifications indicate papillary thyroid carcinoma, whose diagnostic nuclei are clear, crowded and grooved with pseudoinclusions. Salt and pepper chromatin with amyloid describes medullary carcinoma, which is calcitonin rich. Hurthle features suggest an oncocytic neoplasm. Bizarre nuclei with necrosis suggest anaplastic carcinoma in an elderly patient.
  56. The correct answer is A (Tumour cells penetrating the full thickness of the capsule or invading capsular vessels): Follicular carcinoma cannot be diagnosed on cytology and requires capsular or vascular invasion on histology, and its haematogenous spread explains the bone metastasis. Papillary nuclear features and psammoma bodies would have been described. Amyloid with calcitonin positivity indicates medullary carcinoma. Lymphocytic infiltrate with oncocytic change describes Hashimoto thyroiditis and does not explain metastasis.
  57. The correct answer is A (Activating point mutation of the RET proto-oncogene, inherited in an autosomal dominant pattern): A calcitonin secreting tumour in the upper thyroid lobes, where C cells concentrate, with a family history indicates hereditary medullary carcinoma from activating RET mutations (MEN2). MEN1 involves parathyroid, pituitary and pancreatic tumours. APC mutation causes a cribriform morular papillary variant, not a calcitonin tumour. BRAF V600E is a somatic papillary carcinoma driver.
  58. The correct answer is C (Anaplastic carcinoma composed of pleomorphic spindle and giant cells with high mitotic activity): An elderly patient with a longstanding goitre and weeks of aggressive airway invasion has anaplastic carcinoma, composed of pleomorphic spindle, giant and squamoid cells. Insular carcinoma grows more slowly and retains thyroglobulin. Medullary carcinoma is calcitonin secreting and less often rapidly invasive. Lymphoma usually arises in Hashimoto thyroiditis.
  59. The correct answer is D (Cycles of follicular hyperplasia and involution from impaired hormone synthesis, often with iodine deficiency): Multinodular goitre arises from impaired thyroid hormone synthesis, raising TSH and driving cycles of hyperplasia and involution, giving nodules with haemorrhage, cysts and calcification. The nodules are polyclonal, not monoclonal adenomas. Stimulating antibodies cause Graves disease with hyperthyroidism. Lymphocytic destruction describes Hashimoto thyroiditis.
  60. The correct answer is B (A single adenoma composed of chief cells with a compressed rim of normal parathyroid tissue): Primary hyperparathyroidism with stones, bones and abdominal moans is caused by a solitary adenoma in about 85 percent of cases, with a rim of suppressed normal tissue. Multigland hyperplasia accounts for fewer cases. Carcinoma is rare and causes severe hypercalcaemia. Secondary hyperparathyroidism produces appropriately raised PTH with low or normal calcium.
  61. The correct answer is D (Fibrous stroma with haemosiderin-laden macrophages and clusters of osteoclast-like giant cells): Brown tumours are osteoclastic reparative lesions from PTH driven resorption with haemorrhage, giving fibrous stroma, haemosiderin and osteoclast-like giant cells. Giant cell tumour of bone looks similar but has a neoplastic stroma with H3F3A mutation, and the clinical hyperparathyroidism points away from it. Fibrous dysplasia shows woven bone. Langerhans cell histiocytosis shows CD1a positive cells with eosinophils.
  62. The correct answer is B (Diffuse chief cell hyperplasia of all glands driven by hypocalcaemia, phosphate retention and low calcitriol): The rugger jersey spine of renal osteodystrophy reflects secondary hyperparathyroidism, in which all four glands undergo hyperplasia in response to low calcitriol, phosphate retention and low calcium. A solitary adenoma with gland suppression is primary disease. Atrophy would occur with hypercalcaemia. Carcinoma is rare and not driven by renal failure.
  63. The correct answer is B (Golden-yellow solitary encapsulated nodule of lipid-rich clear cells resembling zona fasciculata cells): Unilateral primary aldosteronism is caused by a small aldosterone producing adenoma that is golden yellow because of lipid rich clear cells. The black adenoma is associated with cortisol excess and atrophic adjacent cortex. A 1.5 cm lesion without invasion is not carcinoma. Bilateral hyperplasia is the other cause of primary aldosteronism but does not lateralise.
  64. The correct answer is B (Cortical atrophy with narrowed zona fasciculata and reticularis from prolonged pituitary suppression): An autonomous cortisol secreting adenoma suppresses pituitary corticotrophs, so the normal contralateral cortex atrophies, which matters because post-operative adrenal insufficiency is expected. Nodular hyperplasia needs high ACTH. Zona glomerulosa hyperplasia belongs to renin driven aldosterone excess. Primary pigmented nodular disease is bilateral and not explained by a single large mass.
  65. The correct answer is A (Multiple Weiss criteria including atypical mitoses, diffuse architecture, sinusoidal invasion and high nuclear grade): Malignancy in adrenocortical tumours is judged histologically with systems such as the Weiss score, which counts atypical mitoses, high nuclear grade, diffuse architecture and invasion. Lipid rich cells in a capsule favour an adenoma. Size and weight raise suspicion but do not by themselves establish malignancy. Mixed hormone excess is suggestive but not specific.
  66. The correct answer is C (21-hydroxylase deficiency, impairing cortisol and aldosterone synthesis and shunting precursors toward androgen production): Virilisation of a 46,XX infant with salt wasting is classic salt wasting 21-hydroxylase deficiency, the commonest cause of congenital adrenal hyperplasia, with ACTH driven bilateral adrenal enlargement. 11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency causes hypertension and hypokalaemia. 3-beta-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase deficiency gives only mild virilisation of girls. 17-alpha-hydroxylase deficiency gives hypertension and absent virilisation.
  67. The correct answer is C (Caseating granulomatous destruction of the adrenal cortex and medulla by Mycobacterium tuberculosis): Chronic adrenal insufficiency with enlarged calcified glands in an endemic setting indicates tuberculous adrenalitis with caseating granulomas. Autoimmune adrenalitis is the commonest cause in the West but the glands are small and uncalcified. Haemorrhage presents acutely with high attenuation. Metastases can cause insufficiency, but calcification and the clinical context favour tuberculosis.
  68. The correct answer is D (Neisseria meningitidis septicaemia causing disseminated intravascular coagulation and adrenal haemorrhage): Fulminant meningococcal septicaemia with purpura and bilateral adrenal haemorrhage defines Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome, with DIC causing haemorrhagic necrosis of the cortex. Staphylococcus and pseudomonas can cause shock but are not the classic cause. Streptococcal toxic shock is not classically linked to adrenal haemorrhage. Imaging shows enlarged high attenuation glands.
  69. The correct answer is C (Nests of chromaffin cells in a zellballen pattern surrounded by sustentacular cells): Raised metanephrines with episodic hypertension indicate a phaeochromocytoma, composed of chromaffin cell nests (zellballen) with S100 positive sustentacular cells. Lipid rich cord-like cells are cortical. Homer-Wright rosettes belong to neuroblastoma in children. Amyloid with salt and pepper chromatin describes medullary thyroid carcinoma. Necrosis alone does not define malignancy.
  70. The correct answer is D (SDHB mutation, associated with succinate dehydrogenase loss and a high risk of metastatic disease): Extra-adrenal paragangliomas with metastases in a young patient strongly suggest an SDHB germline mutation, which carries the highest malignant potential among the hereditary forms. Malignancy is defined by metastasis to sites without chromaffin tissue, such as bone. NF1 tumours are usually benign adrenal tumours. VHL and RET tumours are usually benign bilateral adrenal tumours.
  71. The correct answer is D (Mature adipose tissue admixed with haematopoietic elements including megakaryocytes and erythroid precursors): Macroscopic fat within an adrenal mass is diagnostic of myelolipoma, a benign tumour of mature fat with bone marrow elements. Adenomas contain microscopic intracellular lipid and do not show fat attenuation of minus 90 HU. Liposarcoma is exceedingly rare in the adrenal. Angiomyolipoma has smooth muscle and vessels and arises in the kidney.
  72. The correct answer is D (Abundant intracytoplasmic cholesterol-derived lipid within clear vacuolated cells of a benign cortical adenoma): Attenuation below 10 HU and rapid washout characterise a lipid rich cortical adenoma, whose clear cells store cholesterol esters used for steroidogenesis. Haemosiderin would increase rather than decrease attenuation. Phaeochromocytoma has chromaffin granules, usually higher attenuation and slow washout. Lipofuscin rich cells are compact and lipid poor.
  73. The correct answer is C (Solitary small well-differentiated neuroendocrine tumour that is usually non-metastasising and cured by enucleation): Whipple triad with high insulin and C-peptide and an arterial hyperenhancing nodule indicates an insulinoma, which is typically solitary, small, well differentiated and usually benign, so enucleation is curative. Nesidioblastosis is diffuse and usually paediatric or post bariatric. A poorly differentiated carcinoma would be larger with a high Ki-67. Gastrinomas are more often malignant.
  74. The correct answer is C (The gastrinoma triangle formed by the cystic duct, pancreatic neck and third part of duodenum): Zollinger-Ellison syndrome tumours cluster in the gastrinoma triangle, with the duodenum the commonest site and the pancreatic head next. The pancreatic tail is not the typical site. The terminal ileum is the commonest site for serotonin secreting midgut NETs. Antral G cell hyperplasia causes hypergastrinaemia but not a discrete somatostatin receptor avid mass.
  75. The correct answer is D (Grade 2 well-differentiated neuroendocrine tumour, defined by Ki-67 between 3 and 20 percent): WHO grading of well-differentiated NETs uses mitoses and Ki-67: grade 1 is below 2 mitoses per 2 mm² and Ki-67 under 3 percent, grade 2 is 2 to 20 mitoses or Ki-67 3 to 20 percent, and grade 3 is above these. Both parameters here place the tumour in grade 2. Poorly differentiated carcinoma is a separate category defined by morphology.
  76. The correct answer is D (Glucagon secreted by pancreatic alpha cells, causing hyperglycaemia through increased hepatic gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis): Necrolytic migratory erythema with diabetes and weight loss indicates a glucagonoma, typically large, in the pancreatic tail and often metastatic at diagnosis. Somatostatinoma causes the diabetes, gallstones and steatorrhoea triad without a rash. VIPoma gives watery diarrhoea. Pancreatic polypeptide tumours do not produce this skin syndrome.
  77. The correct answer is C (Multiglandular asymmetric hyperplasia or adenomas due to loss of menin, with high recurrence after limited surgery): The three Ps of MEN1 are parathyroid, pituitary and pancreatic tumours caused by MEN1 menin loss. Parathyroid disease is multiglandular, so subtotal parathyroidectomy is usually planned and recurrence is common. A solitary adenoma is the sporadic pattern. Parathyroid carcinoma is linked to CDC73 in hyperparathyroidism-jaw tumour syndrome. Secondary hyperplasia requires renal failure.
  78. The correct answer is B (MEN2B with early aggressive medullary thyroid carcinoma and phaeochromocytoma but no hyperparathyroidism): Mucosal neuromas, marfanoid body habitus and ganglioneuromatosis with medullary carcinoma and phaeochromocytoma define MEN2B (RET M918T), where parathyroid disease is absent and the thyroid cancer is early and aggressive. MEN2A includes hyperparathyroidism. MEN1 involves different glands. VHL has haemangioblastoma and renal cell carcinoma without neuromas.
  79. The correct answer is D (Hyaline thickening of afferent and efferent arterioles with diffusely thickened glomerular basement membranes): Kimmelstiel-Wilson nodules sit alongside diffuse glomerular basement membrane thickening and hyaline arteriolosclerosis of both afferent and efferent arterioles, a pattern more specific for diabetes than hypertension alone. Subepithelial humps with spikes are membranous nephropathy. Crescents indicate rapidly progressive disease. Congo red positive fibrils define amyloidosis, a nodular mimic.
  80. The correct answer is B (Escherichia coli, fermenting glucose to produce carbon dioxide within necrotic parenchyma of the diabetic kidney): Gas in the renal parenchyma in a diabetic patient is emphysematous pyelonephritis, mostly due to Escherichia coli, whose fermentation of glucose in poorly perfused tissue generates gas. Klebsiella is the second most common cause. Proteus is linked to struvite stones and ammoniacal urine. Candida produces fungus balls and is uncommon.
  81. The correct answer is C (Neuropathy with autonomic dysfunction causing repetitive microtrauma, hyperaemia and osteoclast-mediated bone resorption): A painless warm swollen midfoot with fragmentation and dislocation in long standing diabetes is Charcot neuroarthropathy, driven by sensory and autonomic neuropathy with repeated unrecognised trauma and increased blood flow that activates osteoclasts. Septic arthritis is painful and febrile. Avascular necrosis involves ischaemia, not hyperaemia. Gout is acutely painful.
  82. The correct answer is C (Contiguous spread of polymicrobial infection through the skin defect into cortex and marrow): In the diabetic foot, osteomyelitis nearly always arises by contiguous spread from a chronic ulcer, usually polymicrobial, and a probe-to-bone test with a sinus tract supports it. Haematogenous seeding of metaphyses is typical of children. Reactive oedema from neuropathic stress lacks a sinus tract. Sterile ischaemic necrosis would not produce a draining sinus.
  83. The correct answer is B (Insulitis with CD8 positive T cell infiltration and selective loss of beta cells): Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune disease with CD8 T cell insulitis that selectively destroys beta cells. Islet amyloid and compensatory hyperplasia are changes of type 2 diabetes. Autoimmune pancreatitis shows IgG4 rich lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate of ducts and acini and does not selectively remove beta cells.
  84. The correct answer is C (Serotonin-induced desmoplastic fibrosis around nodal metastases from an ileal well-differentiated neuroendocrine tumour): Flushing, raised 5-HIAA and liver metastases indicate a midgut neuroendocrine tumour with carcinoid syndrome. The calcified mesenteric mass is nodal disease surrounded by intense desmoplastic fibrosis driven by serotonin and growth factors, tethering bowel. IgG4 disease and sclerosing mesenteritis do not explain the hormone excess. Desmoid tumours lack calcification and hormone secretion.
  85. The correct answer is A (CD1a and langerin positive cells with grooved nuclei and Birbeck granules on electron microscopy): Diabetes insipidus, exophthalmos and lytic skull lesions form the Hand-Schuller-Christian triad of Langerhans cell histiocytosis, with CD1a and langerin positive cells and Birbeck granules. Germinoma also thickens the stalk but expresses PLAP and OCT4. Rosai-Dorfman disease shows S100 positive emperipolesis without CD1a. Adenomas rarely cause diabetes insipidus.
  86. The correct answer is D (Near universal TP53 mutation, with BRCA pathway defects in a minority): Bilateral solid-cystic masses with omental disease and marked atypia indicate high-grade serous carcinoma, which carries TP53 mutation in nearly every case and homologous recombination defects including BRCA. KRAS and BRAF belong to low-grade serous tumours with bland nuclei. ARID1A and PIK3CA changes mark clear cell and endometrioid carcinomas. FOXL2 is the signature of adult granulosa cell tumour.
  87. The correct answer is A (High-grade serous carcinoma of the ovary, fallopian tube or peritoneum): Serous tubal intraepithelial carcinoma in the fimbria, with abnormal p53 and high proliferation, is the recognised precursor of most high-grade serous carcinomas. Low-grade serous carcinoma follows a borderline tumour sequence with wild-type p53. Endometrioid carcinoma arises from endometriosis. Mucinous carcinoma develops from mucinous cystadenoma and borderline tumour, not from tubal epithelium.
  88. The correct answer is D (Activating KRAS or BRAF mutation, with wild-type TP53 in most cases): Low-grade serous carcinoma evolves stepwise from serous borderline tumour, shows bland nuclei and psammoma bodies, and carries MAPK pathway mutations such as KRAS or BRAF with wild-type TP53. Chromosomal instability and TP53 mutation characterise high-grade serous carcinoma. Mismatch repair loss typifies endometrioid tumours in Lynch syndrome. ARID1A with PIK3CA marks endometriosis-related carcinomas.
  89. The correct answer is A (ARID1A loss with PIK3CA mutation, reflecting origin from endometriosis): Clear cell carcinoma of the ovary arises from endometriosis, is associated with thromboembolism, and typically shows ARID1A and PIK3CA alterations. TP53 and BRCA changes belong to high-grade serous carcinoma. KRAS with a borderline precursor describes low-grade serous or mucinous disease. CTNNB1 mutation with squamous morules fits endometrioid carcinoma, the closest confusable sibling, which lacks clear hobnail cells.
  90. The correct answer is C (Rokitansky protuberance, a mass of mixed ectodermal derivatives including hair follicles and teeth): A mature cystic teratoma contains skin appendages, sebaceous fat and sometimes teeth, concentrated in a Rokitansky protuberance that casts shadows and underlies the fat-fluid level. Borderline tumour papillae lack fat or teeth. Mural nodules in mucinous cysts are rare and sarcoma-like. A corpus luteum cyst has a clot but no fat or hair.
  91. The correct answer is C (Dysgerminoma, a malignant germ cell tumour and the ovarian counterpart of testicular seminoma): Sheets of uniform clear cells, fibrous septa with lymphocytes, raised LDH and a normal alpha-fetoprotein in an adolescent indicate dysgerminoma, histologically identical to seminoma and sensitive to chemotherapy and radiotherapy. Yolk sac tumour secretes alpha-fetoprotein. Granulosa cell tumour occurs in older women with Call-Exner bodies. Immature teratoma shows neuroepithelial rosettes and mixed tissues.
  92. The correct answer is D (Tumour oestrogen secretion causing endometrial hyperplasia and increased endometrial carcinoma risk): Adult granulosa cell tumour is a sex cord stromal tumour that secretes oestrogen and inhibin, so unopposed oestrogen produces endometrial hyperplasia and raises the risk of endometrial carcinoma. Androgen secretion is typical of Sertoli-Leydig tumours and gives virilisation. hCG would cause decidual change in pregnancy-related states. Progesterone effect would thin or transform, not thicken.
  93. The correct answer is A (Ovarian fibroma, a benign sex cord stromal tumour composed of spindle cells): Ascites and a pleural effusion that resolve on tumour removal define Meigs syndrome, classically caused by ovarian fibroma, whose collagen-rich spindle cells give low T2 signal. Brenner tumour is also T2-dark but seldom causes Meigs syndrome. Sertoli-Leydig tumours produce androgens. Krukenberg tumours are malignant, bilateral and produce positive cytology.
  94. The correct answer is C (Lymphatic or haematogenous spread of a gastrointestinal adenocarcinoma, most often gastric): Bilateral solid masses with signet ring cells in cellular stroma are the typical picture of Krukenberg tumour, a metastasis from gastric or other gastrointestinal adenocarcinoma. Primary mucinous carcinoma is usually unilateral, large and multiloculated. Fibroma does not transform into mucin-rich sarcoma. Stromal hyperplasia is a hormonal change without signet ring cells.
  95. The correct answer is B (Insulin resistance with raised LH pulsatility driving excess theca cell androgen production): Polycystic ovary syndrome features multiple small follicles and stromal hyperplasia, with insulin resistance and increased LH pulses stimulating theca cell androgen synthesis. A Sertoli-Leydig tumour would be unilateral and solid, with rapid virilisation. 21-hydroxylase deficiency is an adrenal cause that can mimic the picture. Hyperprolactinaemia suppresses ovulation without producing hirsutism.
  96. The correct answer is D (Chronic haemorrhagic fluid rich in methaemoglobin, with haemosiderin-laden macrophages in the cyst wall): T1 hyperintensity that persists on fat saturation, with T2 shading and no vascularity, reflects repeated haemorrhage into an endometrioma, containing methaemoglobin and haemosiderin-laden macrophages. Teratoma fat loses signal with fat suppression. Mucinous cystadenoma has multiple locules of different signal. Serous neoplasms with papillary projections enhance.
  97. The correct answer is C (Fibromuscular proliferation around endometrial glands and stroma, forming dense scar-like tissue): Deep infiltrating endometriosis of the rectosigmoid contains small endometrial glands and stroma surrounded by exuberant fibromuscular hyperplasia, giving T2-dark nodular thickening, adhesions and T1 haemorrhagic foci. Colorectal adenocarcinoma would show mucosal-based growth. Crohn disease would not cause cyclical symptoms. Diverticulitis produces oedema and abscess, which are T2-bright.
  98. The correct answer is B (Venous and lymphatic obstruction first, causing oedema and haemorrhage before arterial flow stops): The thin-walled veins and lymphatics are compressed before the thick-walled arteries, so the ovary becomes swollen and oedematous with peripheral follicles, and preserved Doppler arterial flow does not exclude torsion. Early arterial occlusion would give a small infarcted ovary. Haemorrhage is secondary. A tubo-ovarian abscess would show a thick enhancing wall and fever.
  99. The correct answer is A (Score 5, high risk of malignancy, with serous carcinoma the likeliest underlying epithelial neoplasm): A cystic lesion with a solid component showing at least four papillary projections, with flow and ascites, is O-RADS US score 5, carrying a risk of malignancy of about 50 percent or more, and serous carcinoma is the usual diagnosis. Score 3 and 2 imply low or negligible risk. A teratoma would be characterised by fat, not flow within papillae.
  100. The correct answer is D (Proliferation and atypia beyond a cystadenoma, without destructive stromal invasion): Borderline serous tumours show proliferation and atypia beyond a benign cystadenoma without destructive stromal invasion. Noninvasive implants do not change the category, though they affect prognosis. Psammoma bodies can occur in benign, borderline and malignant serous tumours. Laterality does not define category.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Stage IIIB, because extrapelvic peritoneal deposits measure 2 cm or less): Macroscopic peritoneal metastasis beyond the pelvis measuring 2 cm or less is stage IIIB, with or without positive retroperitoneal nodes. Stage IIIA1 requires nodal disease without such deposits. Stage IIIC requires deposits greater than 2 cm, or capsular involvement of liver or spleen, and nodal size does not set the stage. Stage II is confined to the pelvis.
  2. The correct answer is C (Ampullary segment of the fallopian tube, the widest and longest part of the tube): Most tubal ectopic pregnancies implant in the ampulla, where fertilised ova normally reside, and often present later than isthmic ones. Isthmic pregnancies rupture early. Interstitial pregnancies are uncommon but carry high haemorrhage risk. Fimbrial implantation is rare. Chronic salpingitis with mucosal scarring is the main predisposing pathology.
  3. The correct answer is D (Distal tubal occlusion after chronic salpingitis with retained serous fluid): A tubular fluid-filled structure with incomplete folds and a thin wall is hydrosalpinx, the end result of chronic salpingitis with fimbrial scarring and sealing of the distal tube. A tubo-ovarian abscess has a thick enhancing wall and acute features. A paratubal cyst is separate from the tube and lacks folds. Tubal carcinoma would contain enhancing solid tissue.
  4. The correct answer is D (Mature teratoma in which thyroid tissue forms the dominant component and secretes hormone): Thyroid tissue making up most of a mature teratoma is called struma ovarii, and it can cause hyperthyroidism with a suppressed TSH, a normal neck gland and pelvic radioiodine uptake. Metastatic thyroid carcinoma is rare. A TSH-secreting pituitary adenoma would raise TSH. A trophoblastic tumour would also give a raised hCG and a stimulated neck gland.
  5. The correct answer is A (Chronic anovulation with unopposed oestrogen, lacking cyclical progesterone to induce secretory change): Anovulation leaves the endometrium exposed to oestrogen without a luteal progesterone phase, giving proliferative change, hyperplasia and a higher carcinoma risk. Androgens do not drive gland proliferation directly. Hyperinsulinaemia lowers sex hormone binding globulin and raises, not lowers, free oestradiol. Progesterone would induce secretory change and limit growth.
  6. The correct answer is A (HPV-independent pathway through differentiated vulval intraepithelial neoplasia in lichen sclerosus): Lichen sclerosus predisposes to HPV-independent squamous carcinoma through differentiated vulval intraepithelial neoplasia with basal atypia, abnormal p53 and negative p16, usually in older women. HPV-associated carcinoma shows block-positive p16 and arises from high-grade lesions in younger women. Condyloma with HPV 6 and 11 is benign. Paget disease is glandular.
  7. The correct answer is D (It is a usual-type lesion linked to high-risk HPV, most often HPV 16, in younger women): Full-thickness atypia with block-positive p16 in a younger smoker is vulval high-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion, the usual type driven by high-risk HPV, particularly type 16. Differentiated lesions are HPV-independent and associated with lichen sclerosus. HPV 6 and 11 produce low-grade lesions and condylomata. Paget disease involves glandular, mucin-positive cells.
  8. The correct answer is A (Extramammary Paget disease, an intraepithelial adenocarcinoma of apocrine type): Large pale mucin-positive and CK7-positive cells within the epidermis define extramammary Paget disease of the vulva, an intraepithelial adenocarcinoma that may accompany an underlying adnexal or visceral carcinoma. Melanoma in situ would stain with S100 and SOX10 but not mucicarmine. Differentiated VIN is squamous. Hailey-Hailey disease is benign acantholysis.
  9. The correct answer is C (Duct obstruction with mucus retention, usually lined by transitional or columnar epithelium): A posterolateral introital cyst with mucoid content arises from obstruction of the Bartholin gland duct, with retained mucus and a transitional or columnar lining. Gartner duct cysts lie along the anterolateral vaginal wall. An inclusion cyst contains keratin. A canal of Nuck hydrocele lies in the anterior labium, extending toward the inguinal canal.
  10. The correct answer is B (Mesonephric (Wolffian) duct remnant, lined by non-mucinous cuboidal epithelium): A cyst along the anterolateral vaginal wall with ipsilateral renal agenesis points to a Gartner duct cyst from the mesonephric duct, whose maldevelopment also disrupts the ureteric bud. It has a cuboidal, non-mucinous lining. Müllerian cysts have mucinous or tubal-type lining. A Skene gland cyst lies beside the urethral meatus.
  11. The correct answer is D (Vaginal adenosis, with Müllerian glandular epithelium replacing the normal squamous mucosa): Clear cell adenocarcinoma of the vagina in a young woman exposed in utero to diethylstilboestrol arises in vaginal adenosis, in which Müllerian glandular epithelium persists in the upper vagina. Squamous intraepithelial neoplasia is HPV-related and squamous. Gartner remnants are mesonephric. Endometriosis-related clear cell carcinoma occurs in the ovary in older women.
  12. The correct answer is C (E6 promotes degradation of p53, and E7 inactivates the retinoblastoma protein, driving cell cycle entry): E6 binds p53 and targets it for ubiquitin-mediated degradation, while E7 binds and inactivates Rb, releasing E2F and driving the cell cycle. The targets are not interchangeable. Loss of Rb function causes p16 to be strongly overexpressed, which is why block-positive p16 is a surrogate marker. HPV oncogenesis does not depend on RAS activation.
  13. The correct answer is D (High-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion, equivalent to CIN3, with strong diffuse p16 staining): Full-thickness basaloid atypia with abnormal mitoses and an intact basement membrane is CIN3, grouped as high-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion, and shows strong block-positive p16. Low-grade lesions involve the lower third and show patchy staining. Microinvasion requires stromal invasion. ASC-US is a cytology category, not a biopsy diagnosis.
  14. The correct answer is D (Immature metaplastic squamous cells in the transformation zone are susceptible to HPV infection): Cervical neoplasia arises in the transformation zone, where columnar epithelium is replaced by immature squamous metaplasia that is readily infected by HPV, which then integrates into basal cells. Mucus is not the mechanism. Mature ectocervical squamous epithelium is relatively resistant. Tubal epithelium is not present on the cervix.
  15. The correct answer is C (Gastric-type endocervical adenocarcinoma, an HPV-independent tumour with an aggressive course): Negative HPV testing, deep stromal infiltration and mucinous glands with foamy cytoplasm and bland nuclei indicate gastric-type adenocarcinoma, an HPV-independent endocervical tumour that is often missed on cytology and has a poor prognosis. Usual-type disease is HPV-driven and p16 positive. Mesonephric carcinoma shows eosinophilic secretions and GATA3. Clear cell carcinoma has hobnail cells.
  16. The correct answer is B (Stage IB2, because invasion exceeds 5 mm and size is between 2 and 4 cm): Under FIGO 2018, a tumour confined to the cervix with invasion beyond 5 mm is stage IB, subdivided by greatest dimension: IB1 up to 2 cm, IB2 more than 2 cm and less than 4 cm, and IB3 at least 4 cm. An intact stromal ring indicates no parametrial invasion, and no vaginal involvement is described.
  17. The correct answer is B (Stage IIB, because of parametrial involvement that stops short of the pelvic sidewall): Tumour extending beyond the cervix into the parametrium without reaching the pelvic sidewall, the lower vagina or causing hydronephrosis is stage IIB. IIA involves the upper two thirds of the vagina without parametrial invasion. IIIB needs sidewall extension or hydronephrosis. IB3 is confined to the cervix, which stromal ring disruption with parametrial soft tissue excludes.
  18. The correct answer is B (Stage IIIB, from ureteric obstruction by parametrial tumour or compressing lymph nodes): FIGO 2018 classifies extension to the pelvic sidewall and any hydronephrosis or nonfunctioning kidney, not explained by another cause, as stage IIIB, because ureteric obstruction implies parametrial or nodal tumour reaching the pelvic wall. Stage IVA requires biopsy-proven mucosal invasion of bladder or rectum. Stage IIIA concerns the lower third of the vagina.
  19. The correct answer is D (Stage IIIC1, because pelvic nodal metastasis upstages disease even when the primary tumour is small): FIGO 2018 incorporates nodal status: pelvic node metastasis is stage IIIC1 and para-aortic node metastasis is IIIC2, whatever the primary tumour size, and stage is annotated r or p for imaging or pathology. The 2009 system ignored nodes. Distant metastasis, including beyond the para-aortic region, defines stage IVB.
  20. The correct answer is B (Bilateral ureteric obstruction by spread of pelvic tumour into the parametria and pelvic sidewalls): Cervical carcinoma spreads laterally through the parametria, encasing the ureters where they pass beside the cervix, so bilateral hydronephrosis and obstructive renal failure were historically the commonest cause of death. Haemorrhage is a separate complication. Lung metastases are late. Renal vein invasion would not explain dilated collecting systems.
  21. The correct answer is D (Many patients have a coexisting endometrioid carcinoma in the hysterectomy specimen): Atypical hyperplasia (endometrioid intraepithelial neoplasia) is the precursor of endometrioid carcinoma, and a sizeable fraction of biopsies hide invasive cancer elsewhere in the uterus. For this reason hysterectomy specimens are examined carefully. Serous carcinoma arises by a different, TP53 driven route from atrophic endometrium. Nodal spread belongs to invasive disease, not to a lesion without stromal invasion.
  22. The correct answer is B (Loss of function of the PTEN tumour suppressor gene on chromosome 10): Endometrioid carcinoma is linked to unopposed oestrogen and commonly shows PTEN inactivation, along with PIK3CA, ARID1A and CTNNB1 changes. BRCA1 germline mutations raise ovarian risk but do not define this tumour. TP53 mutation and marked copy number change characterise serous carcinoma. HER2 amplification occurs in a subset of serous tumours, not in typical endometrioid disease.
  23. The correct answer is A (Early TP53 mutation with aberrant p53 staining and a propensity for transtubal peritoneal seeding): Serous carcinoma arises in atrophic endometrium from serous intraepithelial carcinoma and carries early TP53 mutation. Exfoliated cells can pass through the tubes, producing peritoneal disease out of proportion to myometrial invasion. The mismatch repair deficient and PTEN related groups behave differently and are usually endometrioid, and oestrogen stimulation is not the driver here.
  24. The correct answer is D (Stage II, because tumour has invaded the cervical stroma but remains within the uterus): FIGO 2018 defines stage II as tumour invading the cervical stromal connective tissue without extending beyond the uterus. Stage I applies to disease confined to the corpus, graded by myometrial invasion below or at least half. Stage IIIB needs vaginal or parametrial involvement, which the described MRI excludes. Glandular cervical involvement alone does not upstage.
  25. The correct answer is D (Somatic mutation of the MED12 gene in a large proportion of cases): Most sporadic leiomyomas carry MED12 exon 2 mutations. The JAZF1-SUZ12 fusion belongs to low grade endometrial stromal sarcoma. Complex karyotypes with RB1 and TP53 loss are typical of leiomyosarcoma. Fumarate hydratase mutations underlie hereditary leiomyomatosis and renal cell carcinoma but account for a small minority.
  26. The correct answer is C (Venous thrombosis with haemorrhagic infarction of a leiomyoma during pregnancy): Red or carneous degeneration is haemorrhagic infarction of a fibroid, typically in pregnancy, causing pain, low fever and a fleshy red cut surface. Hyaline degeneration is the commonest change but is pale and painless. Sarcomatous change is rare and shows atypia, not a beefy appearance. Calcification is a late change after prolonged ischaemia.
  27. The correct answer is B (Benign endometrial glands and stroma deep within myometrium, well below the basal layer): Adenomyosis is defined by endometrial glands and stroma within the myometrium, usually more than a few millimetres below the basalis, with reactive smooth muscle hypertrophy. This produces junctional zone thickening and a diffusely enlarged globular uterus. A fibroid is a discrete whorled nodule. Atypical hyperplasia is a mucosal lesion, and intravascular worm-like growth indicates stromal sarcoma.
  28. The correct answer is A (Diffuse moderate to severe nuclear atypia, brisk mitotic activity, and coagulative tumour cell necrosis): Leiomyosarcoma is diagnosed on at least two of three features: significant atypia, high mitotic count and tumour cell necrosis with a coagulative pattern. Degenerative changes such as hyaline change are common in fibroids and not diagnostic. Bland fascicles with no mitoses describe an ordinary leiomyoma. Intravascular stromal-like cells indicate endometrial stromal sarcoma.
  29. The correct answer is A (Chromosomal translocation producing a JAZF1-SUZ12 fusion gene in many low grade tumours): Low grade endometrial stromal sarcoma grows as worm-like plugs in vessels and lymphatics and often carries JAZF1-SUZ12 or related fusions. MED12 mutation is typical of leiomyoma. PTEN loss and microsatellite instability belong to glandular endometrioid carcinoma. TP53 mutation with complex karyotypes marks high grade sarcomas and serous carcinoma.
  30. The correct answer is C (It is a metaplastic carcinoma in which the spindle component derives from epithelial cells): Molecular studies show that the epithelial and mesenchymal components share common mutations, supporting monoclonal origin as a carcinoma that has undergone epithelial to mesenchymal transition. The old collision theory has been abandoned. High-grade malignant glands exclude entrapped benign glands, and the glandular component excludes a pure leiomyosarcoma. It is staged and treated as a high grade carcinoma.
  31. The correct answer is C (Germline mutation in a DNA mismatch repair gene, causing a hereditary cancer syndrome): Loss of MSH2 and MSH6 with preserved MLH1 and PMS2 points to a germline mismatch repair defect, as in Lynch syndrome, and this is confirmed by germline testing. Sporadic mismatch repair loss usually involves MLH1 methylation with PMS2 loss as well. BRCA1 defects do not alter mismatch repair staining. POLE mutated tumours keep normal mismatch repair protein expression.
  32. The correct answer is D (Partial oestrogen agonist activity in the endometrium promotes polyps, hyperplasia and cancer): Tamoxifen blocks oestrogen receptors in breast tissue but acts as a partial agonist in the endometrium, so polyps, hyperplasia and carcinoma are more frequent. Cystic change is common but does not establish a benign process, so persistent bleeding needs sampling. Stromal sarcoma and decidual change are not the recognised mechanisms.
  33. The correct answer is A (Pathogenic mutation in the exonuclease domain of DNA polymerase epsilon): Tumours with pathogenic POLE exonuclease domain mutations are ultramutated and, despite frequent high grade morphology, have an excellent prognosis in the TCGA derived molecular classification. Mismatch repair deficiency is hypermutated but carries intermediate outcomes. TP53 abnormal tumours have the poorest outcomes. CTNNB1 mutated tumours have low mutational burden.
  34. The correct answer is D (Diploid 46,XX karyotype in which the nuclear DNA is entirely paternal): Complete moles are androgenetic: an empty egg is fertilised by one sperm that duplicates, or occasionally by two sperm, giving 46,XX or 46,XY with entirely paternal nuclear DNA. Triploidy characterises partial mole. A biparental diploid set is normal. Trisomy 16 is a frequent cause of spontaneous miscarriage but does not produce diffuse villous swelling.
  35. The correct answer is A (Triploid karyotype resulting from fertilisation of one egg by two sperm): Partial moles are usually triploid, with two paternal and one maternal haploid set, and retain maternal DNA, so p57 is positive. Fetal tissue or fetal red cells may be present. A complete mole is androgenetic and p57 negative. Monosomy X typically causes early loss with hydrops, and tetraploidy is uncommon.
  36. The correct answer is B (A biphasic population of mononuclear cytotrophoblast and multinucleated syncytiotrophoblast cells): Choriocarcinoma consists of cytotrophoblast and syncytiotrophoblast without villi, with haemorrhage and necrosis, and it spreads through the blood to lungs, so hCG stays high. Placental site trophoblastic tumour has intermediate trophoblast and human placental lactogen. Hydropic villi indicate a mole or invasive mole. Epithelioid trophoblastic tumour forms nodules of intermediate trophoblast.
  37. The correct answer is A (Invasive mole, as villous structures persist within the deeply invading trophoblast): An invasive mole is a complete mole whose villi invade myometrium and vessels, so villi are retained. This is the feature separating it from choriocarcinoma, which has no villi. Placental site tumour shows intermediate trophoblast and low hCG. The preceding lesion was a complete mole, so a partial mole is not the cause.
  38. The correct answer is A (Intermediate trophoblast normally present at the placental implantation site): Placental site trophoblastic tumour derives from intermediate trophoblast, which infiltrates between myometrial fibres and expresses human placental lactogen with relatively little hCG. Choriocarcinoma consists of cytotrophoblast and syncytiotrophoblast, and syncytiotrophoblast makes most hCG. Decidual stromal cells are not trophoblastic and do not form this tumour.
  39. The correct answer is C (Mucosal scarring with fusion of the plicae from prior salpingitis, delaying ovum transport): Chronic salpingitis scars and fuses the mucosal folds and damages cilia, so the fertilised ovum is trapped and implants in the tube, usually the ampulla. Congenital tubal anomalies are rare. Progestogen exposure slows rather than speeds transport. Complete obliteration prevents fertilisation instead of allowing implantation.
  40. The correct answer is B (Benign Arias-Stella reaction, supporting an extrauterine gestation rather than neoplasia): The Arias-Stella reaction is a benign hypersecretory change in endometrial glands caused by trophoblastic hormones, with alarming nuclear enlargement. It is seen in ectopic and intrauterine pregnancy and is not preneoplastic. Clear cell and serous carcinoma show true malignant architecture and mitoses. A mole would not simply vanish because villi are not sampled.
  41. The correct answer is C (Late rupture of a highly vascular site supplied by uterine and ovarian arteries): Interstitial or cornual pregnancy implants in the intramural tube near the junction of uterine and ovarian arterial supply. The surrounding myometrium allows it to grow to about 8 to 12 weeks before rupturing with torrential bleeding. Isthmic pregnancies rupture earlier and ampullary ones often abort slowly. Free intraperitoneal blood excludes a purely retroperitoneal haematoma.
  42. The correct answer is C (Poor trophoblast invasion leaving spiral arteries narrow, with hypoperfusion and endothelial injury): Pre-eclampsia begins with failure of extravillous trophoblast to remodel spiral arteries into low resistance vessels. The ischaemic placenta releases antiangiogenic factors such as soluble Flt-1, causing maternal endothelial injury, hypertension and proteinuria. The condition is not an immune complex nephritis, and prostacyclin is reduced rather than raised. Renal artery stenosis is a separate cause of hypertension.
  43. The correct answer is D (Acute atherosis of decidual spiral arteries, a vasculopathy of hypertensive pregnancy): Acute atherosis is fibrinoid necrosis with foamy macrophages in decidual arterioles and is a hallmark of hypertensive disorders of pregnancy. Normal spiral artery conversion loses the media, and its absence underlies pre-eclampsia. Villitis involves villi, and fetal thrombotic vasculopathy affects fetal stem vessels, not maternal vessels.
  44. The correct answer is C (Deficient decidua basalis, usually at a uterine scar, allowing direct villous attachment): Accreta results from absence or deficiency of the decidua basalis, commonly over a caesarean scar or after previous uterine surgery, so villi attach directly to or invade the myometrium. The Nitabuch fibrinoid layer is normally deficient in accreta. Myometrial spasm can trap a normal placenta but it is not the defect. Shallow trophoblast invasion belongs to pre-eclampsia.
  45. The correct answer is C (Rupture of maternal decidual vessels with haemorrhage that strips the placenta from its bed): Abruption follows rupture of decidual arterioles, often with hypertension, cocaine or trauma, and an expanding retroplacental clot detaches the placenta and leaves an indentation on the maternal surface. Vasa praevia and placenta praevia give painless bleeding. Uterine scar rupture presents with loss of fetal station and abdominal pain, not a retroplacental clot.
  46. The correct answer is A (Periportal sinusoidal fibrin deposition with haemorrhage and necrosis from microangiopathy): HELLP is a severe pre-eclampsia variant with microangiopathic haemolysis, elevated liver enzymes and low platelets. The liver shows fibrin deposition in periportal sinusoids with haemorrhage and necrosis, and bleeding may form a subcapsular haematoma. Centrilobular necrosis follows shock. Fatty liver of pregnancy shows microvesicular fat, and cholestasis of pregnancy causes pruritus with raised bile acids.
  47. The correct answer is D (Funisitis, indicating a fetal inflammatory response to ascending infection of the amniotic cavity): Neutrophils within cord vessels and Wharton's jelly define funisitis, the fetal response in ascending amniotic infection, whereas neutrophils limited to membranes represent the maternal response. Chronic villitis implies haematogenous infection and has lymphocytes, not neutrophils. Fetal thrombotic vasculopathy is a vascular lesion with avascular villi.
  48. The correct answer is B (Interruption of maternal spiral artery supply with ischaemic necrosis of downstream villi): A wedge shaped infarct with the base on the maternal surface follows occlusion of a maternal spiral artery, commonly in hypertension or pre-eclampsia, and the villi undergo coagulative necrosis. Fetal thrombotic vasculopathy affects fetal vessels with avascular villi but not in a maternal wedge. Infection produces neutrophils, and massive perivillous fibrin deposition has a different pattern.
  49. The correct answer is A (Chorangioma, a benign proliferation of capillaries within villous stroma): Chorangioma is the most common placental tumour, a hamartoma like capillary proliferation in villous stroma. Large lesions can cause polyhydramnios, fetal anaemia and hydrops. Placental site nodules are small and incidental. Metastasis to the placenta is rare. A subchorionic haematoma is a blood clot, not a circumscribed vascular mass.
  50. The correct answer is D (Anaphylactoid maternal reaction to fetal debris with pulmonary vasospasm and DIC): Amniotic fluid embolism is an abrupt maternal reaction to fetal elements in the circulation, with cardiovascular collapse, hypoxia and DIC. Squames, lanugo and mucin in pulmonary vessels support the diagnosis. Thromboembolism would show thrombus, air embolism would show no particulate debris, and cardiomyopathy would not explain fetal material in pulmonary vessels.
  51. The correct answer is A (Pulmonary hypoplasia caused by lack of amniotic fluid during lung development): Bilateral renal agenesis causes oligohydramnios, which compresses the fetus (Potter facies, limb deformities) and impairs lung growth, giving fatal pulmonary hypoplasia. Hyaline membrane disease is a feature of prematurity. Diaphragmatic hernia causes hypoplasia by a different mechanism and is not related to absent kidneys. Meconium aspiration is an intrapartum event.
  52. The correct answer is C (Haemolysis of fetal red cells by maternal IgG antibodies, causing severe anaemia and heart failure): Rhesus alloimmunisation sends maternal IgG across the placenta to coat fetal Rh positive red cells, causing haemolysis, severe anaemia, extramedullary haematopoiesis and heart failure with hydrops. Parvovirus B19 suppresses erythropoiesis, so it does not cause erythroid hyperplasia. Turner syndrome and arrhythmia cause non-immune hydrops without maternal antibodies.
  53. The correct answer is C (Unbalanced arteriovenous anastomoses within a shared placental parenchyma): Monochorionic placentas almost always have vascular anastomoses. When arteriovenous connections are unbalanced, one twin (donor) loses volume and develops oligohydramnios and growth restriction, and the other (recipient) is overloaded with polyhydramnios and a large bladder. Dichorionic placentas do not share fetal vessels. Velamentous insertion raises vasa praevia risk and not shunting.
  54. The correct answer is A (Impaired fetal swallowing with transudation of fluid from exposed meninges and tissue): Anencephaly is a neural tube closure defect. The fetus cannot swallow effectively because of the absent forebrain and brainstem dysfunction, and exposed tissue leaks fluid, causing polyhydramnios. Alpha-fetoprotein rises because it leaks from the open defect. Maternal diabetes, chorangioma and duodenal atresia can cause polyhydramnios but do not fit this picture.
  55. The correct answer is A (Cytomegalovirus, with periventricular calcification from necrosis of germinal matrix cells): Congenital cytomegalovirus infection causes microcephaly, hepatosplenomegaly, thrombocytopenia, deafness and periventricular calcification, and the infected cells show owl's eye inclusions. Toxoplasmosis calcifies diffusely, Zika produces subcortical calcification with cortical thinning, and rubella causes cataracts and cardiac defects.
  56. The correct answer is A (Apocrine metaplasia, a non-proliferative change carrying no added breast cancer risk): Cysts lined by cells with abundant eosinophilic granular cytoplasm and apical snouts show apocrine metaplasia, which is non-proliferative and carries no added cancer risk. Flat epithelial atypia has columnar cells with low-grade nuclear atypia, not abundant granular cytoplasm. Usual ductal hyperplasia is a proliferative lesion with a streaming epithelial pattern. Atypical lobular hyperplasia involves discohesive monomorphic cells expanding acini.
  57. The correct answer is D (Sclerosing adenosis, a benign lesion that can mimic invasive carcinoma on needle sampling): A lobulocentric pattern with compressed acini and a persistent myoepithelial layer defines sclerosing adenosis, which calcifies and can look infiltrative. Tubular carcinoma lacks myoepithelium and is not lobulocentric. Radial scar has a central fibroelastotic nidus with radiating ducts. Classic lobular carcinoma lacks gland formation and shows discohesive cells.
  58. The correct answer is B (A benign complex sclerosing lesion that mimics carcinoma radiologically and may harbour atypia): A fibroelastotic core with entrapped ducts and radiating proliferative change is a radial scar or complex sclerosing lesion, which mimics carcinoma on mammography and can harbour atypia or malignancy, hence excision. Tubular carcinoma has angulated glands without myoepithelium. Fat necrosis needs a history of trauma and shows necrotic adipocytes. DCIS does not form this architecture.
  59. The correct answer is B (A benign lesion of the terminal duct lobular unit with stromal MED12 mutations): Fibroadenoma is a benign fibroepithelial lesion arising from the terminal duct lobular unit, and recurrent MED12 exon 2 mutations are found in its stroma. Epithelial PIK3CA-driven progression describes carcinoma pathways. Fat necrosis is reactive. TERT promoter mutations are typical of borderline and malignant phyllodes tumours.
  60. The correct answer is D (Dystrophic calcification within hyalinised, involuting stroma of a benign fibroepithelial lesion): Coarse popcorn calcification in a stable circumscribed mass reflects dystrophic calcification in the hyalinised stroma of an involuting fibroadenoma, common after menopause. Secretory calcifications are rod-like and ductal. Comedonecrosis calcification is fine, linear and branching. Psammoma bodies are laminated and occur in some papillary lesions.
  61. The correct answer is C (Solitary central papilloma, a benign lesion arising in a large lactiferous duct): A solitary subareolar papilloma with a two-layered epithelium on fibrovascular cores explains bloody discharge from a single duct. Papillary carcinoma lacks the myoepithelial layer on the papillae. Multiple peripheral papillomas are smaller and linked to atypia. Duct ectasia causes thick discharge, not a filling defect with arborescent cores.
  62. The correct answer is A (Usual ductal hyperplasia, a polyclonal proliferation with slightly increased relative risk of carcinoma): Streaming cells, irregular peripheral spaces, varied nuclei and mosaic CK5/6 staining indicate a polyclonal proliferation, usual ductal hyperplasia. Atypical hyperplasia and low-grade DCIS are clonal, with uniform cells, rigid spaces and diffuse loss of basal keratins. Columnar cell lesions have a different lining architecture.
  63. The correct answer is B (About fourfold to fivefold higher relative risk in both breasts, with possible upgrade on surgical excision): Atypical ductal hyperplasia carries about a four to five fold relative risk, applying to both breasts, and core biopsy diagnoses are commonly upgraded to DCIS or invasive disease at excision. No added risk would describe non-proliferative change. Atypia risk is bilateral. A tenfold figure and mastectomy overstate a lesion below the size and extent of DCIS.
  64. The correct answer is A (Classic lobular carcinoma in situ, a marker of increased risk in both breasts, often multicentric): Discohesive monomorphic cells filling acini with E-cadherin loss are classic LCIS, which is a risk indicator for invasive carcinoma in either breast and is often multifocal and bilateral. Ductal lesions retain E-cadherin. Pleomorphic LCIS shows large atypical nuclei and necrosis and is managed with clear excision.
  65. The correct answer is A (Dystrophic mineralisation of necrotic luminal debris within a high-grade intraductal carcinoma): Calcification of comedo-type necrosis inside ducts in high-grade DCIS produces fine linear branching pleomorphic forms following the duct. Secretory calcification is coarse and rod-like in benign ducts. Calcium oxalate crystals sit in apocrine cysts and are seen on polarisation. Stromal calcification is coarse.
  66. The correct answer is B (pT1mi, because the invasive focus does not exceed 1 mm in greatest dimension): Microinvasion in the TNM system is an invasive focus of 1 mm or less in greatest dimension, giving pT1mi. T1a begins above 1 mm up to 5 mm. The 2 mm and 0.2 mm figures belong to nodal micrometastasis and isolated tumour cell definitions, which do not apply to primary tumour staging.
  67. The correct answer is A (Invasive carcinoma of no special type, the most frequent histological pattern among invasive breast cancers): A stellate, firm tumour with chalky streaks and cords and nests in desmoplastic stroma with no special pattern is invasive carcinoma of no special type, which makes up the majority of invasive breast cancers. Lobular carcinoma shows single files, tubular carcinoma shows angulated open glands, and metaplastic carcinoma shows squamous or spindle differentiation.
  68. The correct answer is D (Biallelic loss of CDH1 causing absent E-cadherin and failure of cell adhesion): Single-file, targetoid infiltration by small uniform ER positive cells describes invasive lobular carcinoma, in which CDH1 inactivation removes E-cadherin and the cells lose cohesion. ERBB2 amplification gives cohesive nests, TP53 loss gives pleomorphic circumscribed tumours, and the ETV6-NTRK3 fusion defines secretory carcinoma.
  69. The correct answer is C (Invasive lobular carcinoma, which spreads to serosal surfaces, gastrointestinal tract and ovaries): Late diffuse infiltration of the gastric wall and peritoneum with sparing of liver and lung is typical of metastatic invasive lobular carcinoma. Carcinoma of no special type favours bone, lung, liver and brain. Pure mucinous and tubular carcinomas rarely metastasise and do not show this pattern.
  70. The correct answer is B (Excellent outcome with rare nodal metastasis, with strong ER positivity and HER2 negativity): Tubular carcinoma is a well-differentiated special type with an excellent prognosis, rare nodal involvement, and an ER positive, PR positive, HER2 negative profile. Triple negative and HER2 amplified patterns are typical of high-grade tumours. It is an infiltrative tumour with a stellate edge, not an encapsulated one.
  71. The correct answer is B (Favourable outcome with a lower rate of axillary node involvement than carcinoma of no special type): Pure mucinous carcinoma is a hormone receptor positive tumour with low-grade cells in mucin pools, a pushing border and a good prognosis with infrequent nodal metastasis. Mixed forms behave worse. Micropapillary carcinoma is aggressive. A lymphoid-rich, receptor negative circumscribed tumour describes medullary-type carcinoma.
  72. The correct answer is A (Triple negative basal-like, expressing CK5/6 and EGFR with high proliferation): BRCA1-related breast cancers are typically high-grade, circumscribed, necrotic, lymphocyte-rich triple negative basal-like carcinomas. Luminal A and B tumours are ER positive and are more typical of BRCA2 related disease. HER2 enriched tumours are driven by ERBB2 amplification and are not the usual phenotype in BRCA1 carriers.
  73. The correct answer is C (Tumour emboli filling dermal lymphatic channels, causing lymphatic obstruction and oedema): Diffuse erythema, warmth and peau d'orange without a mass or antibiotic response indicate inflammatory carcinoma, where tumour emboli block dermal lymphatics. It is staged T4d. Abscess, granulomatous mastitis and duct ectasia produce inflammatory features but do not explain failure of antibiotics with diffuse skin involvement.
  74. The correct answer is D (Grade 2, because the total falls in the intermediate range of six to seven): The Nottingham grade adds the three component scores: 3 plus 3 plus 1 gives 7, which is grade 2 (6 to 7). Grade 1 is a total of 3 to 5 and grade 3 is 8 to 9. No single component decides the grade.
  75. The correct answer is A (Luminal A, expected to benefit from endocrine therapy with limited added benefit from chemotherapy): Strong ER and PR positivity, HER2 negativity and low proliferation define a luminal A-like tumour, treated mainly with endocrine therapy. Luminal B tumours have higher proliferation or lower PR. HER2 enriched needs HER2 amplification. Basal-like tumours are ER negative.
  76. The correct answer is D (ERBB2 amplification drives receptor overexpression; trastuzumab requires monitoring of left ventricular function for cardiotoxicity): Strong complete membranous staining in over 10 percent of cells is HER2 positive, caused by ERBB2 gene amplification. Trastuzumab causes reversible cardiomyopathy, so ventricular function is monitored. PTEN loss, BRCA1 deficiency and ESR1 mutation relate to other pathways, and their drug toxicities differ.
  77. The correct answer is C (Luminal B, with higher proliferation and lower progesterone receptor expression than luminal A tumours): ER positivity with high Ki-67, grade 3 morphology and absent PR indicates luminal B-like disease, which is more proliferative and more endocrine-resistant. Luminal A has low proliferation. A normal-like subtype is low proliferation. HER2 enriched requires HER2 positivity, which is absent here.
  78. The correct answer is A (Malignant phyllodes tumour, with a risk of haematogenous metastasis to lung but rare nodal spread): Marked stromal atypia and overgrowth, 10 or more mitoses per 10 HPF and infiltrative borders make this a malignant phyllodes tumour, which metastasises through blood, typically to lung, and rarely to nodes. Borderline tumours have fewer mitoses. A benign cellular fibroadenoma has bland stroma. Metaplastic carcinoma has an epithelial origin.
  79. The correct answer is C (Stromal hypercellularity accentuated around ducts, with exaggerated leaf-like clefts): Phyllodes tumour shows leaf-like architecture, periductal stromal condensation and stromal cellularity exceeding that of fibroadenoma, and rapid growth supports it. Slit-like ducts in uniform myxoid stroma, epithelial hyperplasia with apocrine change, and hyalinised calcified stroma all occur in fibroadenoma.
  80. The correct answer is C (Relative excess of oestrogen effect over androgen effect during pubertal hormonal change): Transient pubertal gynaecomastia reflects a relative excess of oestrogen action over androgen action, with ductal proliferation and oedematous stroma and no testicular abnormality. Prolactinoma gives galactorrhoea. Klinefelter syndrome causes small firm testes. A discrete mass would suggest a fibroepithelial neoplasm.
  81. The correct answer is A (BRCA2, which accounts for a larger share of male cases than the other BRCA gene): BRCA2 pathogenic variants are the dominant inherited cause of male breast cancer, with a markedly higher risk than BRCA1. The tumours are usually ER positive invasive carcinoma of no special type. CDH1 and TP53 account for few male cases.
  82. The correct answer is C (CD30 positive and ALK negative large T-cell lymphoma cells): Late seroma around a textured implant with large cells with horseshoe nuclei is breast implant associated anaplastic large cell lymphoma, which is CD30 positive and ALK negative. ALK positive disease is a different systemic lymphoma. B-cell markers and cytokeratins do not describe this entity.
  83. The correct answer is C (Foreign-body granulomatous reaction with vacuolated macrophages and giant cells around droplets): Extruded silicone provokes a foreign-body reaction with vacuolated histiocytes and giant cells, and droplets are carried to axillary nodes. Sarcoidosis shows tight non-necrotising granulomas with inclusions. Infection shows neutrophils. Anaplastic large cell lymphoma shows sheets of CD30 positive cells.
  84. The correct answer is A (Necrotic adipocytes surrounded by foamy macrophages, multinucleated giant cells and fibrosis): A history of trauma and a firm yellow-white gritty lesion indicate fat necrosis, with necrotic fat, foamy macrophages, giant cells and later fibrosis and calcification, which can mimic carcinoma. Carcinoma shows atypical infiltrating cells. Duct ectasia and granulomatous mastitis have different distributions.
  85. The correct answer is C (Idiopathic granulomatous lobular mastitis, a diagnosis of exclusion): Lobule-centred granulomas with neutrophils, in a parous young woman with negative microbiology and no caseation, indicate idiopathic granulomatous lobular mastitis, after excluding infection and sarcoidosis. Duct ectasia is periductal in older women. Sarcoidosis needs systemic features. Fat necrosis follows trauma.
  86. The correct answer is A (pN1mi, because the deposit exceeds 0.2 mm but does not exceed 2.0 mm): Isolated tumour cells are 0.2 mm or less. A deposit greater than 0.2 mm up to 2.0 mm is a micrometastasis, pN1mi. A macrometastasis exceeds 2.0 mm and gives pN1a. The pN2a category requires four to nine involved nodes.
  87. The correct answer is B (Underlying in situ or invasive carcinoma in most cases, spread along lactiferous ducts): Large vacuolated Paget cells within the epidermis expressing CK7, often HER2 positive, indicate Paget disease of the nipple, usually associated with underlying DCIS or invasive carcinoma. Dermatitis lacks intraepidermal glandular cells. Melanoma is S100 positive and CK7 negative. Bowen disease shows squamous atypia.
  88. The correct answer is C (MYC amplification, often with FLT4 co-amplification, in radiation-associated cases): Atypical vascular channels positive for CD31 and ERG after radiotherapy indicate angiosarcoma, and radiation-associated cases show MYC amplification, frequently with FLT4. ERBB2 and PIK3CA changes belong to epithelial carcinoma. CTNNB1 mutations occur in fibromatosis.
  89. The correct answer is C (Flat epithelial atypia, a low-grade lesion considered a possible early neoplastic precursor): Columnar lining cells with low-grade monomorphic nuclei and no complex architecture define flat epithelial atypia, an early low-grade neoplastic lesion often found with calcification. Columnar cell change lacks nuclear atypia. Atypical ductal hyperplasia requires architectural complexity. Atypical lobular hyperplasia shows discohesive cells.
  90. The correct answer is A (Metaplastic carcinoma, which spreads by blood to lung more than to axillary nodes): Squamous differentiation with spindle cells in a triple negative circumscribed tumour is metaplastic carcinoma, which tends to metastasise through blood to lung, with infrequent nodal spread. Malignant phyllodes has a benign epithelial component. Adenoid cystic carcinoma has a cribriform epithelial pattern and indolent course.
  91. The correct answer is D (Hyaline cartilage and fibrous tissue within the organising haematoma): At about two weeks the haematoma has been replaced by a soft callus of fibrocartilage and hyaline cartilage, which is radiolucent or faintly dense. Lamellar bone and remodelling appear months later. Periosteal woven bone forms a hard callus at the periphery but does not account for most of the central bridge. Granulation tissue precedes cartilage and is already being replaced.
  92. The correct answer is A (Adequate blood supply with excessive motion preventing bridging): Hypertrophic non-union shows a vascular, biologically active fracture with abundant callus but inadequate stability, so motion repeatedly disrupts bridging. Atrophic non-union has poor vascularity with little callus and tapered ends. Interposed soft tissue and infection can cause non-union but do not produce this florid sclerotic flared appearance with preserved fibrocartilage.
  93. The correct answer is D (Marrow fat globules entering torn veins and lodging in capillary beds): Fat embolism follows fractures of long bones when marrow fat enters ruptured venules and lodges in lung capillaries, then free fatty acids cause endothelial injury and pneumonitis. Passage through the lung into systemic circulation explains the cerebral and skin features. Pulmonary thromboembolism gives a different pattern, and the petechiae with delayed onset are typical of fat rather than air or septic emboli.
  94. The correct answer is D (Widened osteoid seams with delayed mineralisation of new matrix): The transverse lucent bands are pseudofractures (Looser zones) that arise because unmineralised osteoid is mechanically weak. Osteomalacia is defined by a mineralisation defect, so osteoid accumulates in thickened seams. Osteoporosis has qualitatively normal bone, hyperparathyroidism produces osteoclastic resorption with marrow fibrosis, and Paget disease gives a mosaic cement line pattern.
  95. The correct answer is D (Expanded hypertrophic zone of uncalcified cartilage with disordered ossification): Impaired calcification of cartilage matrix prevents vascular invasion and removal of hypertrophic chondrocytes, so the zone widens and the plate flares and cups. Physeal bars follow trauma, retained primary spongiosa occurs in osteopetrosis, and marrow fibrosis with resorption belongs to hyperparathyroidism.
  96. The correct answer is B (Increased RANKL-driven osteoclast activity after loss of oestrogen restraint): Oestrogen normally restrains osteoclastogenesis by reducing RANKL and cytokine signalling, so deficiency raises resorption and the largely trabecular skeleton is affected first. Mineralisation is normal in osteoporosis, no hormone excess is described, and collagen mutations cause osteogenesis imperfecta in younger patients.
  97. The correct answer is C (Multinucleated giant cells in a vascular fibrous stroma with haemosiderin): This is a brown tumour of hyperparathyroidism, a reparative giant cell lesion in which haemorrhage and haemosiderin give the brown colour. Subperiosteal phalangeal resorption supports the diagnosis. Myeloma, Ewing sarcoma and chondrosarcoma can look lytic but have distinct cell populations.
  98. The correct answer is B (Secondary hyperparathyroidism driving high osteoblastic turnover at the endplates): The rugger jersey spine reflects high turnover renal osteodystrophy: phosphate retention and low calcitriol drive secondary hyperparathyroidism, and osteoblasts lay down thickened trabeculae at the endplates. Aluminium bone disease causes adynamic osteomalacia. Osteopetrosis and sclerotic metastases are separate entities without the symmetric banded pattern.
  99. The correct answer is B (Wedge-shaped subchondral infarct with its base against the articular surface): Osteonecrosis is ischaemic death of bone and marrow, producing a triangular or wedge-shaped infarct beneath the cartilage with the apex toward the centre of the head. The double line rim is the reactive interface. Subchondral collapse gives the crescent. Osteoarthritis, osteomyelitis and Ewing sarcoma give different gross findings.
  100. The correct answer is C (Creeping substitution of necrotic trabeculae by new viable bone): In idiopathic avascular necrosis of the capital femoral epiphysis, dead bone is slowly resorbed by osteoclasts and replaced by new bone laid on the old trabeculae, termed creeping substitution. Cartilage does not regenerate the epiphysis. Fibrous replacement is not typical, and an involucrum is a feature of infection.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Sluggish venous flow in looping metaphyseal capillaries beside the physis allows bacterial seeding): In children, nutrient artery branches form terminal loops with slow flow beneath the physis, and endothelial gaps permit bacteria to lodge, typically Staphylococcus aureus. The physis restricts spread to the epiphysis in older infants. The other options misstate cortical, oxygen or physeal factors.
  2. The correct answer is C (Salmonella species infecting bone infarcts from repeated vaso-occlusion): Sickle cell disease causes functional asplenia and bone infarcts, which are dead tissue that Salmonella can colonise. Staphylococcus aureus is still the most common cause overall. Pseudomonas, Kingella and pneumococcus are tied to other settings.
  3. The correct answer is B (Subacute localised pyogenic osteomyelitis contained by reactive bone formation): A Brodie abscess is a walled-off intraosseous infection, usually Staphylococcus aureus, in which host response limits spread. The transphyseal channel is a recognised sign. Osteoid osteoma has a nidus smaller than 1.5 cm made of osteoid, and Ewing sarcoma has an ill-defined margin without a contained pus cavity.
  4. The correct answer is A (Absence of proteolytic enzymes from the organism, unlike pyogenic bacteria): Mycobacterium tuberculosis lacks the proteolytic enzymes of pyogenic bacteria, so the disc stays relatively intact until late, in contrast to bacterial discitis where the disc is destroyed early. Subligamentous spread occurs and contributes to anterior scalloping, but does not explain disc sparing. Caseating granulomas drive the destruction of bone.
  5. The correct answer is D (Mosaic pattern of lamellar bone formed by prominent haphazard cement lines): Paget disease of bone shows excessive osteoclastic resorption followed by chaotic osteoblastic repair, leaving thick, disorganised lamellar bone with a mosaic of cement lines. Alkaline phosphatase is raised and calcium usually normal. Fibrous dysplasia and nonossifying fibroma have different matrices, and osteomalacia has thickened osteoid.
  6. The correct answer is C (Bony overgrowth narrowing the internal auditory foramina and compressing the cochlear nerve): Paget disease thickens the skull base and can narrow foramina, compressing the eighth cranial nerve, and may also affect the ossicles. The mixed lytic and sclerotic skull pattern reflects the transition between the active resorptive and sclerotic phases. Otosclerosis, metastases and cholesteatoma do not account for the calvarial changes.
  7. The correct answer is B (Interlacing osteoid and woven bone within a vascular fibrous stroma): The lucent focus is a nidus, a small vascular lesion of osteoid and woven bone ringed by reactive sclerosis. Prostaglandin production within the nidus sensitises nerve endings, which explains night pain relieved by NSAIDs. The other histologies belong to different tumours with different clinical patterns.
  8. The correct answer is C (Size above 2 cm with progressive growth and a tendency to occur in the spine): Osteoblastoma and osteoid osteoma are histologically similar, so the discriminating features are lesion size (above 1.5 to 2 cm), growth potential and a predilection for the posterior spine, with less reactive sclerosis and little response to NSAIDs. Cartilage caps and epiphyseal location belong to other lesions.
  9. The correct answer is B (Cartilage cap, with malignant transformation to chondrosarcoma when thick in adulthood): Osteochondromas have a hyaline cartilage cap, which grows by endochondral ossification and arises from displaced physeal tissue, associated with EXT1 or EXT2 changes in hereditary multiple exostoses. A cap exceeding about 2 cm in an adult raises concern for secondary chondrosarcoma. The other options misstate tissue and tumour type.
  10. The correct answer is B (IDH1 or IDH2 mutation producing the oncometabolite 2-hydroxyglutarate): Ollier disease and Maffucci syndrome (with haemangiomas) are non-hereditary multiple enchondromatosis, driven by mosaic IDH1 or IDH2 mutations, as are many solitary enchondromas and chondrosarcomas. EXT genes, GNAS and H3F3A each define different bone lesions.
  11. The correct answer is D (Permeation of marrow fat with entrapment of host trabeculae): Distinguishing enchondroma from low grade chondrosarcoma depends on invasive growth, so permeation of marrow spaces and trabecular entrapment, along with deep endosteal scalloping and cortical thickening, favour malignancy. Cartilage caps, storiform spindle cells and small round cells indicate other diagnoses.
  12. The correct answer is B (Malignant spindle cells that directly produce osteoid matrix): Osteosarcoma is defined by neoplastic cells producing osteoid, even if cartilage or fibrous areas coexist. Codman triangle and sunburst reaction are imaging hallmarks of aggressive periosteal response. Small round cell and giant cell morphologies suggest other diagnoses.
  13. The correct answer is B (Loss of function of the RB1 tumour suppressor gene): Hereditary retinoblastoma reflects germline RB1 loss, and survivors have a markedly raised risk of osteosarcoma, particularly in irradiated fields. Li-Fraumeni syndrome with TP53 mutation is another association. The EWSR1 fusion, APC and NF1 are linked to other tumours.
  14. The correct answer is A (t(11;22) generating an EWSR1::FLI1 fusion gene in the tumour cells): Ewing sarcoma is defined by EWSR1 fusion with an ETS family gene, usually FLI1 in about 85 percent of cases. Diaphyseal location, onion-skin periosteal reaction, fever and CD99 positivity support this. The other translocations define other sarcomas, including a small round cell mimic in alveolar rhabdomyosarcoma.
  15. The correct answer is A (Mononuclear stromal cells that express RANKL and carry H3F3A mutation): The osteoclast-like giant cells are reactive; the neoplastic cells are mononuclear stromal cells with H3F3A G34W mutation that secrete RANKL, recruiting the giant cells. This is the basis for denosumab therapy. Epiphyseal location after physeal closure is classical.
  16. The correct answer is C (Polygonal chondroblasts with grooved nuclei and chicken-wire calcification): Chondroblastoma is an epiphyseal tumour of adolescents composed of chondroblasts with coffee-bean nuclei, with fine pericellular calcification giving a chicken-wire look. H3F3B K36M mutation is typical. Histiocytic cells with eosinophils indicate Langerhans cell histiocytosis.
  17. The correct answer is B (Diffuse sclerotic foci arising from osteoblastic response to tumour cells): Prostate carcinoma classically gives osteoblastic (sclerotic) deposits through tumour-derived factors such as endothelin 1 and Wnt agonists, and vertebral spread occurs via Batson paravertebral venous plexus. Lung and renal cancers are usually lytic, and renal deposits can be expansile.
  18. The correct answer is C (Plasma cell cytokines activating osteoclasts and suppressing osteoblast differentiation): Myeloma cells secrete RANKL, MIP-1 alpha and Dickkopf-1, increasing osteoclast activity and inhibiting osteoblast activity, so lesions are purely lytic with little reactive bone, which explains a normal bone scan. Osteoid production, PTHrP and caseation are unrelated.
  19.
