The correct answer is C (Postzygotic activating mutation of GNAS affecting the Gs alpha subunit): McCune-Albright syndrome is caused by mosaic activating GNAS mutation, producing polyostotic fibrous dysplasia, endocrine hyperfunction and café-au-lait pigmentation. Histology shows irregular woven bone trabeculae in a fibrous stroma, without osteoblastic rimming, often described as Chinese-letter shapes.
  20. The correct answer is B (Clear straw-coloured fluid from a cyst with a thin fibrous lining): A unicameral (simple) bone cyst is a fluid-filled single cavity with a thin fibrous lining, centred in the metaphysis and near the physis. The fallen fragment sign, where a fractured cortical piece drops to the cyst base, is nearly diagnostic. Aneurysmal bone cysts contain blood and show septation.
  21. The correct answer is B (USP6 rearrangement driving overexpression of a ubiquitin-specific protease): Primary aneurysmal bone cyst is a neoplasm with recurrent USP6 rearrangement, commonly with CDH11 or other partners. The cysts are blood-filled spaces separated by septa containing fibroblasts, giant cells and reactive woven bone. Secondary cysts arise within other lesions, such as giant cell tumour.
  22. The correct answer is A (Bland spindle cells in a storiform pattern with giant cells and foam cells): Nonossifying fibroma (fibrous cortical defect when small) is a common developmental lesion of children, often with KRAS or FGFR1 mutations, and usually regresses spontaneously. Histology shows storiform spindle cells with multinucleated giant cells and haemosiderin or foam cells.
  23. The correct answer is A (CD1a and langerin positivity with Birbeck granules on electron microscopy): Langerhans cell histiocytosis consists of clonal dendritic cells with BRAF V600E mutation in many cases, expressing S100, CD1a and langerin, with tennis-racket Birbeck granules ultrastructurally. Eosinophils accompany the cells. Ewing sarcoma and myeloma have distinct markers.
  24. The correct answer is B (Nuclear brachyury expression together with cytokeratin positivity in the cells): Chordoma arises from notochordal remnants in the sacrum, clivus or mobile spine, showing physaliferous (bubbly) cells and brachyury positivity, a defining marker. It is cytokeratin and S100 positive. Typical histology distinguishes it from chondrosarcoma, which is brachyury negative.
  25. The correct answer is A (Marrow space obliteration by retained primary spongiosa from failed osteoclast resorption): Osteopetrosis results from osteoclast dysfunction, commonly impaired acid secretion, leaving unresorbed calcified cartilage and bone that crowd the marrow. This leads to anaemia, extramedullary haematopoiesis and cranial nerve compression by narrowed foramina. The other options produce different patterns.
  26. The correct answer is C (Mature peripheral bone surrounding a cellular, immature central zone): In myositis ossificans circumscripta the zonal phenomenon places the immature fibroblastic area centrally and the mature lamellar bone at the periphery, reflected by peripheral rim calcification. Osteosarcoma shows the reverse, with central mineralisation and a pleomorphic periphery.
  27. The correct answer is B (Amplification of MDM2 and CDK4 on chromosome 12q): Parosteal osteosarcoma is a low grade surface osteosarcoma of the posterior distal femur, with well-differentiated fibroblastic stroma and bony trabeculae. MDM2 amplification helps distinguish it from benign fibro-osseous lesions. Dedifferentiation to high grade sarcoma is the complication.
  28. The correct answer is D (Macrophages with wrinkled paper cytoplasm from glucocerebroside storage): Gaucher disease is a lysosomal storage disorder from glucocerebrosidase deficiency, and engorged macrophages with fibrillar, wrinkled cytoplasm accumulate in marrow, causing remodelling defects (Erlenmeyer flask), infarcts and avascular necrosis. The other options describe different marrow infiltrates.
  29. The correct answer is B (Squamous cell carcinoma arising in the epithelium lining the sinus tract): Long-standing sinus tracts from chronic osteomyelitis can develop squamous cell carcinoma, termed a Marjolin ulcer when arising in chronic wounds. It is usually well differentiated and slow to metastasise. Sarcoma and lymphoma are not classically linked to chronic infection of bone.
  30. The correct answer is C (Progressive fibrillation and loss of articular cartilage with failed chondrocyte repair): Osteoarthritis is primarily a disease of articular cartilage: surface fibrillation, loss of proteoglycan and failed repair thin the cartilage, producing asymmetric loaded-compartment narrowing. Sclerosis and osteophytes are the bone response. Pannus erosion would give uniform narrowing with marginal erosions, urate would give tophi and punched out erosions, and osteonecrosis begins in bone with subchondral collapse.
  31. The correct answer is B (Synovial fluid forced through cartilage defects into bone under load, with focal myxoid change): Subchondral cysts, also called geodes, arise when synovial fluid is driven through breaches in the cartilage into subchondral bone, and from focal bone necrosis with myxoid change. They sit under the sclerotic loaded surface and communicate with the joint. Gout and rheumatoid disease produce marginal erosions, and brown tumours need a systemic parathyroid disorder with other resorptive changes.
  32. The correct answer is B (Villous hyperplasia with dense lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate and germinal centre formation): Rheumatoid synovium shows lining cell hyperplasia, villous architecture and a dense chronic infiltrate of T cells, B cells and plasma cells that can form follicles with germinal centres. This cellular, hypervascular tissue accounts for avid enhancement. Haemosiderin and giant cells indicate tenosynovial giant cell tumour, cartilage nodules indicate chondromatosis, and neutrophilic exudate points to infection.
  33. The correct answer is C (Inflamed synovium lies directly on bone where no cartilage covers it): Marginal or bare area erosions occur where the synovial membrane lies directly on bone within the joint capsule, outside the cartilage covering. Pannus enzymes and osteoclast activation destroy bone there first. Mechanical overload is the osteoarthritis story, crystal deposition belongs to gout, and entheseal new bone formation is typical of the seronegative spondyloarthropathies.
  34. The correct answer is B (Enthesis and periosteum centred inflammation with new bone formation): Psoriatic arthritis combines erosive change with enthesitis and periostitis, so bone destruction coexists with fluffy new bone formation, and bone density is typically preserved. Pencil-in-cup deformity results from erosion with adjacent proliferation. Rheumatoid disease gives erosion with osteopenia, gout gives overhanging edges, and infection is usually monoarticular and does not follow the skin disease.
  35. The correct answer is D (The enthesis, where outer annulus fibres insert into the vertebral body): Spondyloarthropathies are enthesitis driven diseases. Inflammation at the annulus insertion leads to erosion at the vertebral corner, then ossification of the outer annulus, producing marginal syndesmophytes. DISH instead shows thick flowing ossification of the anterior longitudinal ligament with preserved discs and no sacroiliac erosions.
  36. The correct answer is A (Amorphous urate deposits surrounded by a foreign body granulomatous reaction): A tophus is a deposit of monosodium urate surrounded by macrophages, giant cells and fibrous tissue, which erodes adjacent bone while preserving the joint space until late. The overhanging edge is characteristic. CPPD affects cartilage, pigmented synovitis contains haemosiderin, and rheumatoid nodules show central necrosis with palisading histiocytes.
  37. The correct answer is B (Calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate crystals deposited within hyaline and fibrocartilage): Calcium pyrophosphate crystals are rhomboid or rod shaped and weakly positively birefringent, whereas urate crystals are needle shaped and negatively birefringent. Chondrocalcinosis in the triangular fibrocartilage supports the diagnosis. Hydroxyapatite is not seen under polarised light and cholesterol crystals appear as notched plates.
  38. The correct answer is C (Involvement of the radiocarpal and patellofemoral compartments, usually spared in idiopathic disease): CPPD arthropathy resembles osteoarthritis but affects unusual sites: the radiocarpal joint, second and third metacarpophalangeal joints and the patellofemoral compartment, often with prominent cysts and chondrocalcinosis. Osteophytes and sclerosis are shared features. Primary osteoarthritis favours the hand DIP joints, thumb base and loaded knee compartments.
  39. The correct answer is A (Neutrophil and bacterial proteases digesting cartilage as pus raises intracapsular pressure): In septic arthritis, neutrophil proteases, matrix metalloproteinases and bacterial toxins degrade cartilage within days, and raised intracapsular pressure in the infant hip can compromise epiphyseal perfusion, leading to dislocation and growth disturbance. Pannus and haemosiderin require a chronic course, and vascular compromise is a contributor rather than the sole cause.
  40. The correct answer is C (Periarticular osteopenia, peripheral erosions and gradual joint space narrowing): Tuberculous arthritis is granulomatous with caseating necrosis, so it progresses slowly. The classic Phemister triad is osteopenia, peripheral erosions and gradually narrowing joint space. Pyogenic infection destroys cartilage quickly, while osteoarthritis, CPPD and gout have a crystal or degenerative basis.
  41. The correct answer is D (Haemosiderin in macrophages and stromal cells within a proliferating synovial mass): Tenosynovial giant cell tumour, formerly pigmented villonodular synovitis, is a neoplasm driven by CSF1 rearrangement, and contains haemosiderin laden macrophages, foam cells and multinucleate giant cells. Haemosiderin causes marked susceptibility effect, hence blooming. Fat, calcification and myxoid change give different signal behaviours.
  42. The correct answer is A (Cartilaginous metaplasia of subsynovial tissue forming nodules that may calcify): Primary synovial chondromatosis arises when subsynovial cells undergo cartilaginous metaplasia, forming numerous nodules of similar size that may calcify or ossify and detach as loose bodies. Secondary loose bodies from osteoarthritis are fewer and more variable in size. Myositis ossificans is intramuscular, and haemosiderin points to giant cell tumour.
  43. The correct answer is D (Absent pain sensation and autonomic dysregulation allowing microtrauma and hyperaemic resorption): Charcot arthropathy follows neuropathy: absent pain sensation permits repeated trauma, and autonomic dysfunction increases blood flow and osteoclast activity, so bone fragments, joints sublux and debris accumulates, with sclerosis from repair. Infection must be considered separately, but the picture reflects neuropathic change rather than inflammatory or crystal disease.
  44. The correct answer is D (Iron from repeated bleeds triggering synovial hypertrophy and cytokine mediated chondrocyte death): Repeated haemarthroses deposit iron in synovium, stimulating hypertrophy and inflammatory cytokines that kill chondrocytes. Hyperaemia during growth causes epiphyseal enlargement and a widened notch. Mechanical, infective and crystal disorders do not produce this combination in a young person with a coagulopathy.
  45. The correct answer is A (Amplification of MDM2 and CDK4 on chromosome 12q13 to 15): Well-differentiated liposarcoma, also called atypical lipomatous tumour, harbours ring or giant marker chromosomes producing MDM2 and CDK4 amplification, which is used on biopsy to separate it from lipoma. Lipomas often carry HMGA2 rearrangement. Thick septa, nodularity and deep location raise suspicion, but molecular testing confirms.
  46. The correct answer is B (Translocation t(12;16) producing a FUS-DDIT3 fusion in the neoplastic cells): Myxoid liposarcoma is characterised by t(12;16) FUS-DDIT3 fusion, a chicken wire capillary pattern and lipoblasts in myxoid stroma. The cystic appearance on MRI can mislead, and metastases favour fat-bearing sites such as retroperitoneum and bone. The other fusions belong to different sarcomas.
  47. The correct answer is D (Dedifferentiated liposarcoma with an adjacent well-differentiated lipogenic component): Dedifferentiated liposarcoma is a well-differentiated component adjacent to a distinct non-lipogenic sarcoma, often high-grade, and usually retroperitoneal. The sudden transition between fat and solid enhancing tissue is the key imaging and pathological clue, and prognosis is worse than for the well-differentiated component alone.
  48. The correct answer is D (Activating postzygotic mutation of the GNAS gene on chromosome 20): Intramuscular myxomas contain sparse spindle cells in abundant myxoid stroma, hence fluid-like signal with a fat rim. Multiple myxomas with fibrous dysplasia constitute Mazabraud syndrome, caused by a postzygotic activating GNAS mutation. The other genes are tied to unrelated soft tissue tumours.
  49. The correct answer is D (Cellular Antoni A areas centrally with myxoid Antoni B areas at the periphery): Schwannomas are encapsulated, eccentric to the parent nerve, and composed of cellular Antoni A and loose, myxoid Antoni B zones. T2 signal is lower centrally in cellular tissue and higher peripherally, creating the target sign. Neurofibromas incorporate the nerve fibres rather than displacing them.
  50. The correct answer is B (It is pathognomonic for NF1 and carries a risk of malignant transformation): Plexiform neurofibroma is a diffuse expansion of nerve fascicles and is virtually specific for NF1. The central low signal dot corresponds to fibrous tissue. Lifetime risk of malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumour is increased, and rapid growth, pain or loss of the target sign should prompt concern.
  51. The correct answer is C (Translocation t(X;18) producing an SS18-SSX fusion gene in tumour cells): Synovial sarcoma, despite its name, does not arise from synovium and commonly occurs near joints in young adults. Biphasic tumours contain spindle and epithelial cells and carry t(X;18) SS18-SSX1, 2 or 4. The triple signal arises from solid, haemorrhagic and cystic components. Other fusions identify different sarcomas.
  52. The correct answer is D (It recurs locally after excision but does not metastasise): Desmoid-type fibromatosis is a clonal myofibroblastic proliferation often driven by CTNNB1 mutation or APC loss in familial polyposis. It infiltrates locally, may follow trauma or surgery, and recurs, but it never metastasises. Many are now observed initially because some stabilise or regress.
  53. The correct answer is C (Mature bone peripherally with immature fibroblastic tissue centrally): Myositis ossificans matures from the outside inwards, so the periphery contains lamellar bone and the centre remains immature, cellular and lucent. This zoning distinguishes it from extraskeletal osteosarcoma, where mineralisation is densest centrally. Timing after trauma and separation from the cortex also support benignity.
  54. The correct answer is A (A reactive pseudotumour with abnormal elastic fibres from repetitive friction): Elastofibroma dorsi is a benign reactive fibroelastic lesion at the inferior scapular angle, often bilateral, in older people with repetitive shoulder movements. Histology shows thick, abnormal elastic fibres. The typical site, muscle-like signal and fatty streaks prevent unnecessary biopsy.
  55. The correct answer is C (Resorptive phase, with macrophage removal of hydroxyapatite deposits): Calcific tendinitis is cell-mediated deposition of carbonate apatite in a formative phase, followed by a painful resorptive phase when vascular ingrowth and phagocytosis dissolve the deposit, giving a fluffy, ill-defined appearance. Symptoms are worst in this phase and may include bursal rupture.
  56. The correct answer is C (Disordered collagen, excess ground substance and new vessels with few inflammatory cells): Tendinosis is a failed healing response: collagen fibres lose their orderly arrangement, mucoid ground substance accumulates, tenocytes proliferate and new vessels grow, with minimal inflammatory cells. This degeneration accounts for intrasubstance signal on short echo time sequences and tendon thickening.
  57. The correct answer is B (Absent dystrophin causing sarcolemmal fragility, repeated necrosis and replacement by fat): Duchenne dystrophy results from dystrophin deficiency, destabilising the sarcolemma so fibres undergo repeated necrosis and regeneration until fat and collagen replace them. This pseudohypertrophy of calves appears enlarged because of fatty replacement. Selective sparing of sartorius, gracilis and semitendinosus is a recognised pattern.
  58. The correct answer is A (Perifascicular atrophy with perivascular inflammation from microvascular injury): Dermatomyositis is a complement mediated microangiopathy: capillary injury produces perifascicular atrophy, with perivascular lymphocytes and B cells. The skin signs, subcutaneous oedema and fascial calcification in the later disease also reflect vascular injury, and there is an association with malignancy in adults.
  59. The correct answer is C (Expanded extracellular water and capillary volume in denervated fibres before atrophy): Within days of denervation, muscle shows increased T2 signal from enlarged extracellular and vascular spaces, with normal T1 signal and preserved bulk. Atrophy and fatty replacement develop over months if the nerve does not recover. This timing helps separate denervation from ischaemia or haematoma.
  60. The correct answer is A (Distension of a communicating bursa, usually secondary to a chronic knee effusion): A Baker cyst is a distended gastrocnemius-semimembranosus bursa that communicates with the joint through a posteromedial capsular gap, commonly in osteoarthritis or inflammatory arthritis. The neck between the tendons is characteristic. Rupture can mimic deep venous thrombosis.
  61. The correct answer is C (Lined by flattened connective tissue cells without a true synovial lining): Ganglia are mucin filled cysts arising from myxoid degeneration of capsule, ligament or tendon sheath, and lack a true epithelial or synovial lining. They often connect to the joint through a stalk and recur after simple aspiration. The other options describe different cystic or nodular lesions.
  62. The correct answer is A (Thickness from the granular layer to the deepest invasive cell, with ulceration status): Melanoma T category is defined by Breslow thickness, measured from the granular layer of the epidermis to the deepest invasive tumour cell, together with ulceration. A 3.2 mm ulcerated tumour is T3b. Clark level and diameter are not used to assign T category, and sentinel node status guides nodal staging.
  63. The correct answer is A (COL1A1-PDGFB fusion arising from translocation t(17;22) in the tumour): Dermatofibrosarcoma protuberans is a low-grade cutaneous sarcoma that infiltrates fat in a honeycomb pattern, with tentacle-like extensions on MRI that explain its high local recurrence after narrow excision. The COL1A1-PDGFB fusion drives PDGF signalling and is responsive to imatinib.
  64. The correct answer is B (Basophilic cells and shadow cells with calcification from hair matrix differentiation): Pilomatrixoma is a benign tumour of hair matrix cells, usually in children and young adults on the head and neck, with CTNNB1 mutation. Basophilic cells transform into ghost cells that calcify, accounting for the shadowing hyperechoic nodule. Epidermal cysts, basal cell carcinoma and DFSP differ histologically.
  65. The correct answer is A (Merkel cell polyomavirus integration with immunosuppression in older adults): Merkel cell carcinoma is a neuroendocrine skin carcinoma arising on sun-damaged skin, linked to clonal integration of Merkel cell polyomavirus and to immunosuppression and ultraviolet exposure. Perinuclear dot CK20 staining supports the diagnosis. It spreads early to regional nodes, so nodal imaging is central.
  66. The correct answer is A (Chronic microvascular injury with fibrosis, ischaemia and dystrophic calcification): Systemic sclerosis causes endothelial injury, obliterative vasculopathy and excess collagen deposition. Ischaemic or damaged tissue develops dystrophic calcification, giving subcutaneous calcinosis, and distal tufts resorb. Serum calcium is normal. Gout and rheumatoid arthritis lack the skin tightening and acro-osteolysis.
  67. The correct answer is C (Normal sacroiliac joints with thick flowing ligamentous ossification and intact discs): Diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis shows thick flowing ossification of the anterior longitudinal ligament across four or more levels, usually with preserved disc height and normal sacroiliac and facet joints. Ankylosing spondylitis has sacroiliitis, thin syndesmophytes and often HLA-B27. Degenerative disease has narrowed discs.
  68. The correct answer is A (Rupture of small penetrating arteries weakened by lipohyalinosis and microaneurysm formation at branch points): Chronic hypertension damages deep penetrating lenticulostriate arteries, causing lipohyalinosis and Charcot-Bouchard microaneurysms that rupture in the putamen, thalamus, pons and cerebellum. A saccular aneurysm bleeds mainly into the subarachnoid space. Haemorrhagic transformation needs a preceding infarct. Amyloid angiopathy favours lobar cortical and subcortical bleeds in older patients.
  69. The correct answer is D (Amyloid beta deposition in the media and adventitia of cortical and leptomeningeal small arteries): Cerebral amyloid angiopathy deposits beta amyloid in small cortical and leptomeningeal vessels, giving lobar haemorrhage and peripheral cortical microbleeds with sparing of the deep nuclei. Hypertensive arteriopathy produces deep haemorrhage. Fibromuscular dysplasia involves larger extracranial and intracranial arteries. A cavernous malformation is a single or familial multiple lesion with popcorn appearance.
  70. The correct answer is A (Energy failure with sodium pump dysfunction causing intracellular water shift and cytotoxic oedema): Within minutes of arterial occlusion, ATP depletion halts the sodium potassium pump, so water enters cells and restricts diffusion. Vasogenic oedema from barrier breakdown develops later with increased diffusion. Neutrophils arrive after about a day. Macrophages dominate from several days onward.
  71. The correct answer is A (Autosomal dominant polycystic disease from PKD1 or PKD2 mutation): Berry aneurysms cluster in autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease, where the kidneys are enlarged and cystic. Vascular Ehlers-Danlos weakens arteries but does not give enlarged cystic kidneys. Tuberous sclerosis and VHL both affect the kidney yet are not linked with saccular aneurysm rupture.
  72. The correct answer is A (Delayed arterial narrowing from smooth muscle contraction and intimal changes after blood breakdown products): Subarachnoid blood releases spasmogens that cause arterial vasospasm peaking between days 4 and 10, with smooth muscle contraction and later intimal thickening, producing delayed ischaemic deficits. Haematoma related oedema is a different entity, sinus thrombosis is uncommon, and salt wasting causes diffuse swelling rather than a vascular territory syndrome.
  73. The correct answer is B (Tear of the middle meningeal artery where it runs beneath thin temporal bone): A temporal fracture lacerates the middle meningeal artery, and arterial pressure strips dura from bone to make a biconvex collection limited by suture attachments. The classic lucid interval follows. Bridging veins cause subdural crescents. Sinus tears are less common, and pial contusion bleeding is intra-axial.
  74. The correct answer is A (Stretched bridging veins crossing a widened subdural space as the brain shrinks): Age related volume loss widens the subdural space and stretches bridging veins, which tear after minor trauma, especially with anticoagulation. The collection crosses sutures but not dural reflections. An epidural arterial bleed is lens shaped. Deep penetrating artery and aneurysmal bleeds are intraparenchymal and subarachnoid respectively.
  75. The correct answer is A (Rotational acceleration and deceleration shearing axons, producing axonal stretch and disconnection): Diffuse axonal injury follows rotational forces that shear axons at density interfaces, so lesions sit at the grey-white junction, corpus callosum and dorsolateral brainstem, often with small haemorrhages and beta amyloid precursor protein positive swellings. Contusions occur on frontal and temporal surfaces. Herniation and watershed ischaemia need mass effect or hypotension.
  76. The correct answer is C (Brain movement over rough bony surfaces of the anterior and middle cranial fossae): Contusions are bruises of the gyral crests that occur where the moving brain meets rough bone, namely the orbitofrontal surfaces and temporal poles, and they often blossom after an initially normal scan. Shear injury is deeper. Sinus thrombosis and vasospasm do not match the history.
  77. The correct answer is D (Fibrosis of the arachnoid and leptomeninges impairing cerebrospinal fluid absorption after purulent exudate): A communicating hydrocephalus results when purulent exudate organises into leptomeningeal fibrosis that blocks absorption at the arachnoid granulations, so all ventricles dilate including the fourth. Aqueduct stenosis spares the fourth ventricle. Sinus thrombosis and empyema do not fit the leptomeningeal thickening and symmetrical dilatation.
  78. The correct answer is B (Poorer blood supply to deep white matter slows fibroblast and granulation tissue formation): A capsule forms as fibroblasts and new vessels wall off the cerebritis. Cortex is better vascularised than deep white matter, so the capsule is thicker laterally and thinner towards the ventricle, which is why abscesses tend to rupture into the ventricle. The other options invoke mechanisms that do not account for the asymmetry.
  79. The correct answer is A (Necrotising inflammation with intranuclear eosinophilic Cowdry type A inclusions in neurons and glia): Herpes simplex encephalitis causes haemorrhagic necrosis of the inferomedial temporal lobes, insula and orbitofrontal cortex, with Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions. Giant cell microglial nodules suggest HIV, Negri bodies suggest rabies, and owl's eye inclusions suggest cytomegalovirus.
  80. The correct answer is D (Necrotising abscesses with tachyzoites and encysted bradyzoites in the adjacent viable brain): Toxoplasma forms multiple basal ganglia and corticomedullary abscesses with central necrosis, and tachyzoites and cysts at the periphery. Primary CNS lymphoma is typically solitary and homogeneously enhancing. Septic emboli require a cardiac source. JC virus lesions are non-enhancing white matter plaques.
  81. The correct answer is D (Oligodendrocytes infected by polyomavirus JC, leading to loss of myelin): JC virus infects oligodendrocytes, causing asymmetric demyelination of subcortical white matter, with enlarged oligodendroglial nuclei and bizarre astrocytes, and typically no mass effect or enhancement. HIV, cytomegalovirus and varicella zoster each produce a distinct imaging and pathological pattern.
  82. The correct answer is D (Thick gelatinous granulomatous exudate around the basal cisterns with caseation and obliterative arteritis): Tuberculous meningitis forms a basal exudate that entraps cranial nerves, obstructs cerebrospinal fluid flow and causes arteritis with infarcts. Pyogenic meningitis favours the convexities. Neurosarcoidosis has non-caseating granulomas. Viral meningitis lacks a gelatinous basal exudate.
  83. The correct answer is C (Accumulation of protease resistant misfolded prion protein with vacuolation of neuropil and neuronal loss): Sporadic Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease causes spongiform vacuolation, astrogliosis and neuronal loss, with protease resistant prion protein, and cortical ribboning on diffusion MRI. Tau tangles, viral inclusions and Lewy bodies are slower, have different distributions and do not produce spongiform change.
  84. The correct answer is B (Sharply demarcated plaques of perivenular demyelination with macrophages and relative axonal preservation): Multiple sclerosis plaques centre on small veins, perpendicular to the ventricles as Dawson fingers, with myelin loss, macrophages and relative axonal preservation early on. Vasculitis, trauma and JC virus infection give different patterns.
  85. The correct answer is C (Aquaporin 4 water channel on astrocytic end feet, causing complement mediated astrocyte injury): Neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder is an astrocytopathy with antibodies to aquaporin 4 on astrocyte end feet, causing longitudinally extensive myelitis, optic neuritis and area postrema syndrome. MOG antibody disease and GFAP astrocytopathy are separate entities, and myelin basic protein is not the recognised target.
  86. The correct answer is B (Oligodendrocyte loss and myelin breakdown with relative preservation of axons and no inflammation): Rapid correction of chronic hyponatraemia damages oligodendrocytes, giving non-inflammatory myelin loss in the central pons, with preserved axons. Wernicke encephalopathy is the confusable alcohol related lesion but involves mammillary bodies and periaqueductal grey. Multiple sclerosis is inflammatory and perivenular.
  87. The correct answer is D (Sequential cleavage of amyloid precursor protein by beta and gamma secretase producing aggregation prone peptide): Presenilin is the catalytic subunit of gamma secretase. Cleavage of amyloid precursor protein by beta then gamma secretase yields the Abeta peptide that aggregates into plaques. Alpha secretase cleavage is non-amyloidogenic. Tau hyperphosphorylation gives neurofibrillary tangles, and prion misfolding gives spongiform change.
  88. The correct answer is A (Intraneuronal alpha synuclein inclusions in brainstem, limbic and neocortical neurons): Dementia with Lewy bodies shows Lewy bodies and Lewy neurites of alpha synuclein in brainstem, limbic and neocortical regions, often with coexisting Alzheimer pathology. Pick bodies, hippocampal plaques and spongiform change belong to other dementias with different clinical syndromes.
  89. The correct answer is A (TAR DNA binding protein 43 mislocalised from the nucleus into the cytoplasm): C9orf72 expansion causes frontotemporal lobar degeneration with motor neuron disease, and the inclusions are TDP-43 positive, with the protein cleared from the nucleus. Tau, alpha synuclein and SOD1 aggregates define other disorders.
  90. The correct answer is B (CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion on chromosome 4 with caudate and putamen atrophy): Huntington disease is an autosomal dominant CAG repeat disorder of the huntingtin gene on chromosome 4, showing anticipation especially with paternal transmission, and striatal atrophy that flattens the frontal horns. The other options describe fragile X, Friedreich ataxia and familial Alzheimer disease.
  91. The correct answer is A (TERT promoter mutation, present in an IDH wild type diffuse astrocytic glioma): CNS5 classes an IDH wild type diffuse astrocytic glioma as glioblastoma, grade 4, when it has necrosis or microvascular proliferation, or one of TERT promoter mutation, EGFR amplification, or gain of chromosome 7 with loss of chromosome 10. The other findings relate to prognosis or to different tumour types.
  92. The correct answer is D (Grade 4 astrocytoma, IDH mutant, on the basis of the CDKN2A/B deletion): In CNS5, IDH mutant diffuse astrocytomas are graded 2 to 4 within one entity, and homozygous CDKN2A/B deletion assigns grade 4 even without necrosis. The tumour keeps its IDH mutant astrocytoma label and is not reclassified as IDH wild type glioblastoma.
  93. The correct answer is D (IDH mutation with whole arm codeletion of chromosomes 1p and 19q): WHO CNS5 defines oligodendroglioma as IDH mutant with 1p/19q codeletion, typically with calcification and chicken wire capillaries. Wild type TERT mutant tumours are glioblastoma. BRAF alterations suit circumscribed gliomas. ATRX loss with TP53 mutation marks astrocytoma, mutually exclusive with codeletion.
  94. The correct answer is A (KIAA1549 and BRAF gene fusion leading to MAPK pathway activation): Pilocytic astrocytoma, a grade 1 tumour, usually carries a KIAA1549-BRAF fusion activating MAPK signalling, and shows Rosenthal fibres and eosinophilic granular bodies. MYC amplification, IDH1 mutation and VHL loss characterise other tumours.
  95. The correct answer is D (This WNT activated group has CTNNB1 mutation and the most favourable outcome): Nuclear beta-catenin with CTNNB1 mutation identifies the WNT activated medulloblastoma, which has the best prognosis and often arises near the cerebellopontine angle or fourth ventricle. Group 3 has MYC amplification and poor outcome, SHH tumours have PTCH1 alterations, and group 4 has isochromosome 17q.
  96. The correct answer is C (Elongated tumour cells radially arranged around vascular cores with mucoid perivascular degeneration): Myxopapillary ependymoma arises from the filum terminale, forming papillary arrangements of cuboidal to elongated cells around hyalinised, mucin-rich vascular cores, now graded 2 in CNS5. Schwannoma, meningioma and haemangioblastoma can all enhance but have separate histology.
  97. The correct answer is C (Loss of the NF2 tumour suppressor gene on chromosome 22q): Around half of sporadic meningiomas have NF2 inactivation with monosomy 22, and radiation is a recognised risk factor. EGFR amplification, IDH1 mutation and VHL deletion belong to glioblastoma, diffuse glioma and haemangioblastoma.
  98. The correct answer is D (Dense sheets of large atypical B cells with angiocentric growth around vessels): Primary CNS lymphoma is usually diffuse large B cell lymphoma, with dense cellularity, restricted diffusion and homogeneous enhancement, and cells cuffing vessels. Steroid responsiveness is a clue. Glioblastoma, tuberculoma and ependymoma differ in histology and enhancement.
  99. The correct answer is B (Alternating cellular Antoni A and loose Antoni B areas with S100 positive spindle cells): Schwannoma is encapsulated and shows Antoni A and B areas with Verocay bodies, strong S100 positivity, and NF2 inactivation. Meningioma, neurofibroma and malignant nerve sheath tumour differ in architecture and immunophenotype.
  100. The correct answer is B (Malignant transformation into a malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumour): Plexiform neurofibroma is nearly specific for neurofibromatosis type 1 and carries a lifetime risk of malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumour, suggested by rapid growth, pain and loss of target sign. Dural ectasia and vestibular schwannoma belong to different disease processes.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Foamy stromal cells set within a dense capillary network, associated with VHL loss): Haemangioblastoma shows lipid-rich stromal cells in a rich capillary network, may secrete erythropoietin, and is associated with von Hippel-Lindau disease. Rosenthal fibres, Homer Wright rosettes and ependymal rosettes characterise the other cerebellar tumours.
  2. The correct answer is A (Cavity interrupting spinothalamic fibres crossing the anterior white commissure in the central cord): A syrinx associated with Chiari I malformation lies centrally and destroys decussating pain and temperature fibres, giving a cape distribution with preserved light touch because dorsal columns are spared. The other options produce different sensory patterns.
  3. The correct answer is B (Vacuolar myelopathy with myelin swelling and axonal loss in posterior and lateral columns): Vitamin B12 deficiency impairs myelin synthesis, causing vacuolation of myelin in the dorsal and lateral columns, seen as symmetrical dorsal column signal on MRI. Compressive Wallerian degeneration, anterior spinal infarction and tabes dorsalis are distinguished by history and distribution.
  4. The correct answer is C (Fibrovascular replacement of marrow with oedema and inflammatory cells beneath the damaged endplate): Modic type 1 change is fibrovascular marrow with oedema and inflammation next to a degenerate disc, low on T1 and high on T2. Type 2 is fatty marrow and type 3 is sclerosis. Discitis shows disc hyperintensity and endplate destruction.
  5. The correct answer is A (Ossification of the anterior longitudinal ligament and adjacent entheses with preserved discs): Diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis involves ossification of the anterior longitudinal ligament and entheses over four or more levels, with preserved disc height and normal sacroiliac joints, and is associated with obesity and diabetes. Ankylosing spondylitis, spondylosis and pyrophosphate disease differ in disc and joint findings.
  6. The correct answer is A (Axon and myelin breakdown with macrophage clearance and Schwann cell proliferation forming guiding bands): After transection the distal axon and myelin degenerate, macrophages clear debris, and Schwann cells proliferate and form bands of Bungner that guide regenerating axons at about 1 mm per day. Segmental demyelination is a different pattern of neuropathy.
  7. The correct answer is C (Myxochondroid stroma with epithelial and myoepithelial cells, and tumour extending through a thin capsule): Pleomorphic adenoma contains myxoid and chondroid matrix that holds water, which produces the very high T2 signal, and its capsule is thin with pseudopod-like extensions, so simple enucleation leaves tumour behind. Warthin tumour has lymphoid stroma and oncocytes but is not strikingly T2 bright. Adenoid cystic carcinoma has a cribriform pattern and nerve tracking, and mucoepidermoid carcinoma shows mixed cell types with cysts, but neither fits a six year indolent mass.
  8. The correct answer is B (Mitochondria-rich oncocytic epithelium that traps tracer within a papillary cystic tumour): Warthin tumour is composed of oncocytes with abundant mitochondria, and this oncocytic epithelium accumulates pertechnetate, giving a hot nodule. Vascularity alone would wash tracer out rather than retain it. Salivary ductal cells do not express thyroid-type iodide transport in this way, and reactive nodes may sit in the same site but do not account for intense retention in a cystic papillary lesion.
  9. The correct answer is B (Basaloid cribriform cells with tropism for nerve sheaths, spreading well beyond palpable margins): Adenoid cystic carcinoma has a cribriform or tubular basaloid architecture and a marked tendency for perineural invasion, so tumour tracks along cranial nerves and recurs years later. Mucoepidermoid carcinoma and acinic cell carcinoma are malignant but seldom produce nerve enhancement of this extent. A pseudocapsule with myxoid stroma describes the benign mixed tumour, which does not paralyse the facial nerve.
  10. The correct answer is A (Solid growth with necrosis, neural invasion and frequent mitoses in the epidermoid component): Mucoepidermoid carcinoma is graded on factors such as cystic proportion, necrosis, neural invasion, mitotic rate and nuclear atypia, and solid growth with necrosis and perineural invasion indicates high grade. Large mucin-filled cysts with bland nuclei are the hallmark of low grade disease. Mucin extravasation with granulomatous reaction is an incidental inflammatory response and does not raise the grade.
  11. The correct answer is A (Extranodal marginal zone lymphoma of mucosa-associated type arising from chronic lymphoepithelial lesions): Sjogren syndrome produces lymphoepithelial lesions with chronic B cell stimulation, and this carries a markedly raised risk of marginal zone lymphoma in the salivary glands. A new dominant, fast growing mass in a long standing autoimmune gland should prompt that concern. Warthin tumour and acinic cell carcinoma can occur in the parotid but are not driven by the autoimmune process, and Hodgkin lymphoma is not the typical association.
  12. The correct answer is C (Non-keratinising tonsillar crypt carcinoma, with diffuse p16 from high-risk papillomavirus activity): Cystic nodal metastases from an occult small tonsil or base of tongue primary are characteristic of human papillomavirus related oropharyngeal carcinoma, which is non-keratinising, arises in reticulated crypt epithelium and overexpresses p16. Tobacco related keratinising tumours usually produce solid or necrotic nodes. A true branchial cyst carcinoma is rare and a benign lymphoepithelial cyst would not show an enhancing mural nodule with a tonsil lesion.
  13. The correct answer is C (Viral E7 inactivates RB, removing feedback control, while E6 degrades p53 and p16 accumulates): In high-risk papillomavirus tumours E7 binds and inactivates RB, and the loss of RB negative feedback causes p16 to accumulate, while E6 promotes p53 degradation. Swapping the roles of E6 and E7 is a common error. Tobacco related carcinomas more often show p16 loss through deletion or methylation, and Epstein-Barr virus belongs to nasopharyngeal carcinoma rather than papillomavirus related oropharyngeal disease.
  14. The correct answer is A (Stage I, because clinical N1 nodes up to 6 cm are downstaged with p16 positivity): The p16-positive oropharyngeal system uses a separate scheme in which clinical N1 is one or more ipsilateral nodes of 6 cm or less, so a T2 tumour with N1 and no metastases is stage I. The p16-negative scheme counts multiple ipsilateral nodes as N2b and gives a higher stage, which is the trap in the other choices. Advanced stages require contralateral or large nodes or distant disease.
  15. The correct answer is B (T3, because depth beyond 10 mm raises the category above that implied by surface size): In the current oral cavity scheme depth of invasion modifies the category, and a tumour of 4 cm or less with depth over 10 mm is T3. Surface size alone would give T2, which is the tempting error. Depth is considered at every size, not just for larger tumours, and invasion of intrinsic tongue muscle is not by itself a T4a feature.
  16. The correct answer is D (Non-keratinising undifferentiated carcinoma with syncytial tumour cells among abundant lymphocytes and plasma cells): Endemic nasopharyngeal carcinoma is typically non-keratinising, often undifferentiated, and is tightly linked to Epstein-Barr virus, which explains the positive viral RNA. The tumour cells grow in syncytial sheets within a lymphoid background. Keratinising squamous carcinoma is the type related to tobacco and has weak viral association, while adenoid cystic carcinoma and lymphoma do not fit viral RNA positivity in this setting.
  17. The correct answer is B (Direct extension through the foramen lacerum to the cavernous sinus, affecting the abducens nerve): Nasopharyngeal carcinoma often grows upward through the foramen lacerum and enters the cavernous sinus, where the abducens nerve lies free next to the internal carotid artery and is vulnerable early. Spread along the mandibular nerve would produce jaw and facial sensory symptoms. Retropharyngeal nodes and the jugular foramen relate to cranial nerves IX to XI, so they do not explain isolated lateral gaze weakness.
  18. The correct answer is B (Endophytic nests of thickened squamous epithelium growing into stroma, with carcinoma in a minority): Inverted papilloma grows inward as thick epithelial ridges, which creates the cerebriform pattern on MRI, and the site of attachment shows focal hyperostosis. It recurs if the attachment is not removed and a minority undergo squamous carcinoma transformation. Fungiform papilloma is exophytic and septal, a hamartoma lacks convoluted epithelium, and fibrous dysplasia is a bone process.
  19. The correct answer is D (Olfactory neuroepithelium with lobular growth and Homer Wright rosettes, graded by the Hyams system): A cribriform plate mass with intracranial dumbbell extension and peritumoral cysts, in a patient with anosmia, points to esthesioneuroblastoma, which arises from olfactory neuroepithelium and shows lobules, neurofibrillary matrix and rosettes. It is graded by Hyams system. Adenoid cystic carcinoma, myoepithelial tumours and squamous carcinoma are sinonasal tumours but lack the neuroendocrine features and the characteristic location.
  20. The correct answer is A (Broad aseptate hyphae invading vessel walls, causing thrombosis and infarction across tissue planes): Mucorales fungi thrive in ketoacidosis and invade blood vessels, producing thrombosis, black necrotic eschar and fast extension into the orbit and brain, with fat stranding preceding bone erosion. Allergic fungal disease and fungus balls remain luminal, and granulomatosis with polyangiitis causes vasculitis without the fungal angioinvasion and metabolic context described.
  21. The correct answer is A (Thick eosinophilic mucin containing sparse hyphae and concentrated calcium, iron and manganese salts): Allergic fungal rhinosinusitis in an atopic patient produces eosinophilic mucin laden with hyphae, and heavy metal and calcium content explains the high CT attenuation and the T2 signal void. The fungus does not invade tissue. Tuberculosis and invasive fungal disease would behave differently, and retained secretions without organisms may be hyperdense but lack the allergic mucin and polyps.
  22. The correct answer is A (Sparse lymphatic network in the vocal fold mucosa, which limits early regional spread): The true vocal fold has very few lymphatic channels, so small glottic cancers seldom involve nodes and tend to present early because hoarseness appears quickly. Supraglottic tumours drain richly through the thyrohyoid membrane and often present with nodes. Subglottic drainage goes to paratracheal and pretracheal nodes, and the conus elasticus does influence local spread but does not explain nodal absence.
  23. The correct answer is C (T3, because invasion of the pre-epiglottic space places the tumour above the limits of T2): Vallecular involvement alone is T2, but invasion of the pre-epiglottic or paraglottic space, vocal cord fixation or inner thyroid cartilage cortex makes the tumour T3. The pre-epiglottic space lies within the larynx, so this is not T4a, which needs outer cortex or extralaryngeal invasion. Prevertebral space or carotid involvement defines T4b.
  24. The correct answer is D (Mass at the aortopulmonary window compressing the left recurrent laryngeal nerve): The left recurrent laryngeal nerve hooks beneath the aortic arch beside the ligamentum arteriosum, so mediastinal nodes or a mass at the aortopulmonary window can paralyse the left cord. The right nerve loops the subclavian artery and is not the affected side. Superior laryngeal injury causes pitch problems, and a vagal lesion at the skull base would also impair the palate and swallowing.
  25. The correct answer is B (Overlapping clear nuclei with grooves, and psammoma bodies from calcified papillary tips): Papillary carcinoma shows optically clear, overlapping nuclei with grooves and pseudoinclusions, and psammoma bodies form from calcification of papillary tips, which produces punctate microcalcifications and often cystic nodal metastases. Follicular carcinoma is diagnosed by invasion, medullary carcinoma has amyloid and calcitonin, and oncocytic tumours have granular cytoplasm without these nuclear features.
  26. The correct answer is B (Capsular or vascular invasion, favouring blood-borne dissemination over nodal spread): Follicular carcinoma cannot be diagnosed on cytology because malignancy depends on capsular or vascular invasion, and its vascular invasion explains blood-borne spread to bone and lung. Papillary carcinoma, by contrast, shows nuclear features and spreads to nodes. The medullary and anaplastic descriptions do not match a radioiodine-avid metastasis.
  27. The correct answer is C (Activating germline RET mutation, with C-cell hyperplasia as precursor and amyloid within the tumour stroma): Medullary carcinoma arises from parafollicular C cells, which are concentrated in the upper poles, and hereditary forms carry germline RET activating mutations as in multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2, which also includes phaeochromocytoma. The tumour secretes calcitonin and shows amyloid. MEN1 involves other glands, and BRAF and RAS mutations are linked to follicular cell tumours.
  28. The correct answer is C (Dedifferentiation of a pre-existing differentiated carcinoma, with TP53 loss, and every case classed as stage IV): Anaplastic carcinoma is thought to arise by dedifferentiation of papillary or follicular carcinoma, often in a long-standing goitre, with TP53 inactivation and loss of iodine uptake, and the staging system places every case in stage IV. Lymphoma is a separate diagnosis, medullary carcinoma derives from C cells, and anaplastic tumours do not concentrate radioiodine.
  29. The correct answer is C (B cell lymphoma of marginal zone or diffuse large cell type arising in chronic lymphocytic thyroiditis): Hashimoto thyroiditis carries a markedly raised risk of primary thyroid B cell lymphoma, and a rapidly enlarging, very hypoechoic, pseudocystic and FDG-avid mass without calcification fits this. Papillary carcinoma usually has microcalcification, Riedel thyroiditis causes a hard fibrotic gland rather than a rapid avid mass, and subacute thyroiditis is a painful, self-limiting inflammatory condition.
  30. The correct answer is A (Stimulating autoantibodies at the TSH receptor, causing diffuse follicular hyperplasia with scalloped colloid): Graves disease results from antibodies that stimulate the TSH receptor, producing diffuse hyperplasia, increased vascularity and uniform high tracer uptake. A toxic adenoma gives focal uptake with suppressed background. Destructive processes such as Hashimoto or subacute thyroiditis release hormone from damaged follicles and show low uptake rather than high uptake.
  31. The correct answer is D (Mitochondria-rich oxyphil cells in a single enlarged gland, which retain tracer longer than thyroid tissue): Sestamibi accumulates in mitochondria, and adenomas rich in oxyphil cells retain it while the thyroid clears it, so a single focus persists on delayed images. A solitary hypoechoic nodule with a suppressed rim of normal parathyroid tissue favours adenoma. Vascularity and symporter expression do not explain delayed retention, and four-gland hyperplasia would be multifocal.
  32. The correct answer is B (Intense osteoclastic resorption with haemorrhage and multinucleated giant cells in fibrous stroma): In hyperparathyroidism excess hormone drives osteoclastic resorption, and focal areas fill with fibrous tissue, haemorrhage and giant cells, forming a brown tumour that looks lytic on imaging. Subperiosteal phalangeal resorption supports the systemic cause. Giant cell tumour of bone, myeloma and fibrous dysplasia can look similar but are primary bone processes with different microscopy.
  33. The correct answer is A (Persistent epithelial tract from the foramen caecum, lined by respiratory or squamous epithelium, with rare carcinoma): The thyroid descends from the foramen caecum along the thyroglossal tract, and failure of the tract to involute leaves an epithelial lined cyst in the midline that moves with swallowing and tongue protrusion. A small number contain papillary thyroid carcinoma. Branchial cysts are lateral, lymphatic malformations are multiloculated, and lingual thyroid is a solid lesion.
  34. The correct answer is A (Incomplete obliteration of the cervical sinus, with squamous lining and lymphoid tissue in the wall): Second branchial cleft cysts are the most common branchial anomaly and arise from the cervical sinus. They lie at the anterior sternocleidomastoid border, often with a beak between the carotid arteries, and the wall contains squamous epithelium with lymphoid tissue. First cleft anomalies sit near the parotid and ear canal, and thyroglossal cysts are midline.
  35. The correct answer is D (Endothelium-lined lymphatic cysts without venous communication, with lymphoid aggregates in some walls): Lymphatic malformations are cystic, endothelium-lined, multiloculated lesions with fluid-fluid levels from internal haemorrhage and enhancement limited to the septa. Haemangiomas enhance avidly and are GLUT1 positive, venous malformations show phleboliths, and dermoid cysts contain fat and adnexal structures.
  36. The correct answer is A (Nests of chief cells in a vascular network, often linked to succinate dehydrogenase mutations): Carotid body tumours are paragangliomas with nests of chief cells, called Zellballen, surrounded by sustentacular cells and a rich capillary network, and many are linked to succinate dehydrogenase gene mutations. This explains the hypervascularity and flow voids. A vagal schwannoma displaces the carotids anteriorly rather than splaying them, and lymph node and thyroid tumours do not fit.
  37. The correct answer is B (Keratinising squamous epithelium in the middle ear, shedding debris and driving bone resorption): Cholesteatoma is a collection of keratinising squamous epithelium in the middle ear that sheds keratin, and the matrix and inflammatory mediators resorb bone. Restricted diffusion without central enhancement is typical. Cholesterol granuloma is bright on T1, paraganglioma enhances avidly, and histiocytosis is rare and enhances.
  38. The correct answer is D (Pseudomonas aeruginosa spreading through the fissures of Santorini to the skull base): Necrotising otitis externa in diabetic or immunocompromised older patients is usually caused by Pseudomonas aeruginosa, which spreads through the fissures of Santorini in the cartilaginous canal to the skull base. Fungal and mucor infections are uncommon causes, and pneumococcal middle ear infection arises from the tympanic cavity rather than the external canal.
  39. The correct answer is A (Spongy vascular bone replacing otic capsule, later sclerotic, fixing the stapes footplate): Otosclerosis is a focal remodelling disorder of the otic capsule in which spongy vascular bone later becomes sclerotic, typically at the fissula ante fenestram, and it fixes the stapes footplate to cause conductive loss. Tympanosclerosis involves the drum, Paget disease affects older patients, and cholesteatoma produces discharge and an abnormal drum.
  40. The correct answer is D (Biallelic RB1 inactivation, with Flexner-Wintersteiner rosettes and dystrophic calcification in necrotic tumour): Retinoblastoma is the commonest intraocular malignancy of childhood, caused by loss of both RB1 alleles, and shows rosettes and calcification, which explains the leukocoria and the dense foci. Persistent fetal vasculature has a small globe without calcification, and Coats disease shows exudative detachment without a mass.
  41. The correct answer is A (Glycosaminoglycan deposition and oedema in muscle from fibroblasts activated by TSH receptor autoimmunity): In thyroid eye disease orbital fibroblasts are activated and produce glycosaminoglycans that attract water, enlarging the muscle bellies, with tendon sparing as the key discriminator. Idiopathic orbital inflammation involves tendons, IgG4 related disease shows lacrimal gland and infraorbital nerve enlargement, and lymphoma is usually unilateral and not tied to thyroid status.
  42. The correct answer is D (Spindle and epithelioid melanocytes with GNAQ or GNA11 mutations, spreading by blood to liver): Uveal melanoma arises from choroidal melanocytes, carries GNAQ or GNA11 mutations rather than BRAF, and has no lymphatic drainage, so it metastasises by blood to the liver. Melanin shortens T1 and T2, giving the described signal. Lymphoma and haemangioma do not have melanin signal characteristics.
  43. The correct answer is D (Dense lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate rich in IgG4 plasma cells, storiform fibrosis and obliterative phlebitis): IgG4 related disease affects the lacrimal and salivary glands and peripheral nerves, and histology shows lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate with many IgG4 positive plasma cells, storiform fibrosis and obliterative phlebitis. Sarcoidosis shows granulomas, Sjogren syndrome shows lymphoepithelial lesions, and granulomatosis with polyangiitis shows necrotising vasculitis.
  44. The correct answer is A (N3b, because clinically overt extranodal extension upgrades a node of any size to this category): In the current system for non-viral head and neck squamous carcinoma, clinical extranodal extension with muscle infiltration makes any node N3b, whatever its size. Size alone would give N2a for a single node between 3 and 6 cm, which is the tempting error. N2b requires multiple ipsilateral nodes and N3a requires a node over 6 cm without extension.
  45. The correct answer is D (Compensatory blood cell production in paravertebral tissue driven by ineffective erythropoiesis and sustained erythropoietin stimulation): Ineffective erythropoiesis in thalassaemia drives marrow expansion, which causes the skull vault thickening, and haematopoietic tissue also forms in the paravertebral region, liver and spleen. The masses are smooth and lack bone destruction, which fits haematopoietic tissue rather than tumour. Neuroblastoma is tempting by site and age but shows calcification and aggressive features. Iron overload damages organs but does not produce discrete soft tissue masses, and a tuberculous collection would erode vertebral bodies.
  46. The correct answer is D (Repeated vaso-occlusion with infarction and fibrosis, leaving a shrunken autosplenectomised organ): Sickled cells obstruct the splenic microcirculation, causing repeated infarction that ends in a small fibrotic and calcified spleen in adulthood. Children with sickle cell disease usually have splenomegaly early, before this stage. Portal hypertension gives an enlarged spleen, and granulomatous infection produces discrete calcified foci rather than diffuse mineralisation of a shrunken organ. Pigment gallstones result from chronic haemolysis.
  47. The correct answer is A (Stage III with B symptoms, the spleen counted as nodal tissue and given the S suffix): Disease above and below the diaphragm makes stage III, and the spleen is classified as lymphatic tissue, so splenic involvement is recorded with the S suffix rather than moving the patient to stage IV. Weight loss over ten percent in six months, with drenching sweats, adds the B designation. Stage IV needs diffuse or disseminated involvement of an extranodal organ such as liver or marrow. The Lugano classification retains this approach.
  48. The correct answer is D (Cytokines from plasma cells stimulate osteoclasts and suppress osteoblasts, so little reactive new bone forms): Myeloma cells release factors such as RANKL and suppress osteoblast activity, so resorption is not matched by repair. Because new bone formation is minimal, technetium diphosphonate uptake is often normal or low in the lytic lesions. Solid tumours with parathyroid hormone related peptide typically provoke a reactive bone response. Amyloid can complicate myeloma, but it does not explain the punched-out skull lesions.
  49. The correct answer is B (Somatic MYD88 L265P mutation activating Toll-like receptor signalling in the neoplastic B cell clone): Lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma with an IgM paraprotein and hyperviscosity (Waldenstrom macroglobulinaemia) carries MYD88 L265P in over 90 percent of cases. The translocation involving cyclin D1 occurs in myeloma and mantle cell lymphoma, and BRAF V600E marks hairy cell leukaemia. The BCL2 translocation is characteristic of follicular lymphoma. MYD88 testing helps distinguish this disease from marginal zone lymphoma.
  50. The correct answer is C (BRAF V600E mutation together with CD25, CD103 and annexin A1 expression on the neoplastic cells): Splenomegaly without nodes, pancytopenia with monocytopenia, a dry tap from reticulin fibrosis and cells with abundant pale cytoplasm point to hairy cell leukaemia. Virtually all classic cases carry BRAF V600E and express CD25, CD103 and annexin A1. Mantle cell lymphoma shows cyclin D1, and lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma shows an IgM paraprotein with MYD88. A BCR::ABL1 fusion defines chronic myeloid leukaemia, which causes leucocytosis rather than pancytopenia.
  51. The correct answer is C (Blast phase with additional genetic lesions, producing myeloid sarcoma and diffuse marrow replacement by immature cells): Blast counts of 20 percent or more in blood or marrow, or extramedullary blast proliferation such as myeloid sarcoma, define blast phase of chronic myeloid leukaemia. Blast phase is usually myeloid but about a quarter of cases are lymphoid. Accelerated phase is defined by lower blast percentages, basophilia, thrombocytosis and clonal evolution. Richter-type transformation belongs to chronic lymphocytic leukaemia, and haemophagocytic syndrome does not produce 35 percent circulating blasts.
  52. The correct answer is B (CD30 and CD15 positive, with absent CD45 and weak or absent B cell antigen expression): Fibrous bands, lacunar cells and a young woman with mediastinal disease fit nodular sclerosis classic Hodgkin lymphoma. Reed-Sternberg cells are CD30 and CD15 positive and usually lack CD45 and most B cell markers. Nodular lymphocyte predominant disease keeps CD20 and CD45. Anaplastic large cell lymphoma is CD30 positive but is ALK positive with cytotoxic markers in these cases and lacks CD15.
  53. The correct answer is A (Multiple angiomatoid nodules separated by fibrosclerotic stroma with a central fibrous scar, a benign non-neoplastic process): Sclerosing angiomatoid nodular transformation is a benign splenic lesion in which angiomatoid nodules lie in a fibrosclerotic stroma. Contrast imaging shows the typical spoke-wheel enhancement from the central scar. Littoral cell angioma is multifocal with cells staining for CD31 and CD68, and a hamartoma consists of disorganised red pulp. An infarct shows necrosis rather than enhancing fibrous septa.
  54. The correct answer is D (Clonal stem cell proliferation with JAK-STAT activation, and megakaryocyte-derived growth factors driving reticulin and collagen deposition): Primary myelofibrosis is a clonal myeloproliferative neoplasm with driver mutations in JAK2, CALR or MPL. Dysplastic megakaryocytes release transforming growth factor beta and platelet derived growth factor, leading to marrow fibrosis, osteosclerosis and extramedullary haematopoiesis in the spleen. Metastatic disease can also cause sclerosis but spleens are not usually massive. Aplastic anaemia has a hypocellular marrow and no splenomegaly.
  55. The correct answer is C (CD1a and langerin expression with Birbeck granules resembling tennis rackets on electron microscopy): Lytic punched-out skull lesions, diabetes insipidus from pituitary stalk involvement and exophthalmos form the classic triad of multisystem Langerhans cell histiocytosis. The cells express CD1a, langerin and S100 and contain Birbeck granules. Rosai-Dorfman cells are CD1a negative, and juvenile xanthogranuloma cells are also CD1a negative. Haemophagocytic syndrome is a systemic macrophage activation state and does not form discrete bone lesions.
  56. The correct answer is D (Unicentric Castleman disease of hyaline vascular type, usually cured by complete surgical removal): The hyaline vascular variant produces a solitary, avidly enhancing mass with small regressed germinal centres surrounded by concentric mantle layers, called onion skinning, and radial hyalinised vessels. Surgical excision is usually curative. The multicentric plasma cell type is systemic with fever and cytopenias. Follicular lymphoma loses mantle zones, and thymoma lies in the anterior compartment.
  57. The correct answer is B (A translocation placing BCL2 beside the immunoglobulin heavy chain enhancer, blocking apoptosis of germinal centre cells): Follicular lymphoma carries t(14;18) in most cases, which overexpresses the anti-apoptotic BCL2 protein so germinal centre cells escape apoptosis. This explains the indolent course. MYC translocation belongs to Burkitt lymphoma, MALT1 rearrangement to marginal zone lymphoma at mucosal sites, and cyclin D1 to mantle cell lymphoma.
  58. The correct answer is D (Hyperuricaemia, hyperkalaemia and hyperphosphataemia from rapid release of intracellular contents with acute kidney injury): Burkitt lymphoma has near 100 percent proliferation, the starry sky pattern from tingible body macrophages, and a very high cell turnover. Effective chemotherapy causes rapid cell death, releasing potassium, phosphate and nucleic acids that become uric acid, risking acute kidney injury. Hypercalcaemia, hyperviscosity and DIC are not the typical early complication in this setting.
  59. The correct answer is C (Deauville score 4 with reduced uptake from baseline, indicating a partial metabolic response at interim assessment): On the five point Deauville scale, a score of 4 means uptake moderately above liver, and 5 means markedly higher, usually two to three times liver uptake or new lesions. With reduced uptake from baseline at interim, score 4 or 5 is a partial metabolic response, whereas at end of treatment persistent 4 or 5 signals residual disease. Scores 1 to 3 are regarded as a complete metabolic response in the Lugano criteria.
  60. The correct answer is A (Squamous cell carcinoma of the hypopharynx and oesophagus arising in chronically damaged mucosa): Iron deficiency anaemia, dysphagia and a cervical oesophageal web define Plummer-Vinson syndrome, which predisposes to squamous cell carcinoma of the upper aerodigestive tract. Barrett adenocarcinoma follows reflux at the lower oesophagus. Achalasia and Zenker diverticulum cause dysphagia but are not associated with iron deficiency or a web, and are not premalignant in the same way.
  61. The correct answer is D (Membrane skeleton protein deficiency produces rigid spheroidal cells that are trapped and destroyed in splenic cords): Hereditary spherocytosis arises from defects in ankyrin, spectrin, band 3 or protein 4.2, which cause loss of membrane surface and spherocytes. These less deformable cells are sequestered in the splenic cords and removed by macrophages, causing splenomegaly and extravascular haemolysis. Chronic haemolysis forms pigment gallstones. Splenectomy reduces haemolysis.
  62. The correct answer is A (PML::RARA fusion from t(15;17), conferring sensitivity to all-trans retinoic acid): Acute promyelocytic leukaemia causes disseminated intravascular coagulation and hyperfibrinolysis, so intracranial haemorrhage is a leading early cause of death. The defining lesion is t(15;17) generating PML::RARA, which blocks differentiation until treated with all-trans retinoic acid and arsenic trioxide. The core binding factor leukaemias have Auer rods but do not usually cause this severe coagulopathy. BCR::ABL1 defines chronic myeloid leukaemia.
  63. The correct answer is C (Precursor T cell neoplasm arising from thymic lymphoblasts, with cytoplasmic CD3 and TdT positivity): An adolescent boy with a bulky anterior mediastinal mass, tracheal and caval compression and TdT positive blasts has a T lymphoblastic neoplasm. When marrow blasts exceed 25 percent the disease is called leukaemia, otherwise lymphoma. TdT marks immature lymphoid precursors and is absent in mature lymphomas and in Hodgkin cells.
  64. The correct answer is D (The t(11;18) producing API2::MALT1 fusion, which activates NF-kB signalling independent of antigen stimulation): Early gastric extranodal marginal zone lymphoma depends on antigen driven signalling from H. pylori and often regresses after eradication. Lymphomas carrying API2::MALT1 from t(11;18) have constitutive NF-kB activation, so they do not depend on bacterial stimulation and rarely regress. The other translocations belong to follicular, Burkitt and mantle cell lymphomas.
  65. The correct answer is D (Toxoplasma lymphadenitis with follicular hyperplasia, epithelioid histiocyte clusters and monocytoid B cell proliferation): The triad of florid follicular hyperplasia, small groups of epithelioid histiocytes in and around germinal centres, and monocytoid B cell sinus proliferation is characteristic of Toxoplasma lymphadenitis. Preserved fatty hila and oval shape favour a benign reactive node. Kikuchi disease shows necrotic foci, cat scratch disease shows stellate abscess-like granulomas and sarcoidosis has well-formed non-necrotising granulomas.
  66. The correct answer is A (Glucocerebrosidase deficiency, causing accumulation of glucosylceramide within lysosomes of macrophages): Gaucher disease results from glucocerebrosidase deficiency, so glucosylceramide accumulates in macrophages, which expand the marrow and spleen. The result is splenomegaly, cytopenias, bone crises and the Erlenmeyer flask deformity. Niemann-Pick disease shows foamy macrophages and a cherry red spot, Tay-Sachs affects neurones without hepatosplenomegaly, and Fabry disease affects vessels and kidneys.
  67. The correct answer is B (Activating JAK2 V617F mutation producing erythropoietin-independent signalling in myeloid progenitors): Over 95 percent of polycythaemia vera carries JAK2 V617F, which makes red cell production independent of erythropoietin, hence the suppressed level. Budd-Chiari syndrome from hepatic vein thrombosis is a recognised complication. CALR mutations occur in essential thrombocythaemia and myelofibrosis, BCR::ABL1 defines chronic myeloid leukaemia and KIT D816V marks systemic mastocytosis.
  68. The correct answer is B (Monoclonal immunoglobulin light chain fragments produced by a clonal plasma cell population): Macroglossia, nephrotic syndrome and infiltrative cardiomyopathy with low voltage ECG point to systemic AL amyloidosis, in which light chains from a clonal plasma cell disorder misfold into beta-pleated sheets. Congo red apple-green birefringence confirms amyloid but not its type. Transthyretin is mainly cardiac, AA amyloid follows chronic inflammation and beta-2 microglobulin is dialysis related.
  69. The correct answer is A (Smouldering myeloma, defined by marrow plasma cells of ten to sixty percent without myeloma defining events): Smouldering myeloma requires a serum paraprotein of 30 g/L or more or 10 to 60 percent marrow plasma cells, with no CRAB features or other myeloma defining events. MGUS has plasma cells under ten percent and paraprotein under 30 g/L. Symptomatic myeloma needs end organ damage or biomarkers such as 60 percent clonal plasma cells. A solitary plasmacytoma requires a single lesion with normal marrow.
  70. The correct answer is B (Transformation to diffuse large B cell lymphoma, with sheets of large cells replacing the small lymphocytes): Sudden growth of a single node, constitutional symptoms, rising lactate dehydrogenase and very high uptake on PET suggest Richter transformation, usually to diffuse large B cell lymphoma. Biopsy of the most avid site is needed because SUVmax above about 10 predicts transformation. Prolymphocytic progression is judged on the blood film, and benign reactive nodes rarely show such avid uptake.
  71. The correct answer is D (Fibrous tissue with haemosiderin and calcium at sites of previous small haemorrhages in congested red pulp): Portal hypertension congests the splenic red pulp, causing small haemorrhages that heal as siderotic nodules containing haemosiderin and calcium, called Gamna-Gandy bodies. Their paramagnetic content produces blooming on gradient echo MRI. Diffuse iron deposition has a different distribution, healed granulomas are calcified but not blooming iron-rich nodules, and melanoma would show a mass.
  72. The correct answer is C (Translocation of CCND1 beside the immunoglobulin heavy chain enhancer, leading to cyclin D1 overexpression): CD5 positive, CD23 negative small B cells with lymphomatous polyposis are typical of mantle cell lymphoma, which carries t(11;14) producing cyclin D1 overexpression. Chronic lymphocytic leukaemia is CD23 positive and often has 13q14 deletion. BCL2 translocation is characteristic of follicular lymphoma, and BRAF V600E marks hairy cell leukaemia.
  73. The correct answer is D (Eosinophilic hyaline membranes lining collapsed terminal airspaces, with atelectasis and congested alveolar walls): Surfactant deficiency in a preterm infant of a diabetic mother (insulin delays surfactant synthesis) causes diffuse atelectasis. Plasma proteins and necrotic type 2 pneumocyte debris form hyaline membranes on the airspace walls. Bronchopulmonary dysplasia is the later complication of ventilation and oxygen. Meconium aspiration affects term or post-term infants, and bacterial pneumonia would show neutrophils rather than a uniform granular pattern.
  74. The correct answer is A (Coagulative necrosis of the terminal ileum and right colon with bacterial gas production in the submucosa): Necrotising enterocolitis in premature infants involves mucosal ischaemic injury, enteral feeding and bacterial colonisation, producing coagulative necrosis, most often in the terminal ileum and proximal colon. Gas-forming organisms create submucosal and subserosal gas (pneumatosis intestinalis) that can track into the portal veins. Crohn disease and Hirschsprung enterocolitis have different substrates, and volvulus would show a whirl and midgut infarction without pneumatosis.
  75. The correct answer is A (Profound acute hypoxic ischaemia affecting metabolically active deep grey nuclei and the sensorimotor cortex): In term infants, profound acute hypoxia selectively injures regions with high metabolic demand and myelinating activity: the ventrolateral thalami, posterior putamina, brainstem and perirolandic cortex. Prolonged partial hypoxia instead produces parasagittal watershed injury. Periventricular leukomalacia and germinal matrix haemorrhage are lesions of the premature brain, related to vascular and cellular immaturity.
  76. The correct answer is A (A vascular subependymal layer with fragile thin-walled vessels and little supporting stroma): The germinal matrix is a highly vascular, cellular layer that involutes by about 36 weeks and has thin-walled vessels with little supportive stroma. Fluctuations in cerebral blood flow and venous pressure in the sick premature infant make it bleed, and blood can rupture into the ventricle. Watershed injury and white matter injury are separate lesions.
  77. The correct answer is C (Coagulative necrosis and gliosis of the deep white matter beside the lateral ventricles in neonates): Periventricular leukomalacia follows hypoxia or infection in premature infants and affects the arterial end-zone white matter beside the ventricles, where pre-oligodendrocytes are vulnerable. Necrosis is followed by gliosis and volume loss, and the corticospinal fibres to the legs lie closest to the ventricles, producing spastic diplegia. Venous infarct and term watershed injury have different distributions and cell targets.
  78. The correct answer is D (Small round blue cells with neuropil and Homer Wright rosettes, expressing neuroendocrine markers): Neuroblastoma arises from sympathetic neural crest in the adrenal medulla or paraspinal ganglia, secretes catecholamines and often has stippled calcification. Neuropil and Homer Wright rosettes are characteristic, and the mass typically encases vessels. Wilms tumour is triphasic and displaces vessels, while Ewing sarcoma and rhabdomyosarcoma have their own markers and lack catecholamine production.
  79. The correct answer is C (Tumours in young infants commonly lack MYCN amplification and can regress spontaneously or differentiate): Stage MS (formerly 4S) disease in infants under 18 months involves skin, liver and marrow without cortical bone disease and often regresses. Favourable biology includes age under 18 months, absent MYCN amplification and hyperdiploidy. MYCN amplification, older age and a high mitosis-karyorrhexis index mark unfavourable disease. Pattern of organ spread alone does not decide the outcome.
  80. The correct answer is D (Blastemal, epithelial tubule and stromal components within one tumour, recapitulating nephrogenesis): Wilms tumour (nephroblastoma) is an embryonal kidney tumour with blastemal, epithelial and stromal elements, and it may invade the renal vein and inferior vena cava. The claw sign denotes a renal origin. Congenital mesoblastic nephroma is a spindle cell tumour of the first months of life. Neuroblastoma and clear cell carcinoma differ in origin and histology.
  81. The correct answer is B (An embryonal liver tumour of fetal and embryonal epithelial cells, sometimes with mesenchymal elements): Hepatoblastoma is the commonest primary liver malignancy of early childhood, linked to prematurity, Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome and familial adenomatous polyposis. It has epithelial fetal and embryonal patterns, with or without osteoid and cartilage, and it secretes alpha-fetoprotein. Hepatocellular carcinoma usually needs a diseased liver at this age, and the other options have different features and a lower marker rise.
  82. The correct answer is D (Embryonal rhabdomyosarcoma of botryoid type, with nuclear desmin and myogenin expression): Botryoid sarcoma is a subtype of embryonal rhabdomyosarcoma that forms grape-like polypoid masses beneath mucosal surfaces of the vagina, bladder and nasopharynx, with a dense subepithelial cambium layer. Strap cells with cross striations and nuclear myogenin support skeletal muscle differentiation. The alveolar type carries PAX3 or PAX7 fusions with FOXO1 and affects adolescents.
  83. The correct answer is C (Hypoplasia of the ipsilateral lung with reduced airway branching and muscularised pulmonary arterioles): Herniation of abdominal viscera through a posterolateral (Bochdalek) diaphragmatic defect compresses the developing lung, reducing airway generations and alveoli and producing abnormal pulmonary vasculature with pulmonary hypertension. These two factors drive mortality rather than the bowel itself. Transient tachypnoea and hyaline membrane disease are not structural lung deficits, and sequestration is an unrelated malformation.
  84. The correct answer is B (Association with later malignancy, including pleuropulmonary blastoma and mucinous adenocarcinoma): Congenital pulmonary airway malformation is a hamartomatous cystic lesion, with the pulmonary arterial supply distinguishing it from sequestration. Type 1 is the commonest, with large cysts lined by pseudostratified epithelium. Although resolved by resection, the lesion is linked to pleuropulmonary blastoma (type 4) and to mucinous adenocarcinoma. Infection also occurs, but malignancy is the notable pathology.
  85. The correct answer is D (Macrophages with abundant wrinkled tissue paper cytoplasm from glucocerebroside accumulation): Gaucher disease results from glucocerebrosidase deficiency, with glucocerebroside accumulating in macrophages that appear as Gaucher cells with a wrinkled tissue paper appearance. Splenomegaly, marrow infiltration, thrombocytopenia and an Erlenmeyer flask deformity of the distal femur are typical. Niemann-Pick disease stores sphingomyelin and gives foamy cells, while the other options are not lysosomal storage diseases.
  86.
