The correct answer is A (Hepatocellular adenomas, which can haemorrhage and undergo malignant transformation in this condition): Type I glycogen storage disease (von Gierke) causes glycogen and fat accumulation in the liver and kidneys, with hypoglycaemia, lactic acidosis and hyperuricaemia. Hepatocellular adenomas develop in many patients, can bleed and may transform to carcinoma. Cirrhosis is not a feature of this type, and focal nodular hyperplasia and haemangioma are unrelated to the enzyme defect.
  87. The correct answer is D (Ghon complex, a subpleural caseating focus plus caseous involvement of draining hilar lymph nodes): The Ghon focus is a subpleural caseating granuloma, usually in the lower part of the upper lobe or upper part of the lower lobe, and with caseating lymph node involvement it forms the Ghon complex. Calcification and healing are usual. The Simon focus, Assmann focus and Rasmussen aneurysm relate to later haematogenous, reactivation and cavitary events.
  88. The correct answer is B (Mycobacteria produce few proteolytic enzymes, so the avascular disc is destroyed late relative to bone): Spinal tuberculosis (Pott disease) usually starts in the anterior vertebral body, near the endplate, via haematogenous spread. Mycobacteria produce little proteolytic activity, so the disc is relatively spared until late, in contrast to pyogenic infection that destroys the disc early. Caseation, cold abscess and subligamentous spread are characteristic, and gibbus deformity follows vertebral collapse.
  89. The correct answer is A (Foamy eosinophilic alveolar exudate with cysts that stain with Grocott methenamine silver): Pneumocystis jirovecii causes diffuse interstitial and alveolar infiltration in HIV patients when CD4 counts fall below about 200. The alveoli contain a foamy, eosinophilic frothy exudate in which cup-shaped cysts are shown by silver stains, and the CT appearance is perihilar ground glass. Tuberculosis, CMV and Aspergillus have different histology and imaging patterns.
  90. The correct answer is A (Perivascular spaces distended by encapsulated yeasts and mucoid capsule material, with minimal inflammation): Cryptococcus neoformans, an encapsulated yeast, spreads through the subarachnoid space into Virchow-Robin spaces, where the mucoid capsule and organisms form gelatinous pseudocysts (soap bubble lesions) with a very weak inflammatory response in immunosuppression. Mucicarmine stains the capsule. Abscess, tuberculoma and cysticercosis each elicit enhancement or contain different organisms.
  91. The correct answer is C (Central necrosis with surrounding inflammation containing tachyzoites and a peripheral zone of encysted bradyzoites): Reactivation of latent Toxoplasma gondii in the setting of low CD4 counts causes necrotising encephalitis. Lesions have a central necrotic zone, an inflamed zone with tachyzoites, and an outer zone with encysted bradyzoites. The resulting disruption of the blood-brain barrier produces ring enhancement. Pseudopalisading necrosis defines glioblastoma, and lymphoma is the other common differential diagnosis.
  92. The correct answer is C (Septate hyphae branching at acute angles that invade vessel walls, causing thrombosis and haemorrhagic infarcts): Aspergillus is angioinvasive in neutropenic patients, producing infarcts with a rim of haemorrhage that appears as the halo sign. It has narrow septate hyphae branching at about 45 degrees, in contrast to Mucorales, which have broad aseptate hyphae at right angles. Candida shows yeasts with pseudohyphae, and Cryptococcus has a polysaccharide capsule.
  93. The correct answer is C (Echinococcus granulosus, with germinal layer, laminated membrane and daughter cysts within a fibrous pericyst): Cystic echinococcosis from E. granulosus produces a unilocular cyst whose wall has an outer host fibrous pericyst, an acellular laminated membrane and an inner germinal layer producing brood capsules and daughter cysts. Detachment of the membrane gives the water lily sign. E. multilocularis produces an infiltrative alveolar lesion, and the other options lack this layered wall.
  94. The correct answer is A (Vesicular or colloidal stage, with a degenerating larva and scolex and an increasing host response): Taenia solium larvae pass through viable vesicular (scolex visible, little reaction), colloidal vesicular (degenerating, with rim enhancement and oedema), granular nodular and calcified nodular stages. A cyst with an eccentric scolex and mild enhancement indicates the vesicular to colloidal transition. Racemose cysts are large, lobulated and lack a scolex.
  95. The correct answer is D (Haemorrhagic necrosis of the temporal and orbitofrontal cortex with Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions): HSV-1 causes necrotising, often haemorrhagic encephalitis of the medial temporal lobe, insula and inferior frontal lobe, usually sparing the basal ganglia, which separates it from many infarcts and other encephalitides. Cowdry A inclusions occur in infected nuclei. Prion disease, demyelination and brainstem viral encephalitis have different distributions and histology.
  96. The correct answer is A (Markedly enlarged cells with a large basophilic intranuclear inclusion surrounded by a clear halo): Congenital CMV is the commonest congenital infection and causes periventricular calcification, microcephaly, chorioretinitis and hearing loss. Infected cells are enlarged (cytomegalic) with a large intranuclear basophilic inclusion and halo, sometimes with smaller cytoplasmic inclusions. Toxoplasmosis causes scattered calcification, and herpes and syphilis show different histology.
  97. The correct answer is B (Shearing forces from limb acceleration and deceleration through the immature primary spongiosa at the physis): Classic metaphyseal lesions (corner or bucket handle fractures) are specific for non-accidental injury in infants and arise from torsion and traction when a limb is shaken or yanked. The injury passes through the weakest area, the primary spongiosa, and a short fall from a sofa does not generate these forces. Rickets, scurvy and osteogenesis imperfecta have systemic features and different radiographic patterns.
  98. The correct answer is B (Hypoxic ischaemic injury with swelling, plus tearing of bridging veins from acceleration and deceleration): Abusive head trauma combines rotational acceleration and deceleration that stretch and tear the bridging cortical veins, producing subdural haemorrhage, with brain injury that is mostly hypoxic ischaemic from apnoea and seizures, producing the loss of grey-white differentiation (reversal sign). Multilayered retinal haemorrhages and brain swelling support the mechanism. Aneurysm and venous thrombosis would not account for the full pattern.
  99. The correct answer is A (Sulphur granules of branching filamentous Gram-positive bacteria with peripheral eosinophilic club material in abscesses): Actinomyces israelii is an anaerobic commensal of the oral cavity that causes chronic suppurative infection after tissue damage. The inflammation forms sinus tracts that cross tissue planes, and abscesses contain sulphur granules of filamentous bacteria with eosinophilic club formations. Nocardia is weakly acid-fast and typically affects immunosuppressed patients, and the other options lack granules.
  100. The correct answer is A (Chronic inflammation around deposited eggs, leading to squamous metaplasia and later squamous cell carcinoma): Schistosoma haematobium eggs lodge in the bladder wall, provoking granulomatous inflammation, fibrosis and calcified eggs that line the wall. Chronic injury causes squamous metaplasia and squamous cell carcinoma, which is the usual tumour type in endemic regions. S. mansoni and japonicum affect the liver and bowel, and smoking-related urothelial carcinoma is the common tumour elsewhere.

Short Answer Set 1: Model Answers

Question 1 (6 marks)

Question: A 64-year-old woman has a pelvic mass, ascites and a raised CA-125. Biopsy shows high-grade serous carcinoma. Outline the pathogenesis and describe the macroscopic and microscopic pathology of this tumour. (6 marks)

Model answer

Pathogenesis

1. Most arise from the fallopian tube fimbria: serous tubal intraepithelial carcinoma (STIC) seeds the ovary and peritoneum.

2. TP53 mutation is near universal and occurs early.

3. Defective homologous recombination: germline BRCA1 or BRCA2 mutation in a substantial minority, with further sporadic homologous recombination deficiency.

4. Marked chromosomal instability with widespread copy number change; RB1 and NF1 pathway alterations are common.

Macroscopic

- Often bilateral, solid and cystic masses with papillary excrescences, necrosis and haemorrhage.

- Capsule may be breached; peritoneal and omental deposits (omental caking) are usual at presentation.

Microscopic

- Papillary, glandular and solid slit-like architecture with complex branching.

- Marked nuclear atypia, high nuclear to cytoplasmic ratio, prominent nucleoli, brisk mitoses (often atypical).

- Psammoma bodies may be present.

- Strong diffuse nuclear p53 staining (or complete absence, the null pattern) and WT1 positivity.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Tubal origin via STIC (fimbrial precursor) (1)
  • TP53 mutation as an early, near universal event (1)
  • BRCA1/BRCA2 or homologous recombination deficiency, chromosomal instability (1)
  • Macroscopic: bilateral solid and cystic papillary mass with necrosis or haemorrhage (1)
  • Macroscopic: peritoneal or omental spread at presentation (0.5)
  • Microscopic: papillary/glandular/solid architecture with marked pleomorphism and high mitotic activity (1)
  • Psammoma bodies and/or p53 abnormal and WT1 positive immunohistochemistry (0.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating that serous carcinoma arises from ovarian surface inclusion cysts only, ignoring the tubal origin
  • Confusing high-grade with low-grade serous carcinoma, which has KRAS or BRAF mutations and wild-type TP53
  • Describing imaging features such as ascites or omental caking on CT instead of pathology

Question 2 (6 marks)

Question: Compare the pathology of complete and partial hydatidiform moles, including genetic origin, presence of fetal tissue, villous and trophoblastic changes, p57 staining and risk of choriocarcinoma. (6 marks)

Model answer

Complete mole

- Genetics: diploid, usually 46,XX, all chromosomes paternal. An empty ovum is fertilised by one sperm that duplicates (or less often two sperm).

- No embryonic or fetal tissue.

- Villi: diffuse hydropic swelling with cisterns, avascular.

- Trophoblast: diffuse, often marked circumferential hyperplasia and atypia.

- p57 (maternally expressed gene) is negative in villous stromal cells and cytotrophoblast.

- Highest serum beta-hCG; uterus often large for dates.

- Risk of persistent trophoblastic disease about 15 to 20 per cent, with choriocarcinoma in a small percentage (about 2 per cent).

Partial mole

- Genetics: triploid, usually 69,XXY or 69,XXX: one haploid maternal set and two paternal sets (dispermy).

- Fetal tissue or fetal red cells often present, usually non-viable.

- Villi: two populations, some enlarged and hydropic with scalloped outlines and trophoblastic inclusions, others normal-sized.

- Trophoblast: focal, mild hyperplasia.

- p57 is positive.

- hCG lower; uterus often small for dates.

- Persistent disease uncommon and choriocarcinoma very rare.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Complete: diploid paternal (46,XX) origin from an empty ovum (1)
  • Partial: triploid (69,XXY or XXX) with two paternal sets and one maternal (1)
  • Fetal tissue absent in complete, may be present in partial (1)
  • Villous swelling diffuse in complete versus focal with two populations in partial (1)
  • Trophoblastic hyperplasia diffuse and atypical in complete versus focal and mild in partial (1)
  • p57 negative in complete, positive in partial; choriocarcinoma risk higher in complete and very rare in partial (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reversing p57 staining, which is lost in complete moles because the complete mole lacks a maternal genome
  • Stating that partial moles are diploid
  • Claiming that choriocarcinoma never arises from a complete mole

Question 3 (6 marks)

Question: A 16-year-old has a painful swelling around the knee. Biopsy confirms conventional osteosarcoma. Describe its typical location, macroscopic and microscopic pathology, and name three conditions that predispose to osteosarcoma. (6 marks)

Model answer

Location

- Metaphysis of long bones, most often distal femur and proximal tibia; peak in adolescence.

Macroscopic

- Large, gritty, tan-white, haemorrhagic and necrotic mass arising in the metaphysis.

- Destroys cortex, lifts the periosteum and extends into the soft tissue; may spread within the medullary cavity.

- Usually spares the epiphyseal plate until late, but may extend into the epiphysis.

Microscopic

- Malignant pleomorphic spindle or epithelioid cells with mitoses, including atypical forms.

- Defining feature: direct production of osteoid (and woven bone) by the malignant cells, in lace-like or sheet-like pattern. Cartilage and fibroblastic areas may coexist.

Predisposing conditions (any three)

- Hereditary retinoblastoma (germline RB1 mutation).

- Li-Fraumeni syndrome (germline TP53).

- Paget disease of bone in older adults.

- Prior ionising radiation.

- Bone infarct or other chronic bone lesions (also Rothmund-Thomson syndrome).

Haematogenous metastasis, mainly to lung.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Location: metaphysis of distal femur or proximal tibia in adolescents (1)
  • Macroscopic: bulky gritty haemorrhagic necrotic tumour breaching cortex with soft tissue extension (1.5)
  • Microscopic: pleomorphic malignant cells with mitoses (1)
  • Production of osteoid by tumour cells as the defining feature (1)
  • Three valid predisposing conditions (0.5 each) (1.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Naming bone production by benign osteoblasts rather than malignant cells as the diagnostic feature
  • Placing the tumour in the diaphysis, which is typical for Ewing sarcoma
  • Describing periosteal reaction on radiographs rather than pathology

Question 4 (6 marks)

Question: Compare the pathogenesis and the joint pathology of osteoarthritis and rheumatoid arthritis. Include the principal joint tissues affected and the characteristic structural changes in each. (6 marks)

Model answer

Osteoarthritis (degenerative)

- Pathogenesis: mechanical stress and ageing cause chondrocyte injury; proteases (MMPs, aggrecanases) driven by IL-1 and TNF degrade cartilage matrix faster than it is repaired. Primary (idiopathic) or secondary to prior injury or deformity.

- Tissue affected: articular cartilage first; bone responds secondarily. Synovitis is mild and secondary.

- Changes: surface fibrillation and loss of cartilage, eburnation of exposed subchondral bone, osteophytes at the margins, subchondral cysts and sclerosis, loose bodies.

- Distribution: weight-bearing joints, DIP and PIP joints of the hand, first carpometacarpal joint.

Rheumatoid arthritis (autoimmune)

- Pathogenesis: genetic susceptibility (HLA-DRB1 shared epitope) and environment (smoking, citrullination of proteins) lead to anti-CCP antibodies and rheumatoid factor, CD4 T helper (Th1, Th17) responses, and TNF, IL-1 and IL-6 production.

- Tissue affected: synovium first, with chronic synovitis.

- Changes: synovial hyperplasia with lymphoid follicles, pannus formation that erodes cartilage and bone, marginal erosions, then fibrous or bony ankylosis and deformity.

- Distribution: symmetrical small joints of the hands (MCP, PIP, sparing DIP) and feet, wrists.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • OA pathogenesis: mechanical wear, chondrocyte injury and protease-mediated cartilage loss (1)
  • OA changes: fibrillation, eburnation, osteophytes, subchondral cysts (1)
  • OA distribution (weight-bearing joints, DIP) with cartilage as primary tissue (1)
  • RA pathogenesis: autoimmune, HLA-DRB1, citrullinated antigens, T cell and TNF-driven (1)
  • RA changes: synovitis, pannus, erosions, ankylosis (1)
  • RA distribution (symmetrical small joints, sparing DIP) with synovium as primary tissue (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing osteoarthritis as inflammatory in origin
  • Listing radiographic signs such as joint space narrowing instead of the underlying pathology
  • Stating that rheumatoid arthritis characteristically involves the DIP joints

Question 5 (6 marks)

Question: Outline the pathogenesis of pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, including precursor lesions and the major genetic alterations, and describe its macroscopic and microscopic pathology. (6 marks)

Model answer

Precursors

- Pancreatic intraepithelial neoplasia (PanIN), grades 1 to 3 (low to high grade), the commonest precursor.

- Intraductal papillary mucinous neoplasm and mucinous cystic neoplasm are other precursors.

Genetics (stepwise accumulation)

- KRAS activating mutation, present in more than 90 per cent, occurs early.

- CDKN2A (p16) inactivation.

- TP53 inactivation.

- SMAD4 (DPC4) loss, a late event.

- Germline BRCA2, PALB2, CDKN2A mutations and hereditary pancreatitis (PRSS1) raise risk.

Macroscopic

- Most (about 60 per cent) occur in the head; firm, poorly defined, white-yellow mass.

- Head tumours obstruct the common bile duct and pancreatic duct, causing jaundice and upstream duct dilatation; body and tail tumours present late.

Microscopic

- Infiltrating, well to poorly formed glands lined by mucin-secreting cuboidal or columnar cells with atypia.

- Marked desmoplastic stroma.

- Perineural invasion is very common, with lymphovascular invasion.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • PanIN (or IPMN/MCN) as precursor lesions (1)
  • KRAS mutation early and in more than 90 per cent (1)
  • CDKN2A, TP53 and SMAD4 inactivation as stepwise events (1)
  • Macroscopic: head predominance, firm pale mass with duct obstruction (1)
  • Microscopic: infiltrating mucin-producing glands with desmoplastic stroma (1)
  • Perineural and lymphovascular invasion (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Listing APC or beta-catenin as the key early event, which belongs to colorectal carcinoma
  • Stating that most tumours arise in the tail
  • Giving only imaging features such as the double duct sign

Question 6 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the macroscopic and microscopic pathology of the usual interstitial pneumonia pattern seen in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis, outline its pathogenesis, and list two complications. (6 marks)

Model answer

Macroscopic

- Lungs are shrunken and firm; fibrosis is worst in the subpleural and basal regions.

- Cobblestone pleural surface; honeycomb cysts with thick fibrous walls.

Microscopic

- Patchy fibrosis with temporal heterogeneity: dense old collagen alongside normal lung and newly formed fibroblastic foci (subepithelial aggregates of proliferating myofibroblasts).

- Architectural distortion with honeycomb change; minimal inflammation.

- Smooth muscle hyperplasia is common.

Pathogenesis

- Repeated microinjury to alveolar epithelial cells (type II) with aberrant repair.

- Release of TGF-beta and other profibrotic mediators drives fibroblast and myofibroblast proliferation and collagen deposition.

- Risk factors: ageing, smoking, MUC5B promoter variant, telomerase gene mutations (TERT, TERC) causing short telomeres.

Complications (any two)

- Chronic respiratory failure.

- Pulmonary hypertension and cor pulmonale.

- Acute exacerbation.

- Lung cancer.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Macroscopic: subpleural and basal predominant firm shrunken lung with honeycombing (1)
  • Microscopic: patchy fibrosis with temporal heterogeneity (1)
  • Fibroblastic foci and architectural distortion with honeycomb change (1)
  • Pathogenesis: repeated alveolar epithelial injury with abnormal repair and TGF-beta driven fibroblast activation (1.5)
  • Predisposing factors such as telomerase mutations, MUC5B, smoking, ageing (0.5)
  • Two valid complications (0.5 each) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing the CT pattern of honeycombing rather than the pathology
  • Calling it an inflammatory disease that responds to corticosteroids
  • Placing the fibrosis in the upper zones, which suggests sarcoidosis, silicosis or hypersensitivity pneumonitis

Question 7 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the macroscopic and microscopic pathology of glioblastoma, and state the molecular criteria that define IDH-wildtype glioblastoma, CNS WHO grade 4, in the 2021 WHO classification of central nervous system tumours. (6 marks)

Model answer

Macroscopic

- Poorly demarcated, infiltrative mass, usually in the cerebral hemispheres (frontal and temporal lobes).

- Variegated: grey-pink viable tumour, yellow necrotic centre and areas of haemorrhage.

- May cross the corpus callosum to the opposite hemisphere (butterfly pattern) with surrounding oedema and mass effect.

Microscopic

- Diffusely infiltrating, pleomorphic astrocytic cells with mitotic activity.

- Pseudopalisading necrosis: tumour cells lined up around serpiginous necrosis.

- Microvascular proliferation: glomeruloid, endothelial hyperplastic vessels.

WHO CNS5 2021 definition

- Diffuse astrocytic tumour, IDH-wildtype (and H3-wildtype).

- Qualifies as glioblastoma, CNS WHO grade 4, if there is microvascular proliferation or necrosis, or one or more of: TERT promoter mutation, EGFR gene amplification, combined whole chromosome 7 gain and chromosome 10 loss (+7/-10).

- IDH-mutant astrocytomas are no longer called glioblastoma.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Macroscopic: infiltrative variegated mass with necrosis and haemorrhage (1)
  • Butterfly spread across the corpus callosum with oedema or mass effect (0.5)
  • Microscopic: pleomorphic astrocytic cells with mitoses (0.5)
  • Pseudopalisading necrosis (1)
  • Microvascular proliferation (1)
  • IDH-wildtype requirement (1)
  • Molecular criteria: TERT promoter mutation, EGFR amplification, +7/-10 (any two named) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing the ring-enhancing mass on MRI instead of pathology
  • Including IDH-mutant tumours as glioblastoma, which is superseded in CNS5
  • Omitting that molecular features can establish the diagnosis without necrosis or microvascular proliferation

Question 8 (6 marks)

Question: List six patterns of tissue necrosis. For each, give the typical cause or setting and one tissue or organ in which it characteristically occurs. (6 marks)

Model answer

1. Coagulative necrosis: ischaemia (arterial occlusion); solid organs such as kidney, heart, spleen. Architecture is preserved for days.

2. Liquefactive necrosis: enzymatic digestion of dead tissue, in bacterial or fungal abscesses and in hypoxic injury of the brain (cerebral infarct).

3. Caseous necrosis: tuberculosis and some fungal infections; granulomas in lung or lymph node, soft, cheesy, amorphous granular debris.

4. Fat necrosis: enzymatic in acute pancreatitis (lipase releases fatty acids that form calcium soaps), or traumatic in breast and subcutaneous fat.

5. Fibrinoid necrosis: immune-mediated vasculitis and malignant hypertension; deposits of immune complexes and fibrin in vessel walls (arterioles).

6. Gangrenous necrosis: ischaemic coagulative necrosis of a limb (dry gangrene) or of bowel; wet gangrene when superimposed bacterial infection adds liquefaction.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Six valid patterns named (coagulative, liquefactive, caseous, fat, fibrinoid, gangrenous, or others accepted), 0.5 each (3)
  • Valid cause or setting for each pattern, 0.25 each (1.5)
  • Valid characteristic organ or tissue for each pattern, 0.25 each (1.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Listing apoptosis or pyroptosis, which are not patterns of necrosis
  • Placing coagulative necrosis in the brain, where infarction causes liquefactive necrosis
  • Giving only a definition of each term without a setting or organ

Question 9 (6 marks)

Question: Outline the genetic basis and the macroscopic and microscopic features of the three most common subtypes of renal cell carcinoma: clear cell, papillary and chromophobe. (6 marks)

Model answer

Clear cell carcinoma (most common)

- Genetics: loss of VHL on chromosome 3p (sporadic and von Hippel-Lindau syndrome) leading to HIF accumulation.

- Macro: solitary, golden-yellow mass with haemorrhage, necrosis and cysts; may invade the renal vein.

- Micro: nests of cells with clear cytoplasm (glycogen and lipid) and a delicate rich capillary network.

Papillary carcinoma

- Genetics: trisomy 7 and 17, loss of Y in sporadic cases; MET mutation in hereditary type 1; FH in hereditary leiomyomatosis and renal cell cancer (type 2).

- Macro: often multifocal, bilateral, brown with haemorrhage and cystic change.

- Micro: papillae with fibrovascular cores containing foamy macrophages, psammoma bodies.

Chromophobe carcinoma

- Genetics: multiple chromosomal losses (monosomies), Birt-Hogg-Dube syndrome in some.

- Macro: homogeneous tan-brown, well circumscribed mass.

- Micro: large polygonal cells with prominent cell borders, pale to eosinophilic cytoplasm, perinuclear halos and irregular (raisinoid) nuclei.

- Better prognosis than clear cell carcinoma.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Clear cell: VHL/3p loss (0.5)
  • Clear cell: golden-yellow haemorrhagic necrotic mass; clear cytoplasm with vascular network (1)
  • Papillary: trisomy 7/17, MET (type 1) or FH (type 2) (1)
  • Papillary: papillae with foamy macrophages and psammoma bodies; multifocal (1.5)
  • Chromophobe: multiple chromosomal losses or Birt-Hogg-Dube (1)
  • Chromophobe: tan-brown circumscribed mass; plant-cell-like borders with perinuclear halos and raisinoid nuclei (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Attributing VHL loss to papillary carcinoma
  • Stating that clear cell carcinoma has a white or tan cut surface, instead of golden-yellow
  • Describing CT enhancement patterns instead of gross and microscopic pathology

Question 10 (6 marks)

Question: Define aortic dissection and outline its predisposing factors, the Stanford classification, the underlying pathology, and four complications. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definition

- Blood tracks within the aortic media through an intimal tear, creating a false lumen between layers of the wall.

Predisposing factors

- Hypertension (the commonest, particularly for type B).

- Medial degeneration with loss of elastic tissue and mucoid material (cystic medial degeneration), in Marfan, Ehlers-Danlos and Loeys-Dietz syndromes, and bicuspid aortic valve.

- Pregnancy, trauma or iatrogenic injury, cocaine use, giant cell arteritis.

Stanford classification

- Type A: involves the ascending aorta (with or without descending); surgical emergency.

- Type B: confined to the descending aorta distal to the left subclavian artery; usually managed medically unless complicated.

Pathology

- Intimal tear, usually in the ascending aorta a few centimetres above the aortic valve or just beyond the left subclavian, with intramural haematoma tracking along the media; a distal re-entry tear may form.

Complications (any four)

- Rupture with haemopericardium and cardiac tamponade.

- Haemothorax or rupture into mediastinum.

- Acute aortic regurgitation.

- Branch vessel occlusion: coronary, carotid, spinal, renal, mesenteric or limb ischaemia.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Definition: blood within the media via intimal tear with false lumen (1)
  • Predisposing factors: hypertension and medial degeneration or connective tissue disorders (two valid) (1)
  • Stanford type A and type B correctly defined (1.5)
  • Pathology: intimal tear with intramural tracking of blood, location of tear (0.5)
  • Four valid complications (0.5 each) (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Confusing Stanford with DeBakey classification
  • Stating that atherosclerosis is the usual cause of the tear rather than medial weakness and haemodynamic stress
  • Describing CT findings such as an intimal flap instead of pathological features

Short Answer Set 2: Model Answers

Question 11 (6 marks)

Question: Endometrial carcinoma is traditionally divided into type I and type II tumours. Compare type I and type II endometrial carcinoma with respect to histology, precursor lesion, risk factors, molecular alterations and behaviour. (6 marks)

Model answer

Type I (endometrioid)

- Histology: endometrioid adenocarcinoma, usually low grade (FIGO grade 1 or 2), glands with back to back architecture.

- Precursor: atypical hyperplasia / endometrioid intraepithelial neoplasia.

- Risk factors: unopposed oestrogen (obesity, anovulation, polycystic ovary syndrome, oestrogen-secreting tumours, tamoxifen), diabetes; perimenopausal and early postmenopausal women.

- Molecular: PTEN loss, PIK3CA, KRAS, CTNNB1 mutations; mismatch repair deficiency / microsatellite instability (MLH1 promoter methylation, Lynch syndrome).

- Behaviour: myometrial invasion gradual, lymphatic spread, good prognosis.

Type II (non-endometrioid)

- Histology: serous carcinoma (also clear cell, carcinosarcoma), high grade.

- Precursor: serous endometrial intraepithelial carcinoma in atrophic endometrium.

- Risk factors: older age, not oestrogen driven, atrophic background.

- Molecular: TP53 mutation, p53 abnormal immunostaining, chromosomal instability, often HER2 amplification.

- Behaviour: early deep invasion and peritoneal and extrauterine spread, poor prognosis.

Molecular groups (POLE mutated, mismatch repair deficient, p53 abnormal, no specific molecular profile) now refine prognosis.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Type I endometrioid histology, usually low grade; type II serous or clear cell, high grade (1)
  • Precursor lesions: atypical hyperplasia / EIN versus serous endometrial intraepithelial carcinoma (1)
  • Type I linked to unopposed oestrogen and obesity; type II to older age and atrophic endometrium (1)
  • Type I molecular alterations (PTEN, PIK3CA, KRAS, CTNNB1, MMR deficiency) (1)
  • Type II TP53 mutation (1)
  • Behaviour and prognosis: indolent and favourable versus aggressive with early extrauterine spread (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating that type II tumours are oestrogen driven
  • Attributing TP53 mutation to endometrioid carcinoma as the typical lesion
  • Omitting the precursor lesions
  • Giving imaging features instead of pathology

Question 12 (6 marks)

Question: A core biopsy of a 55 year old woman's breast shows invasive breast carcinoma of no special type with an adjacent component of ductal carcinoma in situ. Describe how each component is graded and the biomarkers assessed on the invasive tumour. (6 marks)

Model answer

Invasive carcinoma: Nottingham (modified Scarff-Bloom-Richardson) grade

- Three components each scored 1 to 3: tubule / gland formation, nuclear pleomorphism, mitotic count (per defined field area).

- Total score 3 to 5 = grade 1, 6 to 7 = grade 2, 8 to 9 = grade 3.

DCIS: neoplastic ductal epithelium confined by the basement membrane with a preserved myoepithelial layer.

- Graded on nuclear grade: low, intermediate or high.

- Necrosis (comedo type) and architecture (solid, cribriform, papillary, micropapillary) are recorded; high nuclear grade DCIS usually has necrosis and calcification.

Biomarkers on the invasive tumour

- Oestrogen receptor and progesterone receptor by immunohistochemistry (nuclear staining, percentage of cells and intensity).

- HER2: immunohistochemistry 3+ is positive; 2+ is equivocal and needs in situ hybridisation for gene amplification.

- Ki-67 proliferation index may be reported.

These define surrogate subtypes: luminal A and B (ER positive), HER2 enriched, and triple negative (ER, PR and HER2 negative), which guide endocrine, anti-HER2 and chemotherapy decisions.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Nottingham grade uses tubule formation, nuclear pleomorphism and mitotic count (1)
  • Each scored 1 to 3 with total thresholds (3 to 5, 6 to 7, 8 to 9) giving grades 1 to 3 (1)
  • DCIS confined by basement membrane, no stromal invasion (1)
  • DCIS graded by nuclear grade (low, intermediate, high) with necrosis and architecture noted (1)
  • ER, PR and HER2 assessed (HER2 by IHC with ISH for equivocal 2+) (1)
  • Link to molecular surrogate subtypes or therapy (luminal, HER2 enriched, triple negative) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Using nuclear grade alone for invasive carcinoma
  • Stating HER2 2+ is positive without in situ hybridisation
  • Grading DCIS with the Nottingham score
  • Confusing DCIS with lobular carcinoma in situ

Question 13 (6 marks)

Question: A 45 year old man has a slowly enlarging painless mass in the superficial lobe of the parotid gland, excised and diagnosed as pleomorphic adenoma. Define the tumour and describe its site, pathology, molecular features and behaviour. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definition

- Benign salivary gland neoplasm composed of epithelial and myoepithelial cells in a myxoid, chondromyxoid or hyaline stroma; the most common salivary tumour.

Site and presentation

- Most arise in the superficial lobe of the parotid gland (also submandibular gland and minor salivary glands, e.g. palate); slowly enlarging painless mass, most often in adults aged 30 to 60 years, with a female predominance.

Macroscopic

- Well circumscribed, lobulated, firm, white to grey cut surface, often glistening myxoid or cartilaginous areas; pseudocapsule is thin and may be incomplete.

Microscopic

- Ducts and sheets of epithelial and myoepithelial cells with a variable mesenchymal-like stroma; squamous metaplasia or calcification can occur.

Molecular

- Rearrangements of PLAG1 or HMGA2.

Behaviour

- Tumour may extend through the pseudocapsule as satellite nodules, so enucleation causes recurrence; surgery is a superficial parotidectomy with facial nerve preservation.

- Carcinoma ex pleomorphic adenoma develops in a small proportion, more likely in long standing or recurrent tumours.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Definition: benign mixed epithelial and myoepithelial tumour with myxochondroid stroma, most common salivary tumour (1)
  • Site: parotid superficial lobe most common (1)
  • Macroscopic: well circumscribed, lobulated, firm with pseudocapsule (1)
  • Microscopic: epithelial and myoepithelial cells in myxoid or chondroid stroma (1)
  • Molecular: PLAG1 or HMGA2 rearrangement (1)
  • Recurrence via capsular penetration and satellite nodules, and risk of carcinoma ex pleomorphic adenoma (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling it a mixed tumour of ectodermal and mesodermal origin rather than epithelial and myoepithelial
  • Placing most tumours in the deep lobe
  • Stating the capsule is complete and recurrence is therefore rare
  • Confusing with Warthin tumour

Question 14 (6 marks)

Question: A 58 year old woman has a dural based, well circumscribed extra-axial mass excised at craniotomy and diagnosed as meningioma. Describe the cell of origin, genetics, microscopic features and WHO CNS5 grading. (6 marks)

Model answer

Origin and risk

- Arise from arachnoid cap (meningothelial) cells; more common in women; risk factors include prior ionising radiation and neurofibromatosis type 2.

Genetics

- NF2 mutation / chromosome 22q loss is the most common alteration; other mutations include TRAF7, KLF4, AKT1 and SMO in NF2-wild-type tumours.

Macroscopic

- Dural based, well circumscribed, globular or en plaque.

Microscopic

- Meningothelial whorls, psammoma bodies, intranuclear pseudoinclusions; immunohistochemistry EMA and SSTR2A positive.

WHO CNS5 grading

- Grade 1: most subtypes (meningothelial, fibrous, transitional, psammomatous, secretory, others).

- Grade 2: atypical meningioma (4 to 19 mitoses per 10 high power fields, or brain invasion), also chordoid and clear cell subtypes.

- Grade 3: anaplastic meningioma (20 or more mitoses per 10 high power fields), or TERT promoter mutation, or homozygous CDKN2A/B deletion; papillary and rhabdoid subtypes are graded by these criteria in CNS5.

Higher grade predicts recurrence.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Arachnoid cap cell origin, with risk factor (radiation or NF2) (1)
  • NF2 / chromosome 22 alteration as the commonest genetic change (1)
  • Microscopic features: whorls, psammoma bodies, EMA positive (1)
  • Grade 1 as the majority and benign pattern (1)
  • Grade 2 criteria (4 to 19 mitoses per 10 HPF, or brain invasion) (1)
  • Grade 3 criteria (20 or more mitoses, or TERT promoter / CDKN2A/B deletion) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating meningiomas arise from the pia or from astrocytes
  • Using the 2016 criteria (three of five atypical features; papillary and rhabdoid automatically grade 3) and omitting TERT promoter mutation and CDKN2A/B deletion as grade 3 criteria
  • Giving dural tail and enhancement features instead of pathology
  • Stating mitotic thresholds incorrectly

Question 15 (6 marks)

Question: Outline the revised Atlanta 2012 classification of acute pancreatitis, including its diagnostic criteria, morphological types, severity categories and the peripancreatic collections that may develop. (6 marks)

Model answer

Diagnosis (any two of three)

- Characteristic upper abdominal pain.

- Serum lipase or amylase more than three times the upper limit of normal.

- Characteristic findings of acute pancreatitis on cross sectional imaging.

Morphological types

- Interstitial oedematous pancreatitis: diffuse or focal enlargement with homogeneous enhancement; most cases.

- Necrotising pancreatitis: necrosis of pancreatic parenchyma and/or peripancreatic tissue.

Severity

- Mild: no organ failure and no local or systemic complications.

- Moderately severe: transient organ failure (under 48 hours) and/or local or systemic complications.

- Severe: persistent organ failure (over 48 hours).

Collections

- Within 4 weeks of interstitial pancreatitis: acute peripancreatic fluid collection.

- Within 4 weeks of necrotising pancreatitis: acute necrotic collection.

- After 4 weeks: pseudocyst (encapsulated fluid, little or no solid material) and walled-off necrosis (encapsulated necrosis).

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Diagnosis requires two of three criteria (pain, enzymes over three times normal, imaging) (1)
  • Interstitial oedematous versus necrotising types (1)
  • Mild severity defined by absence of organ failure and complications (1)
  • Moderately severe and severe defined by transient versus persistent (over 48 hours) organ failure (1)
  • Early collections: acute peripancreatic fluid collection and acute necrotic collection (under 4 weeks) (1)
  • Late collections: pseudocyst and walled-off necrosis (over 4 weeks) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Using the old 1992 term 'infected pseudocyst' or 'phlegmon'
  • Defining severity by imaging necrosis alone rather than organ failure
  • Stating pseudocysts can occur within 2 weeks
  • Confusing amylase threshold with twice normal

Question 16 (6 marks)

Question: A 35 year old woman has bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and a lymph node biopsy shows granulomatous inflammation consistent with sarcoidosis. Outline the pathogenesis and the pathological features of sarcoidosis. (6 marks)

Model answer

Pathogenesis

- Multisystem disease of unknown cause; exaggerated cell mediated immune response in genetically susceptible individuals to an unidentified antigen.

- Activated CD4 helper T cells (Th1 and Th17) release interferon gamma and cytokines, activating macrophages and driving granuloma formation; TNF is important.

Pathology

- Discrete non-necrotising (non-caseating) epithelioid granulomas with multinucleated giant cells and a rim of lymphocytes and fibroblasts, often with little or no necrosis.

- Giant cells may contain Schaumann bodies (calcified conchoid bodies) and asteroid bodies.

- Granulomas lie along lymphatics (perilymphatic distribution in lung: bronchovascular bundles, septa, pleura) and may heal with hyalinised fibrosis.

- Diagnosis is by exclusion of other granulomatous disease, notably mycobacterial and fungal infection.

Organ involvement and associations

- Lung and hilar and mediastinal nodes most often; also skin, eye, liver, spleen, bone marrow.

- Raised serum ACE and hypercalcaemia (macrophage 1-alpha hydroxylase making calcitriol).

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Immune mediated reaction to an unidentified antigen in susceptible hosts (1)
  • CD4 Th1 / Th17 cells, interferon gamma and macrophage activation forming granulomas (1)
  • Non-caseating epithelioid granulomas with giant cells (1)
  • Schaumann or asteroid bodies mentioned (0.5)
  • Perilymphatic distribution in lung and lymph node involvement (1)
  • Exclusion of infection (mycobacteria, fungi) required for diagnosis (1)
  • Systemic associations: raised ACE, hypercalcaemia, extrathoracic organs (0.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing caseating necrosis as typical
  • Stating Schaumann bodies are specific for sarcoidosis
  • Placing granulomas in an alveolar airspace distribution rather than perilymphatic
  • Giving imaging patterns in place of pathology

Question 17 (6 marks)

Question: A 28 year old man has an epiphyseal lytic lesion of the distal femur and biopsy shows giant cell tumour of bone. List six pathological features of this tumour, including usual site, genetic alteration and behaviour. (6 marks)

Model answer

1. Site and age: arises in the epiphysis, extending into the metaphysis, of a skeletally mature patient (after physeal closure), typically aged 20 to 40 years; commonest around the knee (distal femur, proximal tibia), also distal radius and sacrum.

2. Classification: benign but locally aggressive neoplasm (WHO soft tissue and bone, 5th edition).

3. Macroscopic: soft, brown to red, haemorrhagic tumour with cystic change; may erode cortex and extend into soft tissue.

4. Microscopic: sheets of mononuclear ovoid stromal cells with numerous osteoclast-like multinucleated giant cells whose nuclei resemble those of the stromal cells.

5. Molecular: H3F3A (histone H3.3) G34W mutation in the neoplastic mononuclear cells; the stromal cells express RANKL, recruiting the reactive osteoclast-like cells.

6. Behaviour: local recurrence after curettage is common; secondary aneurysmal bone cyst change may occur; rarely pulmonary metastases, and denosumab (RANKL inhibitor) is used in unresectable disease.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Epiphyseal location in skeletally mature adult (20 to 40 years) (1)
  • Common sites: around the knee, distal radius, sacrum (1)
  • Macroscopic: brown haemorrhagic tumour with cystic degeneration (1)
  • Microscopic: mononuclear stromal cells with osteoclast-like giant cells (1)
  • H3F3A G34W mutation and RANKL expression by stromal cells (1)
  • Benign but locally aggressive, recurrence, rare lung metastases (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Saying the giant cells are the neoplastic cells
  • Placing the tumour in the diaphysis or in a skeletally immature patient
  • Stating that malignant transformation is common
  • Giving radiographic appearance (soap bubble) instead of pathology

Question 18 (6 marks)

Question: Apoptosis is a regulated form of cell death distinct from necrosis. Outline the intrinsic and extrinsic pathways of apoptosis, the shared execution phase and the morphological features of an apoptotic cell. (6 marks)

Model answer

Apoptosis is regulated cell death that does not provoke inflammation.

Intrinsic (mitochondrial) pathway

- Triggered by growth factor withdrawal, DNA damage, misfolded proteins.

- Sensor BH3-only proteins activate BAX and BAK, which cause mitochondrial outer membrane permeabilisation; anti-apoptotic BCL2 and BCL-XL oppose this.

- Cytochrome c is released, binds APAF1 to form the apoptosome, activating caspase 9.

Extrinsic (death receptor) pathway

- Ligand binding of death receptors (Fas / CD95 by Fas ligand, TNF receptor type 1) recruits the adaptor FADD, which activates caspase 8; FLIP inhibits it.

Execution phase

- Both pathways activate executioner caspases (3, 6 and 7), which cleave nuclear and cytoskeletal proteins and activate endonucleases producing DNA fragmentation.

Morphology

- Cell shrinkage, nuclear chromatin condensation and fragmentation, membrane blebbing and formation of apoptotic bodies.

- Phosphatidylserine appears on the outer membrane, so phagocytes clear the cell.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Intrinsic triggers and BCL2 family regulation (BAX/BAK, BCL2) (1)
  • Cytochrome c release, apoptosome with APAF1, caspase 9 (1)
  • Extrinsic pathway: death receptors (Fas, TNFR1) and FADD, caspase 8 (1)
  • Executioner caspases (3, 6, 7) common to both pathways (1)
  • Morphology: cell shrinkage, chromatin condensation, apoptotic bodies (1)
  • Phosphatidylserine exposure and phagocytic clearance without inflammation (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Confusing apoptosis with necrosis features such as cell swelling and inflammation
  • Stating caspase 9 acts in the extrinsic pathway
  • Stating BCL2 promotes apoptosis
  • Omitting the executioner caspases

Question 19 (6 marks)

Question: A 32 year old woman has a thyroid nodule and cervical lymphadenopathy, and fine needle aspiration suggests papillary thyroid carcinoma. Describe the pathology, including risk factors, microscopic features, molecular alterations and spread. (6 marks)

Model answer

Epidemiology and risk

- Commonest thyroid malignancy; more common in women; risk factors include ionising radiation exposure in childhood.

Macroscopic

- Solitary or multifocal, firm, poorly circumscribed or infiltrative white tumour, may be cystic or contain calcification.

Microscopic

- Branching papillae with fibrovascular cores, though follicular architecture may predominate in some variants.

- Diagnosis rests on nuclear features: enlargement, overlapping and crowding, optically clear (ground glass) chromatin, nuclear grooves and intranuclear pseudoinclusions.

- Psammoma bodies (laminated calcified bodies) may be present.

Molecular

- BRAF V600E mutation is most common; RET/PTC rearrangements are more frequent in radiation associated tumours; RAS mutations occur in follicular variant.

Spread and prognosis

- Spreads by lymphatics to cervical lymph nodes, which may be the presenting feature; haematogenous spread (lung, bone) is uncommon.

- Excellent overall prognosis; worse with older age, extrathyroidal extension and aggressive variants such as tall cell.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Risk factor: ionising radiation (and commonest thyroid cancer) (1)
  • Macroscopic: firm, infiltrative, may be multifocal or cystic (1)
  • Papillae with fibrovascular cores (1)
  • Nuclear features: clear nuclei, grooves, pseudoinclusions, overlapping (1)
  • Molecular: BRAF V600E and RET/PTC (1)
  • Lymphatic spread to cervical nodes with good prognosis (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Basing diagnosis on papillary architecture alone rather than nuclear features
  • Stating spread is mainly haematogenous
  • Attributing RET/PTC and BRAF to follicular carcinoma
  • Stating psammoma bodies are diagnostic of malignancy in all sites

Question 20 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the pathology of classic Hodgkin lymphoma, including the characteristic neoplastic cell and its immunophenotype, the histological subtypes and the pattern of spread. (6 marks)

Model answer

Neoplastic cell

- Reed-Sternberg cell (large binucleate or bilobed cell with prominent eosinophilic nucleoli, owl eye appearance) and mononuclear Hodgkin variants, derived from germinal centre B cells, scattered in a reactive background of lymphocytes, eosinophils, plasma cells and histiocytes.

- Immunophenotype: CD30 positive, CD15 usually positive, PAX5 weakly positive; CD45 negative and CD20 usually negative or weak.

- Epstein-Barr virus is found in a proportion, especially mixed cellularity.

Subtypes (WHO)

- Nodular sclerosis: commonest in developed countries; young adults, mediastinal nodes; fibrous bands and lacunar cells.

- Mixed cellularity: older patients, abundant mixed infiltrate.

- Lymphocyte rich: few Reed-Sternberg cells among many lymphocytes.

- Lymphocyte depleted: rare, advanced, many Reed-Sternberg cells.

Spread

- Arises in a single nodal group and spreads contiguously along adjacent nodal chains (unlike non-Hodgkin lymphoma); staged by Ann Arbor / Lugano system with B symptoms noted.

Nodular lymphocyte predominant Hodgkin lymphoma is a distinct entity (CD20 positive, CD15 and CD30 negative).

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Reed-Sternberg cell morphology (binucleate, prominent nucleoli) in a reactive background (1)
  • Immunophenotype CD30 and CD15 positive, CD45 negative, CD20 usually negative (1)
  • Nodular sclerosis subtype with lacunar cells and fibrous bands (1)
  • Other classic subtypes: mixed cellularity, lymphocyte rich, lymphocyte depleted (1)
  • Contiguous nodal spread and staging by Ann Arbor / Lugano (1)
  • EBV association or distinction from nodular lymphocyte predominant type (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating Reed-Sternberg cells are CD20 and CD45 positive in classic disease
  • Describing non-contiguous nodal spread as typical
  • Listing nodular lymphocyte predominant Hodgkin lymphoma as a classic subtype
  • Stating the Reed-Sternberg cell is a T cell

Short Answer Set 3: Model Answers

Question 21 (6 marks)

Question: A 34 year old woman has a high grade squamous intraepithelial lesion on cervical screening. Outline the role of human papillomavirus in the pathogenesis of cervical carcinoma and describe the histological grading of cervical intraepithelial neoplasia (CIN). (6 marks)

Model answer

Aetiology: persistent infection with high risk HPV, mainly types 16 and 18 (together about 70% of cancers), also 31, 33 and 45. Low risk types 6 and 11 cause condylomata.

Site: basal and parabasal cells of the metaplastic epithelium at the transformation zone (squamocolumnar junction).

Pathogenesis: most infections are cleared by the immune system within one to two years. Persistence allows viral DNA to integrate into the host genome with loss of E2 regulation. E6 protein degrades p53, and E7 protein binds and inactivates RB, releasing E2F, so cell cycle control and apoptosis are lost. Compensatory p16 overexpression results. Genomic instability and further mutations follow, with progression over years.

Cofactors: early sexual debut, multiple partners, smoking, immunosuppression such as HIV, high parity.

CIN grading:

1. CIN 1 (low grade, LSIL): dysplasia confined to the lower third of the epithelium.

2. CIN 2: dysplasia extending into the lower two thirds.

3. CIN 3: full thickness dysplasia without invasion (carcinoma in situ). CIN 2 and 3 are grouped as HSIL.

Invasion through the basement membrane gives squamous cell carcinoma, the most common type, with adenocarcinoma next. Vaccination targets the high risk types.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • High risk HPV types 16 and 18 named as the main causes (1)
  • Infection of basal cells at the transformation zone with persistence required (1)
  • E6 inactivates p53 and E7 inactivates RB (1.5)
  • CIN 1 lower third, CIN 2 lower two thirds, CIN 3 full thickness (1.5)
  • Correlation of CIN 1 with LSIL and CIN 2 and 3 with HSIL, or progression to invasive squamous carcinoma (0.5)
  • Cofactors such as smoking and immunosuppression (0.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating that HPV types 6 and 11 are the main cause of cervical cancer
  • Describing CIN grades by cell type rather than by the proportion of epithelial thickness involved
  • Omitting the role of E6 and E7 in disabling p53 and RB

Question 22 (6 marks)

Question: Compare the pathological features of a fibroadenoma and a phyllodes tumour of the breast, including the basis for grading phyllodes tumours and their behaviour. (6 marks)

Model answer

Fibroadenoma

1. Commonest benign breast tumour, mostly women under 30 and hormone responsive.

2. Macro: well circumscribed, rubbery, mobile, usually 1 to 3 cm, grey white bulging cut surface with slit-like spaces.

3. Micro: biphasic. Proliferation of both glandular epithelium and loose, hypocellular intralobular stroma, in pericanalicular or intracanalicular patterns. No stromal atypia and no mitoses. Older lesions hyalinise and calcify.

4. Behaviour: benign, no malignant potential in itself, local excision only if needed.

Phyllodes tumour

1. Rare, usually women in their 40s to 50s, often large and rapidly growing.

2. Macro: bulky, bosselated, cut surface shows leaf-like clefts.

3. Micro: leaf-like architecture with epithelium-lined clefts, but stroma is hypercellular, often condensed beneath the epithelium.

4. Graded benign, borderline or malignant by stromal cellularity, atypia, mitotic count, stromal overgrowth and tumour margin.

5. Behaviour: tends to recur locally after inadequate excision, so wide excision is needed. Malignant tumours metastasise haematogenously, mainly to lung and bone, rarely to lymph nodes.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Fibroadenoma: age, circumscribed rubbery mobile gross appearance (1)
  • Fibroadenoma: biphasic epithelial and hypocellular stromal proliferation without atypia or mitoses (1.5)
  • Phyllodes: older age, large bulky tumour with leaf-like clefts (1)
  • Phyllodes: stromal hypercellularity as the distinguishing microscopic feature (1)
  • Grading by stromal cellularity, atypia, mitoses, overgrowth and margin into three grades (1)
  • Local recurrence and haematogenous (not nodal) metastasis of malignant tumours (0.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating that phyllodes tumours commonly metastasise to axillary lymph nodes
  • Describing the epithelium rather than the stroma as the component that determines grade
  • Assuming that leaf-like architecture alone proves phyllodes tumour, since cellular fibroadenomas can have similar patterns

Question 23 (6 marks)

Question: Compare the macroscopic and microscopic pathology of Crohn disease and ulcerative colitis, and give two complications specific to each. (6 marks)

Model answer

Crohn disease

1. Distribution: any part of the gut, most often terminal ileum and right colon. Skip lesions with normal bowel between affected segments. Rectum often spared.

2. Macro: transmural inflammation, deep linear fissuring ulcers, cobblestone mucosa, thick wall, narrowed lumen from fibrosis and stricturing, creeping fat and serositis.

3. Micro: transmural lymphoid aggregates, fissures, submucosal fibrosis, and non-caseating granulomas in a minority of cases (about one third).

4. Complications: strictures and obstruction, fistulae (enteroenteric, enterovesical, perianal), abscesses, small bowel malabsorption.

Ulcerative colitis

1. Distribution: always involves the rectum with continuous proximal extension to a variable extent. Backwash ileitis may occur.

2. Macro: diffuse red granular mucosa, broad-based ulcers, pseudopolyps, mucosal atrophy in chronic disease. Wall of normal thickness.

3. Micro: inflammation limited to mucosa and superficial submucosa, crypt abscesses, crypt distortion and branching, no granulomas.

4. Complications: toxic megacolon, perforation, and a higher colorectal cancer risk related to disease extent and duration. Primary sclerosing cholangitis is more strongly associated.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Distribution: skip lesions and any site versus rectum first and continuous (1)
  • Depth: transmural versus mucosal and submucosal (1)
  • Macroscopic features: fissures, cobblestoning, creeping fat versus pseudopolyps and granular mucosa (1.5)
  • Microscopy: granulomas in Crohn disease versus crypt abscesses and crypt distortion in ulcerative colitis (1.5)
  • Two valid complications specific to each disease (e.g. fistula, stricture; toxic megacolon, carcinoma) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Claiming that granulomas are present in all Crohn disease
  • Attributing skip lesions or transmural disease to ulcerative colitis
  • Listing fistulae as a feature of ulcerative colitis

Question 24 (6 marks)

Question: List six asbestos-related diseases of the chest and state the key pathological feature of each. (6 marks)

Model answer

1. Pleural plaques: discrete areas of hyaline collagen on the parietal pleura, often calcified, typically over the diaphragm and lower chest wall, sparing the costophrenic angles and apices. Not premalignant.

2. Benign asbestos pleural effusion: exudative, often bloody, occurring early (within about 10 years of exposure) and diagnosis of exclusion.

3. Diffuse pleural thickening: visceral pleural fibrosis, often with adhesions and blunting of the costophrenic angle, which may restrict lung expansion.

4. Rounded atelectasis: folded lung with an infolded fibrotic pleura producing a rounded subpleural mass.

5. Asbestosis: diffuse interstitial pulmonary fibrosis that begins in the lower lobes and subpleural zones. Asbestos bodies (golden brown beaded fibres with an iron-protein coat, Prussian blue positive) are seen. Latency is generally 15 or more years and dose related.

6. Bronchogenic carcinoma: risk is multiplied with cigarette smoking (synergistic), any histological type, usually lower lobes in association with asbestosis.

7. Malignant mesothelioma: pleural tumour with encasing rind, epithelioid, sarcomatoid or biphasic types, latency usually over 30 years, amphibole fibres such as crocidolite are most potent, and there is no safe threshold.

(Any six accepted.)

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Six valid diseases named, 0.5 mark each (3)
  • Accurate key pathological feature for each disease listed, 0.5 mark each (3)

Common candidate errors

  • Listing silicosis or coal workers pneumoconiosis rather than asbestos diseases
  • Describing pleural plaques as premalignant or as involving the visceral pleura
  • Stating that chrysotile is the most carcinogenic fibre type

Question 25 (6 marks)

Question: A 70 year old man has an isolated raised serum alkaline phosphatase with normal calcium. Describe the pathology of Paget disease of bone, including its phases and complications. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definition: disordered bone remodelling from excessive osteoclastic resorption followed by chaotic, excessive osteoblastic formation of structurally abnormal bone.

Epidemiology and aetiology: usually over 55 years, more common in people of Anglo-European descent. Mutations in SQSTM1 (p62) are the commonest genetic cause; a viral trigger (paramyxovirus) has been proposed but is unproven.

Distribution: monostotic or polyostotic, axial skeleton favoured: pelvis, lumbar spine, skull, femur and tibia.

Phases:

1. Osteolytic: florid osteoclastic resorption by large, multinucleate osteoclasts.

2. Mixed: osteoblastic and osteoclastic activity with fibrovascular marrow and a mosaic pattern of woven and lamellar bone with prominent cement lines.

3. Sclerotic (burnt out): dense, thick, sclerotic bone with little cellular activity.

Macro: enlarged, thickened, sclerotic bone that is mechanically weak.

Complications: pathological chalkstick fractures, secondary osteoarthritis, nerve compression such as deafness from skull base involvement, high output cardiac failure from marrow vascularity, and osteosarcoma (under 1% of cases), rarely giant cell tumour.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Definition as increased resorption followed by disorganised bone formation (1)
  • Distribution, mainly axial skeleton and older adults (0.5)
  • Three phases correctly described (lytic, mixed, sclerotic) (1.5)
  • Mosaic pattern or cement lines and large multinucleate osteoclasts (1)
  • Weak, thickened, abnormal bone (0.5)
  • Three valid complications including osteosarcoma (1.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing osteoporosis-like loss of bone mass rather than thick, sclerotic and weak bone
  • Stating that serum calcium is typically raised
  • Giving osteosarcoma as a common rather than rare complication

Question 26 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the pathological evolution of an ischaemic cerebral infarct over the first weeks after the event. (6 marks)

Model answer

Mechanisms: thrombosis on atherosclerosis, embolism (cardiac, for example atrial fibrillation, or carotid) and small vessel disease causing lacunar infarcts. An ischaemic core is surrounded by a salvageable penumbra; watershed zones are vulnerable in hypoperfusion.

Timeline:

1. First 6 to 12 hours: little or no change macroscopically; cytotoxic oedema develops.

2. 12 to 24 hours: red (eosinophilic) neurons with microvacuolation; pyknotic nuclei.

3. 24 to 72 hours: neutrophil infiltration; tissue becomes pale, soft with a blurred grey-white junction, and swelling peaks at 3 to 5 days (vasogenic oedema) with risk of herniation.

4. 3 to 5 days: macrophage (microglial) infiltration as dead tissue is phagocytosed; the tissue is liquefying.

5. 1 to 2 weeks: liquefactive necrosis; reactive astrocytosis and neovascularisation at the edge (granulation-like tissue).

6. Weeks to months: a fluid-filled cystic cavity lined by a glial scar remains.

Embolic infarcts may undergo haemorrhagic transformation as reperfusion occurs through damaged vessels.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Mechanism of infarction (thrombotic, embolic or small vessel) and penumbra (1)
  • Early changes: red neurons at 12 to 24 hours (1)
  • Neutrophils at 24 to 72 hours with macroscopic softening and oedema (1)
  • Macrophage phase at about 3 to 5 days (1)
  • Liquefactive necrosis with gliosis and angiogenesis at 1 to 2 weeks (1)
  • End stage cystic cavity with glial scar (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing coagulative rather than liquefactive necrosis as the end result in brain
  • Placing neutrophils before red neurons or macrophages before neutrophils
  • Stating that infarcts are macroscopically obvious within hours

Question 27 (6 marks)

Question: Outline the vascular and cellular events of acute inflammation and name the principal chemical mediators involved. (6 marks)

Model answer

Vascular events:

1. Transient arteriolar vasoconstriction, then vasodilation (histamine, nitric oxide) increasing blood flow.

2. Increased vascular permeability, giving protein-rich exudate. Mechanisms: endothelial contraction (histamine, bradykinin, leukotrienes C4, D4, E4), direct endothelial injury, leukocyte-mediated injury, and increased transcytosis (VEGF).

3. Slowing of flow (stasis) with concentration of red cells.

Cellular events:

1. Margination of leukocytes.

2. Rolling mediated by selectins (E and P selectin on endothelium, L selectin on leukocytes).

3. Firm adhesion via integrins (LFA-1, Mac-1) binding ICAM-1, and VLA-4 binding VCAM-1.

4. Transmigration between endothelial cells, mediated by PECAM-1 (CD31).

5. Chemotaxis along gradients of C5a, IL-8 (CXCL8), leukotriene B4 and bacterial products.

6. Phagocytosis and killing by reactive oxygen species (NADPH oxidase), myeloperoxidase and enzymes.

Mediators: histamine, prostaglandins, leukotrienes, cytokines TNF and IL-1, complement (C3a, C5a), kinins and platelet activating factor.

Neutrophils predominate in the first 6 to 24 hours, then monocytes.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Vasodilation and increased permeability with mechanisms of leakage (1.5)
  • Exudate formation and stasis (0.5)
  • Margination, rolling via selectins and adhesion via integrins (1.5)
  • Transmigration and chemotaxis with named chemoattractants (1)
  • Phagocytosis and intracellular killing (0.5)
  • At least four correct mediators with their actions (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Confusing selectins (rolling) with integrins (firm adhesion)
  • Omitting increased permeability as a separate event from vasodilation
  • Listing cell types without describing the sequence of events

Question 28 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the pathology of prostatic acinar adenocarcinoma, the ISUP grade groups and the T stage categories of the TNM staging system. (6 marks)

Model answer

Pathology: most arise in the peripheral zone, often multifocal. Microscopy shows crowded small glands lacking a basal cell layer, prominent nucleoli, and perineural invasion; AMACR is positive and basal markers (p63, high molecular weight keratin) are negative. Spread is local to seminal vesicles and bladder neck, to pelvic nodes, and to bone, usually osteoblastic metastases.

ISUP grade groups (Gleason score, primary plus secondary pattern):

1. Grade group 1: Gleason 3+3=6.

2. Grade group 2: Gleason 3+4=7.

3. Grade group 3: Gleason 4+3=7.

4. Grade group 4: Gleason 8.

5. Grade group 5: Gleason 9 or 10.

TNM T categories:

- T1: clinically inapparent: T1a incidental in 5% or less of resected tissue, T1b more than 5%, T1c needle biopsy.

- T2: confined to the prostate: T2a half of one lobe or less, T2b more than half of one lobe, T2c both lobes.

- T3: extraprostatic extension (T3a) or seminal vesicle invasion (T3b).

- T4: fixed or invading adjacent structures other than seminal vesicles, for example bladder neck, rectum, levator ani or pelvic wall.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Peripheral zone origin and gland architecture lacking basal cells (1)
  • Perineural invasion, AMACR or basal marker staining and sites of metastasis (0.5)
  • Correct five ISUP grade groups with Gleason equivalents (2)
  • T1 and T2 subdivisions (1)
  • T3 and T4 definitions (1)
  • Distinction of grade group 2 (3+4) from 3 (4+3) (0.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Treating 3+4 and 4+3 as the same grade group
  • Placing seminal vesicle invasion in T4 rather than T3b
  • Stating that most cancers arise in the transition zone

Question 29 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the macroscopic and microscopic evolution of a transmural myocardial infarct from the first hours to the formation of a scar, and name the complications associated with each stage. (6 marks)

Model answer

Cause: acute coronary plaque rupture with occlusive thrombus; necrosis begins after about 30 minutes of ischaemia, with coagulative necrosis.

1. 0 to 4 hours: no gross change; early ultrastructural changes only. Complication: arrhythmia, including ventricular fibrillation.

2. 4 to 12 hours: no gross change; microscopy shows early coagulative necrosis, oedema, haemorrhage and wavy fibres. 12 to 24 hours: gross pallor to dark mottling, with hypereosinophilic myocytes and contraction band necrosis in reperfused areas.

3. 1 to 3 days: yellow-tan mottled centre; dense neutrophil infiltrate and loss of nuclei. Complication: fibrinous pericarditis.

4. 3 to 7 days: central softening with a hyperaemic border; neutrophils disappear and macrophages remove dead myocytes. Peak softening gives the highest risk of free wall rupture (tamponade), septal rupture or papillary muscle rupture.

5. 7 to 14 days: red-grey depressed border; granulation tissue with new vessels and fibroblasts. Complication: mural thrombus and thromboembolism.

6. 2 weeks to 2 months or more: collagen deposition, then a white fibrous scar. Complications: ventricular aneurysm, heart failure, Dressler syndrome (weeks later).

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Coagulative necrosis beginning at 4 to 12 hours, with gross pallor or mottling by 12 to 24 hours (1)
  • Neutrophil infiltrate at 1 to 3 days (1)
  • Macrophage removal and softening at 3 to 7 days (1)
  • Granulation tissue at 1 to 2 weeks (1)
  • Collagenous scar by about 2 months (1)
  • At least three stage-appropriate complications (rupture at peak softening, aneurysm late) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Placing rupture risk in the first 24 hours rather than at 3 to 7 days
  • Omitting macrophages as the cells that remove necrotic myocardium
  • Describing fibrous scar within the first week

Question 30 (6 marks)

Question: Compare the pathological features of primary and secondary (post-primary) pulmonary tuberculosis. (6 marks)

Model answer

Common features: Mycobacterium tuberculosis is an acid-fast bacillus (Ziehl-Neelsen stain). The host response is cell-mediated (type IV hypersensitivity) producing caseating granulomas with epithelioid macrophages, Langhans giant cells and central caseous necrosis.

Primary TB

1. First exposure in an unsensitised individual, often children.

2. Site: Ghon focus, a subpleural lesion in the lower part of the upper lobe or upper part of the lower lobe.

3. Spread to hilar lymph nodes gives the Ghon complex.

4. Most heal by fibrosis and calcification (Ranke complex). Occasionally progressive primary TB, lymphohaematogenous spread and miliary TB occur, especially in immunosuppression.

Secondary TB

1. Reactivation of dormant bacilli or reinfection in a sensitised host, often with immune decline.

2. Site: apices of the upper lobes (high oxygen tension).

3. Strong hypersensitivity causes extensive caseation and cavitation; regional nodes are usually spared.

4. Spread by bronchi to give cavitary disease with positive sputum.

5. Complications: haemoptysis, bronchopleural fistula, empyema, miliary disease, laryngeal and intestinal TB from swallowed sputum.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Caseating granulomas with epithelioid cells and Langhans giant cells common to both (1)
  • Primary: unsensitised host, Ghon focus and Ghon complex (1.5)
  • Primary: healing by fibrosis and calcification, or progressive and miliary forms (0.5)
  • Secondary: reactivation or reinfection in sensitised host (1)
  • Secondary: apical upper lobe site with cavitation and sparing of regional nodes (1)
  • Complications of secondary TB (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Placing primary TB at the apex and secondary TB in the lower zones
  • Stating that primary TB typically cavitates
  • Omitting that hilar nodal involvement is a feature of primary rather than secondary disease

Short Answer Set 4: Model Answers

Question 31 (6 marks)

Question: A 64 year old man with long-standing hepatitis C cirrhosis has a new liver mass and a raised serum alpha-fetoprotein. Describe the aetiology, pathology and routes of spread of hepatocellular carcinoma. (6 marks)

Model answer

Aetiology and risk factors:

1. Cirrhosis from any cause: chronic hepatitis B and C, alcohol, metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease, haemochromatosis.

2. Hepatitis B can cause HCC without cirrhosis through viral DNA integration.

3. Aflatoxin B1 exposure, with TP53 mutation.

Macroscopic pathology:

1. Solitary mass, multifocal nodules, or diffusely infiltrative tumour.

2. Soft, tan to green (bile staining) lesion with haemorrhage and necrosis, often arising in a cirrhotic liver.

3. Characteristic tendency to invade portal and hepatic veins, forming tumour thrombus.

Microscopic pathology:

1. Well differentiated tumour: trabecular plates of hepatocyte-like cells, thickened cell plates, bile production and cytoplasmic inclusions.

2. Poorly differentiated tumour: pleomorphic cells, giant cells, loss of trabecular architecture.

3. Arterial blood supply replaces portal supply, with loss of portal tracts.

Spread:

1. Local intrahepatic spread via portal vein branches.

2. Haematogenous spread to lung and bone.

3. Lymphatic spread to portal and para-aortic nodes is less common.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Cirrhosis as the main substrate, with two named causes (1)
  • Hepatitis B without cirrhosis or aflatoxin exposure (1)
  • Macroscopic patterns: solitary, multifocal or infiltrative (1)
  • Bile staining, necrosis or haemorrhage in the mass (0.5)
  • Microscopy: trabecular architecture with pleomorphism (1.5)
  • Vascular invasion and haematogenous spread to lung or bone (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing imaging enhancement patterns instead of the pathology requested
  • Omitting hepatitis B as a cause of HCC in a non-cirrhotic liver
  • Stating that portal venous supply feeds the tumour

Question 32 (6 marks)

Question: Compare oesophageal adenocarcinoma and oesophageal squamous cell carcinoma with respect to risk factors, usual location and precursor lesion. (6 marks)

Model answer

Adenocarcinoma:

1. Risk factors: chronic gastro-oesophageal reflux, Barrett oesophagus, central obesity, male sex, white ethnicity. Smoking is a lesser risk factor.

2. Location: distal third, near the gastro-oesophageal junction.

3. Precursor: Barrett oesophagus (intestinal metaplasia with goblet cells) progressing through low grade then high grade dysplasia to invasive carcinoma.

4. Microscopy: glandular, mucin-producing tumour.

Squamous cell carcinoma:

1. Risk factors: tobacco and alcohol (synergistic), very hot beverages, achalasia, caustic strictures, Plummer-Vinson syndrome, tylosis, prior head and neck cancer, radiotherapy.

2. Location: most often the middle third, then the lower third.

3. Precursor: squamous dysplasia.

4. Microscopy: keratinising squamous cells with intercellular bridges.

Shared features: both present late with dysphagia and weight loss, spread via lymphatics because the oesophagus lacks a serosa and has a rich submucosal lymphatic network.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Adenocarcinoma risk factors (reflux, Barrett, obesity) (1)
  • SCC risk factors (tobacco and alcohol, plus one other) (1)
  • Adenocarcinoma distal location (0.5)
  • SCC mid oesophagus location (0.5)
  • Barrett metaplasia to dysplasia sequence (1.5)
  • SCC squamous dysplasia precursor (0.5)
  • Histology of each tumour type (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reversing the usual locations of the two tumours
  • Stating Barrett oesophagus is a squamous lesion
  • Giving imaging features instead of pathology

Question 33 (6 marks)

Question: Outline the pathogenesis of pre-eclampsia and describe the placental and maternal organ changes. (6 marks)

Model answer

Pathogenesis:

1. Abnormal placentation: inadequate remodelling of the spiral arteries by extravillous trophoblast, leaving narrow, muscular vessels.

2. Placental hypoperfusion and ischaemia, with release of anti-angiogenic factors (soluble fms-like tyrosine kinase 1) and reduced placental growth factor.

3. Widespread maternal endothelial dysfunction, vasoconstriction, increased vascular permeability and activation of coagulation.

Definition: new hypertension after 20 weeks with proteinuria or other organ dysfunction.

Placental changes:

1. Small placenta, infarcts and retroplacental haematoma.

2. Decidual vasculopathy with acute atherosis (fibrinoid necrosis with foam cells).

3. Accelerated villous maturation with syncytial knots.

Maternal organs:

1. Kidney: glomerular endotheliosis.

2. Liver: periportal haemorrhage and necrosis in HELLP syndrome.

3. Brain: oedema and haemorrhage in eclampsia.

Complications: placental abruption, foetal growth restriction, DIC.

Onset is usually in the third trimester; delivery of the placenta is the definitive cure.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Failed spiral artery remodelling by trophoblast (1.5)
  • Placental ischaemia with anti-angiogenic factor release (1)
  • Maternal endothelial dysfunction (1)
  • Placental changes: infarcts, acute atherosis or villous change (1.5)
  • Renal glomerular endotheliosis (0.5)
  • Hepatic or cerebral changes (0.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing only clinical criteria without pathogenesis
  • Attributing the disease to excess rather than inadequate spiral artery remodelling
  • Using the term foetal growth restriction without the placental cause

Question 34 (6 marks)

Question: Define lung adenocarcinoma, list the WHO growth patterns, and name the main targetable molecular drivers. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definition: malignant epithelial tumour with glandular differentiation, mucin production or pneumocyte marker expression (TTF-1, napsin A). It is the commonest lung cancer, usually peripheral, and the commonest type in never-smokers and women.

Precursors: atypical adenomatous hyperplasia and adenocarcinoma in situ (lepidic growth, no invasion).

WHO growth patterns (invasive non-mucinous):

1. Lepidic: tumour cells along intact alveolar walls.

2. Acinar.

3. Papillary.

4. Micropapillary.

5. Solid.

Prognosis is graded by predominant pattern: lepidic is favourable, while micropapillary and solid are unfavourable. Invasive mucinous adenocarcinoma is a separate variant.

Molecular drivers:

1. EGFR mutation (exon 19 deletion, L858R), treated with tyrosine kinase inhibitors.

2. ALK rearrangement.

3. ROS1 rearrangement.

4. KRAS mutation, now targetable for the G12C variant.

5. Others: BRAF V600E, MET exon 14 skipping, RET, NTRK.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Definition with glandular differentiation and TTF-1 (1)
  • Peripheral location or epidemiology (0.5)
  • Precursors AAH and AIS (0.5)
  • Five growth patterns listed (1.5)
  • Grading by predominant pattern (0.5)
  • EGFR with treatment implication (1)
  • ALK and ROS1 plus one other driver (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Listing squamous cell carcinoma features
  • Omitting lepidic pattern
  • Confusing ALK with EGFR as a mutation rather than a rearrangement

Question 35 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the pathogenesis and pathological features of acute and chronic osteomyelitis, including the named complications. (6 marks)

Model answer

Acute osteomyelitis:

1. Causes: haematogenous spread, direct spread from adjacent infection, or contamination after trauma or surgery. Staphylococcus aureus is the commonest organism; Salmonella is associated with sickle cell disease.

2. Haematogenous infection in children begins in the metaphysis, where sluggish sinusoidal flow favours bacterial seeding.

3. Neutrophilic inflammation raises intramedullary pressure, causing ischaemia and bone necrosis.

4. Pus lifts the periosteum and may form a subperiosteal abscess.

5. Dead bone is termed a sequestrum.

Chronic osteomyelitis:

1. Develops when acute disease is inadequately treated.

2. Involucrum: reactive new bone forming around the sequestrum.

3. Cloacae: openings in the involucrum that drain pus, sometimes through sinus tracts to skin.

4. Chronic inflammatory infiltrate with fibrosis.

Complications:

1. Septic arthritis, particularly in infants where the physis is crossed by vessels.

2. Brodie abscess.

3. Pathological fracture.

4. Squamous carcinoma in a long-standing sinus tract.

5. Amyloidosis.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Organism and route of spread (1)
  • Metaphyseal location and mechanism of seeding (1)
  • Neutrophilic inflammation with ischaemic necrosis (1)
  • Sequestrum (0.5)
  • Involucrum and cloaca (1)
  • Subperiosteal abscess or sinus tract (0.5)
  • Two named complications (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing radiological periosteal reaction instead of pathology
  • Confusing sequestrum and involucrum
  • Omitting the metaphysis

Question 36 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the pathology of multiple sclerosis, including the macroscopic distribution of plaques and the microscopic features of acute and chronic lesions. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definition: chronic immune-mediated demyelinating disease of the central nervous system, with relapsing-remitting course in most patients. It is more common in young women and in people living at higher latitudes.

Pathogenesis: autoreactive T cells, mainly Th1 and Th17, and B cells directed against myelin antigens, with genetic (HLA-DRB1*15:01) and environmental factors, notably Epstein-Barr virus.

Macroscopic:

1. Sharply demarcated grey-tan plaques in white matter.

2. Favoured sites: periventricular white matter, optic nerves and chiasm, brainstem, cerebellar peduncles and spinal cord.

3. Plaques arise around small veins, producing the radial distribution perpendicular to the ventricles.

Microscopic:

1. Acute active plaque: perivenular lymphocytes and macrophages containing myelin debris, with oligodendrocyte loss and relative axonal preservation.

2. Chronic plaque: gliosis, sparse inflammation and marked axonal loss, forming a shadow plaque where remyelination is incomplete.

3. Cortical grey matter lesions also occur.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Definition as CNS demyelination (0.5)
