  • Immune pathogenesis (T cells, plus one other factor) (1)
  • Periventricular and other typical white matter sites (1)
  • Perivenular orientation of plaques (0.5)
  • Acute lesion: macrophages with myelin debris and axonal sparing (1.5)
  • Chronic lesion: gliosis and axonal loss (1.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing MRI lesion appearances instead of pathology
  • Stating that peripheral nerves are involved
  • Stating that oligodendrocytes are spared in acute lesions

Question 37 (6 marks)

Question: Define amyloid and list six types of amyloidosis, stating the precursor protein for each. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definition: extracellular deposition of misfolded proteins arranged as beta-pleated sheet fibrils, which stain pink with Congo red and show apple-green birefringence under polarised light. Fibrils are non-branching and about 7.5 to 10 nm wide, accompanied by serum amyloid P component.

Six types:

1. AL (light chain) amyloidosis: immunoglobulin light chains from a plasma cell clone, as in myeloma. Involves heart, kidney, tongue and nerves.

2. AA (secondary, reactive) amyloidosis: serum amyloid A, an acute phase protein, in chronic inflammation such as rheumatoid arthritis. Kidneys are commonly affected.

3. Hereditary transthyretin amyloidosis (ATTRv): mutant transthyretin; neuropathy and cardiomyopathy.

4. Wild-type transthyretin amyloidosis (ATTRwt): normal transthyretin in the elderly, causing cardiac amyloidosis.

5. Dialysis-related amyloidosis: beta-2 microglobulin.

6. Alzheimer disease: amyloid beta from amyloid precursor protein. Other examples include islet amyloid polypeptide in type 2 diabetes and calcitonin in medullary thyroid carcinoma.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Definition: extracellular misfolded protein with beta-pleated sheet structure (1)
  • Congo red staining with apple-green birefringence (1)
  • Six types each with correct precursor protein, 0.5 mark per type (3)
  • Organ involvement for AL or AA (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Naming organ involvement but no precursor protein
  • Stating Congo red stains amyloid blue
  • Confusing AA with AL precursors

Question 38 (6 marks)

Question: A 28 year old man has a painless testicular mass. Classify testicular germ cell tumours and state the pathological features and tumour markers of each major type. (6 marks)

Model answer

Classification (WHO 2022): derived from germ cell neoplasia in situ (GCNIS) are seminoma and non-seminomatous germ cell tumours (NSGCT). Prepubertal-type tumours, such as teratoma and yolk sac tumour in children, are not derived from GCNIS.

Seminoma:

1. Macroscopy: homogeneous, cream to tan, lobulated mass.

2. Microscopy: sheets of uniform clear cells with central nuclei, separated by fibrous septa containing lymphocytes; granulomas may occur.

3. Markers: placental alkaline phosphatase, OCT3/4, CD117. Beta-hCG is mildly raised in a minority.

Non-seminomatous types:

1. Embryonal carcinoma: haemorrhagic, necrotic mass; pleomorphic cells in glands and sheets.

2. Yolk sac tumour: alpha-fetoprotein; Schiller-Duval bodies.

3. Choriocarcinoma: beta-hCG; cytotrophoblast and syncytiotrophoblast, early haematogenous spread.

4. Teratoma: tissues from all three germ layers.

Mixed tumours are common.

Spread: seminoma tends to spread via lymphatics to para-aortic nodes; choriocarcinoma spreads early by blood.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Origin from GCNIS and division into seminoma and NSGCT (1)
  • Seminoma macroscopy and microscopy (1.5)
  • Seminoma markers (0.5)
  • Embryonal carcinoma features (0.5)
  • Yolk sac tumour with AFP and Schiller-Duval bodies (1)
  • Choriocarcinoma with beta-hCG (0.5)
  • Teratoma with three germ layers (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating seminoma secretes alpha-fetoprotein
  • Omitting the mixed tumour category
  • Describing imaging and staging instead of pathology

Question 39 (6 marks)

Question: Outline the pathogenesis of atherosclerosis and describe the features of a complicated plaque. (6 marks)

Model answer

Risk factors: dyslipidaemia, hypertension, smoking, diabetes mellitus, age, male sex, family history, inflammation.

Pathogenesis (response to injury and lipid retention):

1. Endothelial dysfunction at sites of turbulent flow, with increased permeability.

2. Low density lipoprotein enters and is retained in the intima, where it is oxidised.

3. Monocytes adhere, migrate in and become macrophages that engulf oxidised LDL to form foam cells.

4. Fatty streak: the earliest lesion, composed of foam cells.

5. T cell activation, cytokines and growth factor release stimulate smooth muscle cell migration and proliferation, with extracellular matrix synthesis.

6. Mature plaque: a fibrous cap over a necrotic lipid core.

Complicated plaque:

1. Rupture, ulceration or erosion of the cap.

2. Thrombosis on the exposed surface, causing acute occlusion and infarction.

3. Intraplaque haemorrhage and expansion.

4. Calcification and weakening of the media, leading to aneurysm formation.

5. Embolisation of atheromatous debris.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Risk factors, any three (0.5)
  • Endothelial injury and LDL retention with oxidation (1)
  • Monocyte to macrophage to foam cell (1)
  • Fatty streak as early lesion (0.5)
  • Smooth muscle proliferation and fibrous cap formation (1)
  • Necrotic lipid core (0.5)
  • Complications: rupture with thrombosis, haemorrhage, aneurysm, embolism (1.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing only risk factors
  • Omitting the role of inflammation and macrophages
  • Describing the imaging findings of calcified plaque

Question 40 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the pathology of multiple myeloma and explain the mechanisms of its bone, renal and haematological manifestations. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definition: clonal neoplastic proliferation of plasma cells in the bone marrow, usually producing a monoclonal immunoglobulin (paraprotein) and free light chains. It is usually preceded by monoclonal gammopathy of undetermined significance.

Marrow pathology: sheets of atypical plasma cells with eccentric nuclei and perinuclear clearing; Russell bodies (cytoplasmic) and Dutcher bodies (nuclear) are inclusions. Immunophenotype is CD138 positive.

Bone:

1. Myeloma cells release RANKL and other cytokines, which activate osteoclasts and suppress osteoblasts.

2. Punched-out lytic lesions, generalised osteopenia, pathological fractures and hypercalcaemia.

Renal:

1. Light chains (Bence Jones protein) precipitate in distal tubules as casts, forming cast nephropathy.

2. Hypercalcaemia, amyloid deposition and light chain deposition also injure the kidney.

Haematological:

1. Marrow replacement causes normocytic anaemia and other cytopenias.

2. Rouleaux on the blood film from paraprotein coating red cells.

3. Immunosuppression with infections due to hypogammaglobulinaemia of normal immunoglobulins.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Definition: clonal plasma cell neoplasm with paraprotein (1)
  • Marrow plasma cell morphology (1)
  • CD138 or inclusion bodies (0.5)
  • Bone: osteoclast activation by cytokines such as RANKL (1)
  • Renal: light chain cast nephropathy (1)
  • Anaemia from marrow replacement (0.5)
  • Rouleaux or infection susceptibility (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing skeletal survey appearances rather than mechanism
  • Stating the lesions are osteoblastic
  • Naming Reed-Sternberg cells

Short Answer Set 5: Model Answers

Question 41 (6 marks)

Question: A 62 year old man has a screening colonoscopy that removes a 1.5 cm villous polyp, and later a resected sigmoid carcinoma is staged. Describe the adenoma-carcinoma sequence and outline the pathological basis of TNM staging for colorectal carcinoma. (6 marks)

Model answer

Adenoma-carcinoma sequence (chromosomal instability pathway, about 80 percent):

1. Loss of APC (5q) gives early dysplastic crypts and a small adenoma.

2. KRAS mutation promotes growth of a larger adenoma.

3. Loss of 18q (SMAD4, DCC) and then TP53 (17p) marks transition to invasive carcinoma.

4. Risk of malignancy rises with size over 1 cm, villous architecture and high grade dysplasia. Histological types: tubular, tubulovillous, villous.

5. Alternative microsatellite instability pathway: mismatch repair loss (MLH1 promoter hypermethylation, BRAF V600E) through sessile serrated lesions, or germline defects in Lynch syndrome.

Staging (TNM, depth and spread):

- T1 submucosa; T2 muscularis propria; T3 through muscularis propria into pericolorectal tissue; T4a visceral peritoneum surface; T4b direct invasion of adjacent organs.

- N1: 1 to 3 regional nodes; N2: 4 or more.

- M1: distant metastasis, most often liver by portal drainage, then lung.

- Prognosis also depends on margin status, extramural vascular invasion and grade.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • APC loss as the initiating event with small adenoma (1)
  • KRAS then SMAD4/DCC and TP53 loss in correct order (1)
  • Adenoma risk factors (size, villous, high grade dysplasia) (1)
  • Microsatellite instability or serrated pathway named with mechanism (1)
  • T categories by depth of invasion correctly stated (1)
  • N and M categories with node counts and liver as commonest site (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Placing TP53 loss early and APC loss late
  • Stating T3 as invasion of adjacent organs
  • Omitting the microsatellite instability pathway
  • Giving imaging features instead of the pathological basis of staging

Question 42 (6 marks)

Question: Define cirrhosis. List the main causes and describe the macroscopic and microscopic pathology and the principal complications. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definition: diffuse liver disease with bridging fibrosis and conversion of normal architecture into regenerative nodules, usually irreversible in established form.

Causes: chronic alcohol use; chronic hepatitis B and C; metabolic dysfunction associated steatotic liver disease; autoimmune hepatitis; primary biliary cholangitis and primary sclerosing cholangitis; haemochromatosis; Wilson disease; alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency; cryptogenic.

Mechanism: repeated injury activates hepatic stellate cells, which deposit collagen types I and III.

Macroscopy: shrunken, firm, nodular liver with a knobbly surface. Micronodular (under 3 mm, typical of alcohol) or macronodular (over 3 mm, typical of viral hepatitis) or mixed.

Microscopy: fibrous septa linking portal tracts and central veins, regenerative hepatocyte nodules, loss of the normal lobular pattern, with features of the underlying cause.

Complications:

- Portal hypertension: varices, ascites, splenomegaly.

- Hepatic encephalopathy and coagulopathy from synthetic failure.

- Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis and hepatorenal syndrome.

- Hepatocellular carcinoma.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Definition including bridging fibrosis and regenerative nodules (1)
  • At least five valid causes (1)
  • Stellate cell activation and collagen deposition (1)
  • Macroscopic features including micro versus macronodular (1)
  • Microscopic features (1)
  • Complications including portal hypertension and hepatocellular carcinoma (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing cirrhosis as reversible fibrosis only
  • Stating the liver is always enlarged
  • Omitting regenerative nodules from the definition
  • Listing only portal hypertension as a complication

Question 43 (6 marks)

Question: Compare the pathology of mucinous ovarian tumours with that of a mature cystic teratoma. Name two other ovarian germ cell tumours. (6 marks)

Model answer

Mucinous tumours:

- Surface epithelial tumours, benign, borderline or malignant, mostly unilateral.

- Large multilocular cystic masses filled with sticky mucin.

- Lined by tall columnar mucin-secreting cells resembling intestinal or endocervical epithelium; KRAS mutations are common.

- Carcinomas are usually unilateral and over 10 cm; bilateral, small or surface-involving mucinous tumours are more likely metastatic from the appendix, colon or pancreas, and rupture can cause pseudomyxoma peritonei.

Mature cystic teratoma (dermoid cyst):

- Commonest ovarian germ cell tumour, usually in young women.

- Contains mature tissue from all three germ layers.

- Usually a unilocular cyst lined by skin with hair, sebum, and sometimes teeth; a Rokitansky protuberance is the solid mural nodule. About 10 to 15 percent bilateral.

- Complications: torsion, rupture, autoimmune haemolytic anaemia, anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis, rare squamous carcinoma transformation.

Other germ cell tumours: dysgerminoma, yolk sac tumour (also immature teratoma, choriocarcinoma).

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Mucinous: multilocular, mucin-filled, columnar epithelium, benign to malignant spectrum (1)
  • Mucinous: metastasis from gastrointestinal tract to be excluded with discriminating features (1)
  • Pseudomyxoma peritonei or KRAS mentioned (0.5)
  • Teratoma: three germ layers and young women (1)
  • Teratoma: unilocular cyst with skin, hair, Rokitansky nodule (1)
  • Teratoma complications (1)
  • Two other valid germ cell tumours (0.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing a dermoid as predominantly solid
  • Stating mucinous carcinomas are usually bilateral
  • Forgetting to exclude a gastrointestinal primary
  • Naming a sex cord-stromal tumour as a germ cell tumour

Question 44 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the pathology of small cell lung carcinoma and compare it with typical carcinoid tumour. (6 marks)

Model answer

Small cell lung carcinoma:

- Strongly associated with smoking; central, perihilar mass with early nodal and distant spread.

- Microscopy: sheets of small cells with scant cytoplasm, nuclear moulding, finely granular (salt and pepper) chromatin, absent nucleoli, very high mitotic rate, extensive necrosis, crush artefact and basophilic vascular encrustation (Azzopardi effect).

- Immunohistochemistry: synaptophysin, chromogranin and CD56 positive; Ki-67 very high.

- Biallelic loss of TP53 and RB1.

- Paraneoplastic syndromes: SIADH, ectopic ACTH, Lambert-Eaton syndrome.

Typical carcinoid:

- Low grade neuroendocrine tumour; fewer than 2 mitoses per 2 mm2 and no necrosis (atypical: 2 to 10 mitoses and/or punctate necrosis).

- Younger patients, often non-smokers; central endobronchial polypoid lesion; may be peripheral.

- Organoid nests and trabeculae of uniform cells; carcinoid syndrome is uncommon.

- Excellent prognosis after resection.

The spectrum is typical carcinoid, atypical carcinoid, large cell neuroendocrine carcinoma and small cell carcinoma.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Central location, smoking, early spread (1)
  • Cytological features of small cell carcinoma (1)
  • Necrosis, high mitoses or crush artefact (0.5)
  • Neuroendocrine markers and TP53/RB1 (1)
  • Paraneoplastic syndromes (0.5)
  • Typical carcinoid grading by mitoses and necrosis (1)
  • Carcinoid clinical features and prognosis (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating small cell carcinoma is usually peripheral
  • Saying carcinoids are strongly linked to smoking
  • Confusing typical and atypical carcinoid criteria
  • Omitting neuroendocrine markers

Question 45 (6 marks)

Question: Outline the causes, pathogenesis and pathological features of osteonecrosis (avascular necrosis) of the femoral head. (6 marks)

Model answer

Causes (about 90 percent have a recognised risk factor):

- Trauma, especially displaced femoral neck fracture.

- Corticosteroids and alcohol.

- Sickle cell disease, Gaucher disease, systemic lupus erythematosus.

- Radiation, decompression sickness, bisphosphonate or denosumab exposure (jaw), idiopathic.

Pathogenesis: interruption of blood supply by vascular disruption, intravascular occlusion (thrombosis, fat or gas embolism, sickling) or vessel compression, causing ischaemic death of bone and marrow.

Macroscopy:

- Subchondral wedge-shaped segment, base against the subchondral plate, apex towards the centre.

- Yellow, chalky necrotic bone bordered by a hyperaemic rim.

- Overlying cartilage may survive initially, nourished by synovial fluid, then separates from underlying bone.

- Subchondral fracture and collapse, then secondary osteoarthritis.

Microscopy:

- Dead trabeculae with empty lacunae, necrotic marrow fat, dystrophic calcification.

- Reactive granulation tissue at the margin, with creeping substitution and new bone laid over dead bone.

Medullary infarcts of the metaphysis or diaphysis usually do not collapse.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • At least four valid causes (1)
  • Pathogenesis by interrupted blood supply with a mechanism (1)
  • Subchondral wedge-shaped infarct with base at subchondral plate (1)
  • Hyperaemic rim, cartilage survival and subchondral collapse (1)
  • Histology: empty lacunae and necrotic marrow (1)
  • Reparative response and secondary osteoarthritis (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Placing the wedge apex at the subchondral plate
  • Stating cartilage dies immediately with the bone
  • Using imaging signs such as the crescent sign in place of pathology
  • Listing osteoporosis as a cause

Question 46 (6 marks)

Question: Compare acute bacterial meningitis and cerebral abscess with respect to causative organisms, routes of infection, pathology and complications. (6 marks)

Model answer

Acute bacterial meningitis:

- Organisms by age: group B streptococcus and Escherichia coli in neonates; Streptococcus pneumoniae and Neisseria meningitidis in children and adults; Listeria in the elderly and immunosuppressed.

- Spread: haematogenous from nasopharynx, or direct from sinus, ear or skull fracture.

- Pathology: purulent exudate of neutrophils in the subarachnoid space, congested meningeal vessels, cloudy CSF with neutrophils, raised protein and low glucose.

- Complications: hydrocephalus, cerebral oedema, venous thrombosis and infarction, subdural effusion, cranial nerve deficits and deafness; meningococcal sepsis with adrenal haemorrhage (Waterhouse-Friderichsen).

Cerebral abscess:

- Organisms: viridans and milleri streptococci, Staphylococcus aureus, anaerobes, often mixed.

- Spread: contiguous from otitis media or sinusitis (temporal lobe, cerebellum, frontal lobe), haematogenous from endocarditis or lung sepsis (middle cerebral territory, grey-white junction), or penetrating trauma.

- Pathology: cerebritis, then liquefactive necrosis with a fibrous capsule, surrounded by gliosis and oedema.

- Complications: mass effect and herniation, rupture into the ventricles, seizures.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Organisms of meningitis by age group (1)
  • Pathology and CSF findings of meningitis (1)
  • Complications of meningitis (1)
  • Organisms of abscess (0.5)
  • Routes of spread with typical sites (1.5)
  • Abscess pathology: cerebritis, necrosis, capsule (0.5)
  • Abscess complications (0.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating tuberculous meningitis organisms as typical of acute bacterial disease
  • Describing abscess wall as formed immediately
  • Omitting routes of spread
  • Confusing raised CSF glucose with bacterial infection

Question 47 (6 marks)

Question: State the components of Virchow triad with examples of each. Describe the pathology of deep vein thrombosis and the consequences of pulmonary embolism. (6 marks)

Model answer

Virchow triad:

1. Endothelial injury: trauma, surgery, indwelling lines, inflammation.

2. Abnormal blood flow: stasis from immobility, heart failure, varicose veins, pregnancy.

3. Hypercoagulability: inherited (factor V Leiden, prothrombin G20210A, antithrombin, protein C or S deficiency); acquired (malignancy, pregnancy, oral contraceptive, antiphospholipid syndrome, heparin-induced thrombocytopenia).

Deep vein thrombosis:

- Venous (red) thrombi form in stasis, rich in red cells and fibrin with fewer platelets.

- Mainly leg deep veins (popliteal, femoral, iliac); extend proximally in the direction of flow and may be occlusive.

- Over 95 percent of pulmonary emboli arise from leg deep veins above the knee.

Pulmonary embolism consequences:

- Small emboli are often clinically silent and lyse.

- Large saddle embolus at the bifurcation: sudden death or acute right heart failure.

- Pulmonary infarction in about 10 percent: wedge-shaped, peripheral, haemorrhagic, typically with underlying cardiac or lung disease.

- Recurrent emboli: chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension.

- Paradoxical embolism through a patent foramen ovale.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Three components of the triad named (1)
  • Valid examples for each component, including inherited thrombophilia (1)
  • Venous thrombus composition and propagation (1)
  • Source of emboli: proximal leg deep veins (1)
  • Acute consequences: silent, saddle embolus, sudden death or right heart strain (1)
  • Infarction and chronic consequences (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Naming hypertension as a component of the triad
  • Stating most emboli arise in calf veins and are large
  • Claiming pulmonary infarction is usual in healthy lungs
  • Omitting hypercoagulability examples

Question 48 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the pathology of phaeochromocytoma. Outline the genetic syndromes associated with it, including the multiple endocrine neoplasia (MEN) syndromes. (6 marks)

Model answer

Phaeochromocytoma:

- Tumour of adrenal medullary chromaffin cells producing catecholamines; presents with paroxysmal hypertension, headache, sweating and palpitations; diagnosed by raised plasma or urinary metanephrines.

- Macroscopy: well circumscribed, tan to brown on cut, often with haemorrhage; darkens with dichromate.

- Microscopy: nests (Zellballen) of polygonal cells with granular cytoplasm separated by delicate capillaries and S100-positive sustentacular cells; chromogranin and synaptophysin positive.

- Malignancy cannot be diagnosed from cytology or architecture alone; only metastases to nodes, bone, liver or lung establish it. Extra-adrenal tumours are paragangliomas.

Associated syndromes (a large fraction are hereditary):

- MEN2A (RET): medullary thyroid carcinoma, phaeochromocytoma, parathyroid hyperplasia.

- MEN2B (RET): medullary thyroid carcinoma, phaeochromocytoma, mucosal neuromas, marfanoid habitus.

- Von Hippel-Lindau, neurofibromatosis type 1 and SDHx mutations.

- MEN1 (menin) is pituitary, parathyroid and pancreatic tumours and does not typically include phaeochromocytoma.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Chromaffin cell origin and catecholamine secretion with clinical effect (1)
  • Macroscopic features (1)
  • Zellballen pattern and immunohistochemistry (1)
  • Malignancy defined only by metastases (1)
  • MEN2A and MEN2B components with RET (1)
  • Other associated syndromes and MEN1 correctly distinguished (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Including phaeochromocytoma in MEN1
  • Stating malignancy can be diagnosed by mitotic count alone
  • Placing origin in the adrenal cortex
  • Forgetting hereditary syndromes beyond MEN

Question 49 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the aetiology, risk factors, pathology and complications of infective endocarditis. (6 marks)

Model answer

Aetiology:

- Viridans streptococci: subacute disease on previously damaged native valves.

- Staphylococcus aureus: acute, destructive disease on normal valves, intravenous drug use (often tricuspid) and healthcare-related infection.

- Prosthetic valves: coagulase-negative staphylococci early; also enterococci, HACEK organisms and fungi; culture-negative cases include Coxiella and Bartonella.

Risk factors: rheumatic valve disease, mitral valve prolapse, bicuspid aortic valve, other congenital heart disease, prosthetic valves, intravenous drug use, indwelling lines and dental or other bacteraemia sources.

Pathology:

- Friable vegetations on the line of valve closure (atrial surface of mitral, ventricular surface of aortic), made of fibrin, platelets, organisms and inflammatory cells.

- Valve destruction, leaflet perforation, chordal rupture, and ring abscess with conduction block.

Complications:

- Cardiac: acute regurgitation and heart failure, conduction defects.

- Embolic: infarcts in brain, spleen and kidney; mycotic aneurysm; septic pulmonary emboli from right-sided disease.

- Immune complex: glomerulonephritis, Osler nodes, Roth spots; Janeway lesions and splinter haemorrhages are embolic.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Organisms matched to valve type and acuity (1)
  • Risk factors (1)
  • Vegetation composition and location on valve (1)
  • Valve destruction and ring abscess (1)
  • Embolic complications (1)
  • Cardiac and immune complex complications (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Attributing S aureus to subacute damaged valves
  • Placing vegetations on the opposite surface of the valve
  • Calling Janeway lesions immune complex lesions
  • Omitting right-sided septic emboli

Question 50 (6 marks)

Question: Compare the pathology of neuroblastoma and Wilms tumour (nephroblastoma). (6 marks)

Model answer

Neuroblastoma:

- Tumour of primitive sympathetic neuroblasts; commonest extracranial solid tumour of infancy, mostly under 5 years.

- Sites: adrenal medulla (about 40 percent) and paraspinal sympathetic chain, including abdomen, chest and pelvis.

- Microscopy: small round blue cells with Homer Wright rosettes in a fibrillary neuropil background; differentiating forms contain ganglion cells.

- Urinary catecholamine metabolites (VMA, HVA) are raised.

- MYCN amplification and older age predict poor outcome; stage 4S in infants can regress spontaneously.

Wilms tumour:

- Embryonal renal tumour, peak 2 to 5 years; arises in kidney, forms a large well-circumscribed mass with a pseudocapsule.

- Classic triphasic microscopy: blastemal cells, epithelial tubules and glomeruloid structures, and stroma.

- Diffuse anaplasia (TP53 mutation) indicates adverse prognosis.

- Nephrogenic rests are precursor lesions.

- Syndromes: WAGR and Denys-Drash (WT1), Beckwith-Wiedemann (11p15).

- Spreads along renal vein to inferior vena cava, and to lung.

Both are small round blue cell tumours of childhood; the kidney origin, triphasic pattern and absence of catecholamine production separate Wilms tumour.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Neuroblastoma origin, age and sites (1)
  • Neuroblastoma microscopy with rosettes and neuropil (1)
  • Neuroblastoma biology: catecholamines, MYCN, stage 4S (1)
  • Wilms origin, age and gross appearance (1)
  • Wilms triphasic histology (1)
  • Wilms genetics, anaplasia and spread (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Assigning Homer Wright rosettes to Wilms tumour
  • Stating MYCN amplification is favourable
  • Omitting the blastemal component
  • Describing neuroblastoma as arising in the renal parenchyma

Short Answer Set 6: Model Answers

Question 51 (6 marks)

Question: A 68 year old retired boilermaker presents with breathlessness and a unilateral pleural effusion, and biopsy confirms malignant pleural mesothelioma. Outline the aetiology, the macroscopic and microscopic pathology, and the key molecular and immunohistochemical features of this tumour. (6 marks)

Model answer

Aetiology:

1. Asbestos exposure is the dominant cause, with a long latency of about 20 to 40 years. Amphibole fibres (crocidolite, amosite) are the most potent. Other causes are erionite and prior thoracic radiation.

2. Fibres reach the pleura, cause chronic inflammation and reactive oxygen species, and drive DNA damage.

Macroscopic:

3. Diffuse pleural thickening that encases the lung in a rind of firm grey-white tumour, with obliteration of the pleural space. It extends into fissures and may invade chest wall, diaphragm, pericardium and mediastinum. A pleural effusion is common.

Microscopic:

4. Three main types: epithelioid (most common, tubulopapillary or solid nests), sarcomatoid (spindle cells, worst prognosis) and biphasic (both components).

Immunohistochemistry and molecular:

5. Mesothelial markers are positive: calretinin, WT1, cytokeratin 5/6 and D2-40. Adenocarcinoma markers such as TTF-1, CEA and Ber-EP4 are negative or weak.

6. Loss of BAP1 nuclear staining, homozygous CDKN2A deletion and NF2 mutation are frequent.

Prognosis is poor, with median survival of about 12 months.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Asbestos as the main cause with a latency of decades (1)
  • Other causes (erionite or radiation) and fibre type or mechanism (1)
  • Macroscopic pleural rind encasing lung with local invasion (1)
  • Three histological types named (epithelioid, sarcomatoid, biphasic) (1)
  • Positive mesothelial immunohistochemistry (calretinin, WT1, CK5/6, D2-40) with negative adenocarcinoma markers (1)
  • Molecular changes (BAP1 loss, CDKN2A deletion or NF2) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating a latency of only a few years
  • Describing a discrete mass rather than a diffuse rind encasing the lung
  • Listing TTF-1 as a mesothelioma marker
  • Describing radiological features when only pathology was asked

Question 52 (6 marks)

Question: Define emphysema and chronic bronchitis, and describe the pathology of each. Include the morphological types of emphysema with their usual distribution within the lung and the main associated cause of each type. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definitions:

1. Emphysema is permanent enlargement of the airspaces distal to the terminal bronchiole with destruction of alveolar walls and without obvious fibrosis.

2. Chronic bronchitis is a clinical diagnosis: persistent productive cough for at least 3 months in each of 2 consecutive years, after excluding other causes.

Pathology of chronic bronchitis:

3. Hypertrophy of bronchial mucous glands (increased Reid index), goblet cell metaplasia, chronic inflammation and, later, bronchiolar fibrosis. Mucus plugging narrows the airways.

Types of emphysema:

4. Centriacinar (centrilobular): central part of the acinus is affected, respiratory bronchioles dilated, distal alveoli spared. Upper lobes, apical segments. Cigarette smoking.

5. Panacinar (panlobular): whole acinus uniformly enlarged. Lower lobes. Alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency.

6. Distal acinar (paraseptal): subpleural and along septa, may form bullae and cause spontaneous pneumothorax. Irregular emphysema is associated with scarring.

Pathogenesis: protease and antiprotease imbalance (neutrophil elastase) with oxidative stress from smoke.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Definition of emphysema (permanent airspace enlargement with wall destruction, no fibrosis) (1)
  • Clinical definition of chronic bronchitis (3 months for 2 consecutive years) (1)
  • Mucous gland hypertrophy, goblet cell metaplasia or Reid index (1)
  • Centriacinar type with upper lobe distribution and smoking (1)
  • Panacinar type with lower lobe distribution and alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency (1)
  • Paraseptal or irregular type with correct feature or complication (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reversing the lobar distribution of centriacinar and panacinar emphysema
  • Defining chronic bronchitis histologically rather than clinically
  • Describing CT signs instead of pathology
  • Stating that emphysema involves marked fibrosis

Question 53 (6 marks)

Question: Compare the pathology of endometriosis and adenomyosis. Include definitions, the common sites, the macroscopic appearances, the proposed pathogenesis of endometriosis and the main complications of each. (6 marks)

Model answer

Definitions:

1. Endometriosis: endometrial glands and stroma outside the uterus. Adenomyosis: endometrial glands and stroma within the myometrium, deep to the basalis.

Sites:

2. Endometriosis: ovaries (most common), pouch of Douglas, uterosacral ligaments, fallopian tubes, pelvic peritoneum, bowel serosa, and rarely distant sites or scars. Adenomyosis: the myometrium of the uterine wall, diffusely or as a focal adenomyoma.

Macroscopic:

3. Endometriosis: red-blue to brown mucosal or serosal nodules (powder burn spots), and ovarian endometriomas (chocolate cysts) filled with old altered blood. Repeated bleeding causes fibrosis and dense adhesions.

4. Adenomyosis: globular enlargement of the uterus with a thickened, trabeculated cut surface and small cystic spaces.

Pathogenesis of endometriosis:

5. Retrograde menstruation (regurgitation theory), coelomic metaplasia, and vascular or lymphatic spread.

Complications:

6. Endometriosis: pain, infertility, adhesions and bowel obstruction, and rare malignant transformation to endometrioid or clear cell carcinoma. Adenomyosis: heavy menstrual bleeding and dysmenorrhoea.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Correct definitions of both conditions (1)
  • Sites of endometriosis and adenomyosis (1)
  • Endometriosis macroscopic features (powder burn, chocolate cyst) (1)
  • Adenomyosis macroscopic features (globular uterus, thickened wall) (1)
  • Pathogenesis of endometriosis (retrograde menstruation, metaplasia, spread) (1)
  • Complications of each, including infertility and malignant transformation (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing adenomyosis as endometrial tissue on the uterine serosa
  • Omitting the stroma from the definition
  • Stating that malignant transformation is common
  • Listing MRI signal characteristics when pathology was asked

Question 54 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the pathology of chronic hepatitis B and chronic hepatitis C. Include the virus type, the characteristic histological features of each, the grading and staging of chronic hepatitis, and the major long-term complications. (6 marks)

Model answer

Virus type:

1. Hepatitis B is a DNA hepadnavirus. Hepatitis C is an RNA flavivirus. Chronic infection means persistence beyond 6 months.

Common histology:

2. Portal chronic inflammation (lymphocytes), interface hepatitis (piecemeal necrosis) and lobular spotty necrosis, with variable fibrosis.

Hepatitis B specific:

3. Ground glass hepatocytes (cytoplasm filled with HBsAg in smooth endoplasmic reticulum) and sanded nuclei (HBcAg). Immunostains for HBsAg and HBcAg confirm.

Hepatitis C specific:

4. Lymphoid aggregates or follicles within portal tracts, bile duct damage, and macrovesicular steatosis (especially genotype 3).

Grading and staging:

5. Grade is the necroinflammatory activity (interface and lobular). Stage is the extent of fibrosis from portal expansion through bridging to cirrhosis (for example the METAVIR or Ishak systems).

Complications:

6. Cirrhosis with portal hypertension and hepatocellular carcinoma. HBV can cause carcinoma without cirrhosis through viral integration. HCV infection is now curable with direct acting antivirals.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Virus types (DNA hepadnavirus and RNA flavivirus) and persistence over 6 months (1)
  • Common histological pattern (portal inflammation, interface hepatitis) (1)
  • Ground glass hepatocytes and sanded nuclei for HBV (1)
  • Lymphoid aggregates, bile duct damage or steatosis for HCV (1)
  • Distinction of grade (activity) from stage (fibrosis) (1)
  • Cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma as complications (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Attributing ground glass hepatocytes to hepatitis C
  • Confusing grade with stage
  • Calling hepatitis C a DNA virus
  • Describing elastography findings instead of histology

Question 55 (6 marks)

Question: Compare gout and calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate (CPPD) disease. Describe the crystal morphology and birefringence, the usual joints involved, the pathological lesions produced, and the pathogenesis of each. (6 marks)

Model answer

Gout:

1. Monosodium urate crystals: needle-shaped, strongly negatively birefringent under polarised light (yellow when parallel to the compensator axis).

2. Joints: first metatarsophalangeal joint classically, then ankle, knee and small joints of the foot.

3. Pathology: acute arthritis with neutrophilic synovitis, then chronic tophaceous gout. A tophus is an aggregate of urate crystals surrounded by foreign body giant cells and fibrosis. Tophi occur in the synovium, subcutis (helix of the ear) and bone, producing punched-out erosions with overhanging edges.

4. Pathogenesis: hyperuricaemia from overproduction or reduced renal excretion, crystal phagocytosis and NLRP3 inflammasome activation with interleukin-1 release.

CPPD disease:

5. Calcium pyrophosphate crystals: rhomboid or rod-shaped, weakly positively birefringent. Joints: knee most common, then wrist and symphysis pubis. Pathology: chondrocalcinosis, deposits in hyaline cartilage and fibrocartilage (menisci, triangular fibrocartilage) and pseudogout attacks.

6. Pathogenesis: increased pyrophosphate in cartilage, often age related, or secondary to haemochromatosis, hyperparathyroidism or hypomagnesaemia.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Urate crystal shape and negative birefringence (1)
  • CPP crystal shape and weak positive birefringence (1)
  • Typical joints for both conditions (1)
  • Tophus structure with giant cell reaction and erosions (1)
  • Chondrocalcinosis as the CPPD lesion (1)
  • Pathogenesis of both (hyperuricaemia, inflammasome; pyrophosphate excess and associations) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reversing the birefringence of the two crystals
  • Placing first MTP involvement in CPPD
  • Omitting the foreign body giant cell reaction of the tophus
  • Quoting radiographic signs without pathology

Question 56 (6 marks)

Question: Compare nasopharyngeal carcinoma with HPV-related oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma. Include the causative viruses, the usual anatomical origin, the histological subtypes or appearance, the diagnostic marker used for each, and the relative prognosis. (6 marks)

Model answer

Nasopharyngeal carcinoma (NPC):

1. Cause: Epstein-Barr virus, with strong geographic associations (southern China, Southeast Asia, North Africa) and dietary and genetic contributions.

2. Origin: nasopharyngeal mucosa, most often the lateral wall and fossa of Rosenmuller (pharyngeal recess).

3. WHO types: keratinising squamous cell carcinoma, non-keratinising carcinoma (differentiated or undifferentiated) and basaloid squamous carcinoma. Non-keratinising undifferentiated carcinoma shows large cells with vesicular nuclei and prominent nucleoli, with a dense lymphocytic infiltrate (lymphoepithelial pattern).

4. Marker: EBV-encoded RNA by in situ hybridisation (EBER) is positive.

HPV-related oropharyngeal carcinoma:

5. Cause: high risk HPV, mainly type 16. Origin: lingual and palatine tonsillar crypt epithelium and base of tongue. Histology: usually non-keratinising, basaloid morphology with little maturation. Marker: strong diffuse p16 immunostaining as a surrogate, with HPV testing for confirmation.

Prognosis:

6. HPV-related carcinoma has a better prognosis and response to treatment than HPV-negative tumours. NPC is radiosensitive; EBV-negative keratinising types do worse.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • EBV as the cause of NPC and HPV 16 for oropharyngeal carcinoma (1)
  • Origin of NPC (fossa of Rosenmuller) (1)
  • Origin of HPV-related carcinoma (tonsillar crypts, base of tongue) (1)
  • WHO histological subtypes of NPC and the lymphoepithelial pattern (0.5)
  • Non-keratinising basaloid morphology of HPV-related carcinoma (0.5)
  • EBER and p16 markers (1)
  • Better prognosis of HPV-related carcinoma (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Using p16 as the marker for NPC
  • Stating that HPV-related carcinoma is typically keratinising
  • Placing NPC origin in the oropharynx
  • Listing nodal imaging patterns instead of pathology

Question 57 (6 marks)

Question: Define oncogene and tumour suppressor gene, giving one example of each. Describe the normal functions of p53 and the consequences of its loss in neoplasia. (6 marks)

Model answer

Oncogene:

1. A mutated or overexpressed version (from a proto-oncogene) of a gene that promotes cell growth. It acts by gain of function and is dominant, so one mutated allele is sufficient. Examples: RAS (point mutation, constitutive signalling), MYC (amplification or translocation), ERBB2/HER2 (amplification).

Tumour suppressor gene:

2. A gene that restrains growth or promotes apoptosis, and cancer arises through loss of function. It is generally recessive at cellular level, requiring both alleles to be lost (Knudson two hit hypothesis). Examples: RB, APC, BRCA1 and TP53.

Normal functions of p53 (guardian of the genome):

3. Stabilised after DNA damage via ATM and CHK2 kinases, as MDM2 normally degrades it.

4. Induces cell cycle arrest at G1/S through p21 (CDKN1A), allowing DNA repair.

5. Triggers apoptosis through BAX and PUMA if damage is irreparable, and promotes senescence.

Loss of p53:

6. Damaged cells proliferate, mutations accumulate and genomic instability results. TP53 is mutated in over half of human cancers. Germline mutation causes Li-Fraumeni syndrome (sarcomas, breast cancer, brain tumours, leukaemia, adrenocortical carcinoma).

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Oncogene defined as gain of function and dominant, with an example (1)
  • Tumour suppressor defined as loss of function with the two hit idea, with an example (1)
  • p53 activation by DNA damage and its regulation by MDM2 (1)
  • Cell cycle arrest through p21 (1)
  • Apoptosis (BAX or PUMA) or senescence (1)
  • Consequences of loss including genomic instability and Li-Fraumeni syndrome (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling a tumour suppressor gene dominant
  • Giving a tumour suppressor as an oncogene example
  • Forgetting that MDM2 degrades p53
  • Stating that p53 loss directly causes cell division rather than failure of arrest and apoptosis

Question 58 (6 marks)

Question: Describe the aetiology, the pathological types (including precursor lesions), and the pathological staging of urothelial carcinoma of the bladder. Mention the molecular pathways associated with low and high grade disease. (6 marks)

Model answer

Aetiology:

1. Cigarette smoking (most important), occupational aromatic amines (aniline dyes, 2-naphthylamine), cyclophosphamide, phenacetin and pelvic radiation. Schistosomiasis is linked mainly to squamous carcinoma.

Pathological types:

2. Non-invasive papillary urothelial carcinoma: fibrovascular cores covered by urothelium, low grade or high grade. Low grade often recurs but rarely invades.

3. Carcinoma in situ (flat): high grade cytologically malignant cells confined to the urothelium, often multifocal, high risk of progression to invasion.

4. Invasive urothelial carcinoma, which may show divergent differentiation (squamous or glandular).

Molecular:

5. Low grade papillary tumours: FGFR3 and HRAS mutations. High grade tumours and carcinoma in situ: TP53 and RB inactivation.

Staging (TNM):

6. Ta: non-invasive papillary. Tis: carcinoma in situ. T1: invades lamina propria. T2: invades muscularis propria. T3: invades perivesical tissue. T4: invades adjacent organs such as prostatic stroma, uterus, vagina or pelvic wall. Field change explains multifocality and recurrence.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Smoking and occupational or other aetiological factors (1)
  • Non-invasive papillary carcinoma with low and high grade (1)
  • Carcinoma in situ and invasive types (1)
  • FGFR3 for low grade papillary disease (0.5)
  • TP53 and RB for high grade disease and carcinoma in situ (0.5)
  • T1 and T2 stage definitions (1)
  • T3 and T4 stage definitions and Ta/Tis (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Stating that schistosomiasis causes urothelial rather than squamous carcinoma
  • Defining T1 as muscle invasion
  • Confusing Ta (papillary) with Tis (flat)
  • Describing CT urogram findings instead of pathology

Question 59 (6 marks)

Question: Compare giant cell arteritis with Takayasu arteritis. Include the typical patient, the vessels affected, the histological features, the main complications and the usual laboratory finding. (6 marks)

Model answer

Giant cell (temporal) arteritis:

1. Patient: over 50 years, more common in women, associated with polymyalgia rheumatica.

2. Vessels: branches of the carotid artery, especially the temporal arteries, also the ophthalmic and vertebral arteries and sometimes the aorta.

3. Histology: segmental, patchy (skip) granulomatous inflammation of the media and intima with multinucleated giant cells, fragmentation of the internal elastic lamina and intimal thickening with luminal narrowing.

4. Complications: ischaemic optic neuropathy with permanent blindness, jaw claudication and stroke, and later aortic aneurysm or dissection.

Takayasu arteritis:

5. Patient: usually women under 50 (often under 40), more frequent in Asia. Vessels: aortic arch and its branches, descending aorta, and renal and pulmonary arteries. Histology: granulomatous inflammation with giant cells, followed by medial destruction, marked intimal fibrosis and adventitial scarring. Complications: narrowing causing weak or absent pulses, limb claudication, renovascular hypertension and aneurysm.

6. Both show raised ESR and CRP.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Typical age and sex for both conditions (1)
  • Vessels of giant cell arteritis (temporal and other carotid branches) (0.5)
  • Vessels of Takayasu arteritis (aortic arch and branches) (0.5)
  • Histology of giant cell arteritis (granulomatous, skip lesions, elastic lamina fragmentation) (1)
  • Histology of Takayasu arteritis (granulomatous then fibrosis) (1)
  • Complications of both (blindness; pulseless disease, renovascular hypertension) (1)
  • Raised inflammatory markers (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Giving 'under 50' as the age for giant cell arteritis
  • Describing the histology of the two diseases as clearly different
  • Stating that temporal arteritis spares the eye
  • Ignoring aortic involvement in giant cell arteritis

Question 60 (6 marks)

Question: A child of African ancestry has recurrent painful crises and chronic haemolytic anaemia due to sickle cell disease. Outline the molecular basis and pathogenesis, and list six complications, giving the pathological mechanism of each. (6 marks)

Model answer

Molecular basis:

1. Point mutation in the beta globin gene (GAG to GTG) substitutes valine for glutamic acid at position 6, producing haemoglobin S. Autosomal recessive; heterozygotes (sickle trait) are usually asymptomatic and protected against falciparum malaria.

Pathogenesis:

2. On deoxygenation HbS polymerises into rods that distort the red cell into a sickle shape. Sickling is promoted by hypoxia, acidosis, dehydration and a higher intracellular HbS concentration, and reduced by HbF. Repeated sickling damages the membrane, leading to extravascular haemolysis, and sickled cells adhere to endothelium and occlude microvessels.

Complications:

3. Splenic infarction with autosplenectomy in adulthood: recurrent infarction, predisposing to encapsulated organism sepsis. Splenic sequestration crisis in children.

4. Acute chest syndrome: pulmonary vaso-occlusion and infection.

5. Bone infarcts and avascular necrosis of the femoral head; osteomyelitis, classically Salmonella.

6. Stroke from cerebral vasculopathy, aplastic crisis from parvovirus B19, renal papillary necrosis, priapism and pigment gallstones from chronic haemolysis.

Mark scheme (6 marks)

  • Beta globin point mutation with glutamic acid to valine at position 6 (1)
  • Polymerisation of deoxygenated HbS and sickling (1)
  • Haemolysis and vaso-occlusion as the two consequences (1)
  • Splenic complications with mechanism (1)
  • Two further complications with mechanism (acute chest, bone, stroke or aplastic crisis) (1)
  • Six complications in total with valid mechanisms (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Naming valine to glutamic acid in the wrong direction
  • Stating that the spleen is enlarged in adults with sickle cell disease
  • Omitting the protective role of HbF
  • Describing radiographic findings rather than mechanisms
